Multiplication \(U_xF=\psi_xF\) is a unitary map from \(L^2(\mu_x)_{\rm ph}\) onto \(L^2(dU)_{\rm ph}\). Its operator and form domains are \(H^2_{\rm ph}\) and \(H^1_{\rm ph}\), respectively, under the unchanged coordinate Sobolev scale. The exact conjugate is
\[
 \mathcal L_x=K-2\Gamma(v_x,\cdot)
 =\kappa^{-1}\psi_x^{-1}(H(x)-E(x))\psi_x,
 \quad
 \int\mathcal L_xh\,d\mu_x=0.
 \tag{GPR8}
\]
The last identity holds first for smooth \(h\) by integration by parts and then for distributions by duality with the smooth density. In particular the only physical distributional solutions of \(\mathcal L_xh=0\) are constants: elliptic bootstrapping makes them smooth and (GPR5) applies.

Define on every real Sobolev index
\[
 \mathscr L_x
 =I-2K^{-1}Q_H\Gamma(v_x,\cdot)
 =K^{-1}Q_H\mathcal L_x:
 H^s_{\rm ph,0}\longrightarrow H^s_{\rm ph,0}.
 \tag{GPR9}
\]
The perturbation of the identity maps \(H^s\to H^{s+1}\), because its drift is first order and \(K^{-1}Q_H\) gains two derivatives. It is therefore compact on \(H^s_{\rm ph,0}\). If \(\mathscr L_xh=0\), repeated use of the same one-derivative gain makes \(h\) smooth. The equation says \(\mathcal L_xh=b\) for a constant \(b\). Its \(\mu_x\) mean gives \(b=0\). Equation (GPR5) makes \(h\) constant, and its Haar mean is zero. Hence \(h=0\). An identity minus a compact operator has index zero; its trivial kernel therefore gives a bounded bijection in (GPR9). This argument proves invertibility at every finite real source, with no small-source assumption.

Here is the complete formula. Let \(A_x=H(x)-E(x)\) and let \(A_x^\dagger\) be its inverse on \(\psi_x^\perp\cap L^2(dU)_{\rm ph}\), extended to annihilate \(\psi_x\). Then
\[
 \boxed{
 \mathscr L_x^{-1}f
 =Q_H\,\kappa\psi_x^{-1}A_x^\dagger\psi_x
       [Kf-\mu_x(Kf)] .}
 \tag{GPR10}
\]
For \(f\in H^2_{\rm ph,0}\), the bracket is in \(L^2\), and \(\psi_x[Kf-\mu_x(Kf)]\perp\psi_x\). Applying (GPR8) proves that the expression before \(Q_H\) solves \(\mathcal L_xh=Kf-\mu_x(Kf)\). Subtracting its Haar mean does not change that equation. Applying \(K^{-1}Q_H\) proves (GPR10). Conversely any solution of \(\mathscr L_xh=f\) must have \(\mathcal L_xh=Kf+b\); its \(\mu_x\) mean forces the specific scalar \(b=-\mu_x(Kf)\). Thus the two distinct means in (GPR10) are both necessary.

For arbitrary real \(s\), interpret \(\mu_x(Kf)=\langle Kf,\rho_x\rangle=\langle f,K\rho_x\rangle\). Extend \(A_x^\dagger:H^{s-2}_{\rm ph}\to H^s_{\rm ph}\) by its elliptic inverse with the rank-one vacuum projection removed. To check this extension without assuming it, apply (GPR9) to solve \(\mathcal L_xh=g-\mu_x(g)\) for \(g\in H^{s-2}_{\rm ph}\), setting \(f=K^{-1}Q_Hg\); the missing constant is fixed by the same mean identity. Multiplication by \(\psi_x\) then solves the distributional \(A_x\) equation, and subtraction of the \(\psi_x\) component fixes its unique inverse. This agrees with the \(L^2\) spectral inverse on the intersection of the domains by uniqueness. Consequently (GPR10) is a bounded inverse with exactly the claimed domains at every \(s\in\mathbb R\).

All of these maps depend real analytically on \(x\in\mathbb R^M\), locally as maps in their stated bounded-operator norms. At a fixed \(x_0\), take a circle enclosing only \(E(x_0)\). The bounded potential difference has norm at most \(4\kappa\sum_p|x_p-x_{0,p}|\); a sufficiently small complex neighbourhood makes the resolvent Neumann expansion uniform on that circle. Its contour integral is an analytic rank-one projection. Apply it to \(\psi_{x_0}\), and divide by the analytic square root of its bilinear integral \(\int\phi_x^2dU\), choosing the branch equal to one at \(x_0\). On real nearby sources this is the positive unit vacuum. The resolvent equation and successive Sobolev estimates make this dependence analytic in every \(H^r\). Sobolev embedding at \(r>3N/2+k\) gives it in \(C^k\). Positivity at \(x_0\) and the convergent power series of the logarithm give analytic \(v_x\in C^k\) on a smaller neighbourhood. Thus (GPR9) is analytic on \(H^s\); the ordinary local Neumann expansion around its already proved inverse proves analytic \(\mathscr L_x^{-1}\). Unique positive phases agree on overlaps. This is global real analytic dependence, meaning a compatible local analytic family at every real point; it does not assert a single complex power-series disk reaching all real sources.

\subsection*{3. The exact comparison with every original support label}

For \(t\ge0\) let \(Y_t\) be the physical zero-Haar-mean Fourier space with
\[
 f=\sum_{j\ne0}\operatorname{Tr}(A_j\pi_j),\qquad
 \|f\|_{Y_t}=\sum_{j\ne0}c(j)^t\|A_j\|_1.
 \tag{GPR11}
\]
The original labelled Banach space \(\mathfrak B\) retains all nonempty edge labels \(S\subseteq E_L\) and all coefficients whose active spin support is contained in \(S\), including strict containment. Its norm and assembly are
\[
 \|F\|_{\rm loc}=\max_{e\in E_L}\sum_{S\ni e}\sum_{j\ne0}c(j)\|A_{S,j}\|_1,
 \quad
 \mathsf aF=\sum_j\operatorname{Tr}\!\left[\Big(\sum_{S\supseteq\operatorname{supp}j}A_{S,j}\Big)\pi_j\right].
 \tag{GPR12}
\]
Summing the anchor norms counts each original label at least once, so
\[
 \|\mathsf aF\|_{Y_1}\le N\|F\|_{\rm loc},\qquad
 \|\mathsf aF\|_{H^2}\le\tfrac43N\|F\|_{\rm loc}.
 \tag{GPR13}
\]
For the second inequality use the triangle inequality on the \(L^2\) functions and \((1+c)/c\le4/3\). Absolute coefficient convergence also gives \(\mathsf aF\in C^2\): zeroth coefficients have bound \(c/3\), first derivatives have \(j_e\le\sqrt c\), and each ordered second derivative has \(j_ej_f\le c\), including the case \(e=f\). Every component converges uniformly with these derivatives.

There is a bounded section \(\iota:Y_1\to\mathfrak B\): place each coefficient in the existing full original edge label \(E_L\). It has \(\|\iota f\|_{\rm loc}=\|f\|_{Y_1}\) and \(\mathsf a\iota=I\). This is a comparison map; it does not replace any existing support label. The relation \(F-\iota\mathsf aF\) is kept in \(\ker\mathsf a\), coefficient by coefficient. The original quotient is exactly
\[
 \overline{\mathsf a}:\mathfrak B/\ker\mathsf a\xrightarrow{\cong}Y_1,
 \quad
 \|[F]\|_{\rm quotient}\le\|\overline{\mathsf a}[F]\|_{Y_1}
 \le N\|[F]\|_{\rm quotient}.
 \tag{GPR14}
\]
The first inequality uses the explicit representative \(\iota\mathsf aF\); the second follows from (GPR13) and the infimum over representatives. Both constants depend on the original finite volume as displayed.

The original union-label bilinear map is
\[
 \mathcal B(F,G)_S=K^{-1}Q_H
       \sum_{S_1\cup S_2=S}\Gamma(F_{S_1},G_{S_2}).
\]
It obeys the exact intertwining
\[
 \mathsf a\mathcal B(F,G)=K^{-1}Q_H\Gamma(\mathsf aF,\mathsf aG),
 \qquad
 \mathsf a(I-2\mathcal B(V,\cdot))
 =\mathscr L_x\mathsf a\quad\text{when }\mathsf aV=v_x.
 \tag{GPR15}
\]
First verify this on finite coefficient families, where it is just the displayed sum over every original union. Then use the original G13 bilinear bound and (GPR13) to pass to the Banach completion and \(H^2\), respectively. The assembly kernel is invariant, since the right side vanishes on it.

In particular, in the previously proved labelled small-source disk, the original Neumann inverse and the physical inverse satisfy
\[
 \mathsf a(I-2\mathcal B(V(x),\cdot))^{-1}
 =\mathscr L_x^{-1}\mathsf a.
 \tag{GPR16}
\]
Both sides solve the same physical equation, and (GPR9) gives uniqueness. The same equation holds for the quotient inverse. This proves the inclusion and comparison, without extending the radius of convergence of the old labelled Taylor series.

One can prove more for the original finite-volume quotient, independently of that series. Smooth \(v_x\) belongs to every \(Y_t\). Indeed, trace-norm/Hilbert–Schmidt duality and Peter–Weyl Plancherel give
\[
 \sum_jc(j)^t\|A_j\|_1
 \le\left(\sum_j d_j^2\frac{c(j)^{2t}}{(1+c(j))^r}\right)^{1/2}\|v_x\|_{H^r},
 \tag{GPR17}
\]
where \(d_j=\prod_e(2j_e+1)\). The sum is finite when \(r>2t+3N/2\), by comparison with the product-spin lattice sum in dimension \(3N\). The missing constant coefficient of \(v_x\) is zero.

The original tensor-product trace-norm estimate and spin-generator norm give, for the drift coefficient product, a bound \(3\sum_e j_ek_e\le3\sqrt{c(j)c(k)}\). Any output spin has \(c(\ell)\le2(c(j)+c(k))\), since \(\ell_e\le j_e+k_e\) and \((j_e+k_e)(j_e+k_e+1)\le2[j_e(j_e+1)+k_e(k_e+1)]\). Keeping all three colour derivatives therefore proves
\[
 \|K^{-1}Q_H\Gamma(v_x,h)\|_{Y_{3/2}}
 \le3\sqrt2\big(\|v_x\|_{Y_1}\|h\|_{Y_{1/2}}
                  +\|v_x\|_{Y_{1/2}}\|h\|_{Y_1}\big).
 \tag{GPR18}
\]
The inverse Casimir cancels one power of the output weight. The remaining square root is bounded by \(\sqrt2(\sqrt{c(j)}+\sqrt{c(k)})\); this gives the two terms and the constant in (GPR18). Since \(\|h\|_{Y_{1/2}}\le3^{-1/2}\|h\|_{Y_1}\), this is a bounded map \(Y_1\to Y_{3/2}\). The inclusion \(Y_{3/2}\to Y_1\) is compact: only finitely many finite-dimensional matrix blocks lie below a Casimir cutoff, and the remaining inclusion norm is at most \(R^{-1/2}\). Hence \(\mathscr L_x\) is identity minus a compact operator on \(Y_1\). Its kernel embeds in the already trivial \(H^2\) kernel. The index-zero argument proves a bounded \(Y_1\) inverse at every finite real source. Formula (GPR10) and uniqueness identify it exactly with the Sobolev inverse on \(Y_1\).

Thus (GPR14) transports this independently proved inverse to the original assembly quotient at all finite real sources. The source \(v_x\) and this inverse are real analytic in that quotient, by (GPR17) and the Sobolev analytic result. This does not assert an inverse on the unquotiented relation kernel, a volume-uniform quotient norm, or continuation of the old Taylor series beyond its established disk. Every original relation remains present in (GPR12)–(GPR16).

\subsection*{4. Strict physical response, observation and global source coordinates}

Set \(m_p(x)=\mu_x(W_p)\), \(z_q=\partial_{x_q}v_x\), and \(Z_q=z_q-\mu_x(z_q)\). Differentiating the full normalization and eigen-equation gives
\[
 \partial_{x_q}\psi_x=\psi_xZ_q,\qquad
 \partial_{x_q}E(x)=\kappa(2-m_q),\qquad
 A_x(\psi_xZ_q)=\kappa(W_q-m_q)\psi_x,
 \qquad \mathscr L_xz_q=W_q/3.
 \tag{GPR19}
\]
For the last equation differentiate (GPR7), apply \(K^{-1}Q_H\), and use \(KW_q=3W_q\). All derivatives exist by Section 2.

With \(r_p=(W_p-m_p)\psi_x\), the original physical response is
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 R^{\rm phys}_{pq}(x)
 &=\kappa\langle r_p,A_x^\dagger r_q\rangle_{L^2(dU)}
 =\mu_x[(W_p-m_p)z_q],\\
 \partial_{x_q}m_p&=2R^{\rm phys}_{pq},\qquad
 \partial_{x_p}\partial_{x_q}E=-2\kappa R^{\rm phys}_{pq}.
 \end{aligned}\tag{GPR20}
\]
These identities hold for every finite real source. The matrix is real symmetric and strictly positive definite. To prove strictness, let \(a\in\mathbb R^M\) be nonzero and \(F_a=\sum_pa_pW_p\). The source vector \(r_a=(F_a-\mu_xF_a)\psi_x\) cannot vanish: positivity of \(\psi_x\) and continuity would make \(F_a\) constant everywhere, whereas its Haar mean is zero and its Haar squared norm is \(\sum_pa_p^2>0\). The positive inverse of \(A_x\) on \(\psi_x^\perp\) therefore has strictly positive quadratic form at \(r_a\). The same conclusion for complex coefficient vectors follows by real and imaginary parts.

On \(H^s_{\rm ph,0}\), including distributional \(s\), retain the exact finite-rank observation
\[
 \mathcal O_{\rho_x}h
 =\sum_pW_p\{\langle h,\rho_xW_p\rangle
                       -m_p\langle h,\rho_x\rangle\},\qquad
 Ph=\sum_pW_p\langle h,W_p\rangle.
 \tag{GPR21}
\]
All paired functions are smooth. With the original Haar-orthonormal face basis, the global identity is
\[
 \boxed{3R^{\rm phys}(x)=\mathcal O_{\rho_x}\mathscr L_x^{-1}P.}
 \tag{GPR22}
\]
In the old labelled disk, (GPR16) identifies it with Q46 after assembly. The exact Q47 comparison with the fifth reference remains valid wherever its own inverse exists, with every operator understood through this inclusion. Its two corrections, the observation change and the higher-source resolvent term, remain necessary. At \(x=0\), \(\rho=1\), \(\mathscr L=I\), \(\mathcal O_\rho=P\), and \(R^{\rm phys}=I_M/3\).

There is also a complete global description of the expectation coordinates. Let
\[
 \mathcal C_L=\operatorname{conv}\{(W_p(U))_{p\in\mathcal P_L}:U\in Q_L\}\subset\mathbb R^M,
 \qquad \Lambda(x)=2\sum_px_p-E(x)/\kappa.
 \tag{GPR23}
\]
The convex hull is compact and has nonempty interior. If it were contained in an affine hyperplane, a nonzero \(a\) would make \(F_a\) constant, contrary to the Haar calculation above. Variationally, \(\Lambda(x)\) is the largest eigenvalue of \(-K+\sum_px_pW_p\); the equality retains rather than discards the scalar in \(H(x)\). By (GPR20), \(\nabla\Lambda=m(x)\) and \(D^2\Lambda=2R^{\rm phys}>0\).

For every finite \(x\), \(m(x)\in\operatorname{int}\mathcal C_L\). Otherwise a supporting hyperplane would give a nonconstant \(F_a\le h_{\mathcal C_L}(a)\) whose positive-density integral equals its maximum. Its continuous nonnegative deficit would then vanish everywhere, a contradiction. Strict Hessian positivity makes \(m\) injective, since for \(x\ne y\)
\[
 (x-y)\cdot(m(x)-m(y))
 =2\int_0^1(x-y)^tR^{\rm phys}(y+t(x-y))(x-y)\,dt>0.
 \tag{GPR24}
\]
It is onto this interior as well. Fix \(b\in\operatorname{int}\mathcal C_L\), and take \(\varepsilon>0\) with \(b+\varepsilon\overline B\subset\mathcal C_L\). For each unit vector \(u\), choose a configuration where \(F_u\) attains its maximum and a nonzero smooth bump supported where \(F_u>h_{\mathcal C_L}(u)-\varepsilon/4\). Its unit Rayleigh quotient for \(-K+tF_u\) is at least \(t[h_{\mathcal C_L}(u)-\varepsilon/4]-C_u\), with finite \(C_u=\int\Gamma(\bar\phi_u,\phi_u)dU\). Because \(\|W(U)\|\le2\sqrt M\), both the support function and these trial expectations are \(2\sqrt M\)-Lipschitz in \(u\). Finitely many neighbourhoods of these \(u\)'s cover the unit sphere. Their finite maximum kinetic cost \(C_\varepsilon\) gives, for every unit \(u\) and \(t\ge0\),
\[
 \Lambda(tu)-t\,u\cdot b\ge t\varepsilon/2-C_\varepsilon.
 \tag{GPR25}
\]
The variational inequality is valid in the full Hilbert space, whose largest eigenvalue here equals the physical one by ground positivity. Thus no gauge-variant trial is declared to be a physical state. Equation (GPR25) proves coercivity. The smooth strictly convex function \(\Lambda(x)-x\cdot b\) has a unique minimizer, and its derivative there is \(m(x)-b=0\). The analytic inverse-function theorem and uniqueness join the local inverses. Therefore
\[
 \boxed{m:\mathbb R^M\xrightarrow{\text{real analytic diffeomorphism}}
                   \operatorname{int}\mathcal C_L,\qquad
 D(m^{-1})=(2R^{\rm phys})^{-1}.}
 \tag{GPR26}
\]
This is a result about the actual finite-box ground state and the original joint Wilson image. It supplies no uniform limit of these convex bodies or inverses as \(L\to\infty\).

\subsection*{5. Explicit bounds, including a polynomial local bound}

Set
\[
 s_e(x)=\sum_{p:e\in\partial p}|x_p|,\quad
 S(x)=2\left(\sum_es_e(x)^2\right)^{1/2},\quad
 \Omega(x)=8\pi\sum_es_e(x)=32\pi\sum_p|x_p|.
 \tag{GPR27}
\]
In the metric where the original \(X_{e,\alpha}\) are orthonormal, each factor is a radius-two three-sphere, with Ricci tensor one half its metric and diameter \(2\pi\). Write \(u=\log\psi_x\), \(\Delta=-K\), and \(w_e=|\nabla_eu|^2\). The full vacuum equation says \(\Delta u+|\nabla u|^2=(V_x-E)/\kappa\). Differentiating it and the squared partial gradient gives the complete product Bochner identity
\[
 (\Delta+2\nabla u\cdot\nabla)w_e
 =2\sum_f|\nabla_f\nabla_eu|_{\rm HS}^2+w_e
       +2\kappa^{-1}\langle\nabla_eu,\nabla_eV_x\rangle.
 \tag{GPR28}
\]
The factor-\(e\) curvature contributes \(2\operatorname{Ric}_e=w_e\); mixed factors have zero curvature. The derivative of \(-|\nabla u|^2\) cancels the displayed drift, retaining every mixed Hessian. For an original face containing \(e\), bi-invariance and inversion reduce its trace gradient to that of \(2u_0\) on the radius-two sphere, with squared norm \(1-u_0^2\le1\). Consequently \(\|\nabla_eV_x\|_\infty\le\kappa s_e\). At the maximum of \(w_e\), (GPR28) gives \(0\ge w_e-2s_e\sqrt{w_e}\). Thus
\[
 \|\nabla_e\log\psi_x\|_\infty\le2s_e,
 \quad\|\nabla_e\log\rho_x\|_\infty\le4s_e,
 \quad\|\nabla v_x\|_\infty\le S,
 \quad\frac{\max\rho_x}{\min\rho_x}\le e^\Omega.
 \tag{GPR29}
\]
The last inequality follows by moving each original link along a shortest geodesic. It uses \(\sum_es_e=4\sum_p|x_p|\), including boundary incidences. Normalization gives \(e^{-\Omega}\le\rho_x\le e^\Omega\).

Write \(m_\rho=\min\rho_x\), \(M_\rho=\max\rho_x\), \(q_\rho=M_\rho/m_\rho\le e^\Omega\). For physical \(h\in H^1\), minimize over the constant, then use the Haar mean and the exact physical gap \(3\):
\[
 \operatorname{Var}_{\mu_x}h
 \le M_\rho\int|h-P_Hh|^2dU
 \le\frac{M_\rho}{3m_\rho}\int\Gamma(\bar h,h)d\mu_x.
\]
Therefore
\[
 \gamma_x:=\delta(x)/\kappa\ge3/q_\rho\ge3e^{-\Omega},\qquad
 \|A_x^\dagger\|_{2\to2}\le e^\Omega/(3\kappa).
 \tag{GPR30}
\]
For the original zero-Haar-mean energy norm \(\|h\|_{\dot H^1_H}^2=\int\Gamma(\bar h,h)dU\), a quantitative inverse estimate follows directly from (GPR10). Test \(\mathcal L_xh=Kf-\mu_xKf\) against \(h-\mu_xh\), and integrate \(Kf\) in Haar measure with the full factor \(\rho_x\). The two terms are \(\int\rho_x\nabla\bar h\cdot\nabla f\) and \(2\int\rho_x(\bar h-\mu_x\bar h)\nabla v_x\cdot\nabla f\). Cauchy–Schwarz and (GPR30), followed by the conversion between weighted and Haar gradient norms, prove
\[
 \boxed{\|\mathscr L_x^{-1}\|_{\dot H^1_H\to\dot H^1_H}
 \le\sqrt{q_\rho}\left(1+\frac{2S}{\sqrt{\gamma_x}}\right)
 \le e^{\Omega/2}\left(1+\frac{2S}{\sqrt3}e^{\Omega/2}\right).}
 \tag{GPR31}
\]
The estimate is first proved for smooth \(f\) and then extends by density and the already proved inverse. Its two norms are fixed original Haar energy norms; no weighted norm has been substituted for them.

The next argument gives a much better bound for the finite face response inverse. Let \(F_a=\sum_pa_pW_p\), and fix one original face \(p\) and two distinct edges \(e,f\in\partial p\). No other elementary face contains both \(e\) and \(f\), since two distinct original faces share at most one edge. Conditional on all other links, \(F_a\), as a function of these two links, is
\[
 a_pW_p(U_e,U_f)+B_e(U_e)+B_f(U_f)+B_0,
 \tag{GPR32}
\]
where the \(B_e,B_f\) are finite sums of original single-occurrence trace words with fixed surrounding matrices. This is an exact decomposition, including every adjacent face and its coefficient. Under two-link Haar probability, \(W_p\) has squared norm one and is orthogonal to \(1,B_e,B_f\), by integration in the missing link. Hence, for every constant \(c\),
\[
 \int|F_a-c|^2dU_edU_f\ge |a_p|^2.
 \tag{GPR33}
\]
Let \(p_{ef}(U_e,U_f\mid y)\) be the actual conditional density, where \(y\) denotes every other original link. Its logarithmic derivatives in \(e,f\) obey (GPR29), since its denominator is independent of those links. Moving the two links proves a density ratio at most \(\exp[8\pi(s_e+s_f)]\), and its integral is one. This alone proves conditional variance at least
\[
 e^{-8\pi(s_e+s_f)}|a_p|^2.
 \tag{GPR34}
\]

A polynomial bound follows without replacing the actual density. Choose an arbitrary center \((U_e^0,U_f^0)\) for the two original links. Write its conditional density value as \(p_{ef}^0\). For each of the two links use its translated stereographic coordinates
\[
 U=U^0\frac{(1-|t|^2)I+2i\sum_{\alpha=1}^3t_\alpha\sigma_\alpha}{1+|t|^2},\qquad
 dU=\frac4{\pi^2}(1+|t|^2)^{-3}d^3t.
 \tag{GPR35}
\]
The exceptional point of this chart has Haar measure zero. Its distance from \(U^0\) in the original radius-two metric is \(4\arctan|t|\). For \(t\in[-r,r]^3\), this distance is at most \(4\sqrt3r\). Thus throughout the product of the two cubes the conditional density is at least
\[
 p_{ef}^0\exp[-16\sqrt3r(s_e+s_f)].
 \tag{GPR36}
\]
Every original occurrence of \(U\) or \(U^{-1}\) in (GPR32) has the common denominator \(1+|t|^2\) and a numerator of degree at most two. Consequently
\[
 P(t,t')=(1+|t|^2)(1+|t'|^2)(F_a-c)
 \tag{GPR37}
\]
is a real polynomial of degree at most two in each three-variable block and total degree at most four. Denote its coefficient vector by \(b\). Its nine coefficients of \(t_\alpha t'_\beta\) have a stronger bound than the estimate from its total degree. The first derivatives of both denominators vanish at the center, and the other terms in (GPR32) depend on only one link. Thus these nine coefficients are exactly \(a_p\partial_{t_\alpha}\partial_{t'_\beta}W_p(0,0)\).

To evaluate them with the original metric, each stereographic tangent in (GPR35) is \(-4T_\alpha\). Inversions contribute the corresponding minus sign and adjoint rotation. Move the fixed unitary factors around the trace and conjugate each generator through those factors. The two generator triples are changed only by real orthogonal adjoint matrices and orientation signs. Therefore the sum of squares of the nine mixed derivatives equals \(16^2\sum_{\alpha,\beta}|\operatorname{Tr}(T_\alpha T_\beta G_0)|^2\), where \(G_0\) is the original face holonomy at the chosen two-link center and the fixed exterior. Write \(G_0=q_0I+i\sum q_\gamma\sigma_\gamma\), with \(q_0^2+\sum q_\gamma^2=1\), and put \(w_p^0:=W_p(U_e^0,U_f^0,y)=\operatorname{Tr}G_0=2q_0\). The original Pauli product gives
\[
 \operatorname{Tr}(T_\alpha T_\beta G_0)
 =-\tfrac12q_0\delta_{\alpha\beta}
   +\tfrac12\sum_\gamma\varepsilon_{\alpha\beta\gamma}q_\gamma,
 \quad
 \sum_{\alpha,\beta}|\operatorname{Tr}(T_\alpha T_\beta G_0)|^2
 =\tfrac14(3q_0^2+2\sum_\gamma q_\gamma^2).
 \tag{GPR37a}
\]
The two orientations and fixed exterior factors have not been removed from the face word; their exact adjoint maps preserve this Frobenius sum. Consequently its actual mixed coefficient sum is
\[
 \sum_{\alpha,\beta}|b_{t_\alpha t'_\beta}|^2
 =|a_p|^2(128+16(w_p^0)^2)\ge128|a_p|^2.
 \tag{GPR37b}
\]
This calculation is uniform even when the base holonomy has trace zero or is central.

For the three monomials \(1,z,z^2\) on \([-1,1]\), the integral Gram matrix is
\[
 G=\begin{pmatrix}2&0&2/3\\0&2/3&0\\2/3&0&2/5\end{pmatrix}\ge\tfrac18I.
 \tag{GPR38}
\]
Indeed the middle diagonal after subtraction is \(13/24>0\), and the remaining \(2\times2\) block has first entry \(15/8>0\) and determinant \(41/576>0\). Tensoring six copies and restricting to the actual monomials gives, for \(0<r\le1\),
\[
 \int_{[-r,r]^6}|P|^2dt\,dt'
 \ge8^{-6}r^6\sum_\alpha|b_\alpha|^2r^{2|\alpha|}
 \ge128\,8^{-6}r^{10}|a_p|^2.
 \tag{GPR39}
\]
In the first inequality the coefficient of each monomial after \(t=rz\) is \(b_\alpha r^{|\alpha|}\). In the second inequality keep the nine mixed degree-two coefficients just evaluated; all other coefficient squares are nonnegative and remain present in the first expression. The Haar factors in (GPR35) and the squared denominator in (GPR37) contribute power five of each \(1+|t|^2\), giving the complete conditional bound
\[
 I_r(U_e^0,U_f^0;c):=
 \int_{U_e^0D_r\times U_f^0D_r}p_{ef}(U_e,U_f\mid y)|F_a-c|^2dU_edU_f
 \ge p_{ef}^0\frac{128(4/\pi^2)^2}{8^6}
       \frac{r^{10}e^{-16\sqrt3r(s_e+s_f)}}{(1+3r^2)^{10}}|a_p|^2,
 \tag{GPR40}
\]
where \(D_r\) is the stereographic image of \([-r,r]^3\) centered at the identity. Equation (GPR40) holds for every center, every \(r\in(0,1]\), all exterior links and every constant \(c\). Its mixed-derivative bound (GPR37b) is uniform in the center. This permits averaging rather than fixing a density maximum.

Integrate (GPR40) over both centers with their original Haar probability. The right side integrates \(p_{ef}^0\) to one. On the left, Fubini and Haar invariance give exactly
\[
 \int I_r(U_e^0,U_f^0;c)dU_e^0dU_f^0
 =v_r^2\int p_{ef}(U_e,U_f\mid y)|F_a-c|^2dU_edU_f,
 \qquad
 v_r=\frac4{\pi^2}\int_{[-r,r]^3}(1+|t|^2)^{-3}d^3t.
 \tag{GPR40a}
\]
Indeed write \(U_e=U_e^0q\), \(U_f=U_f^0q'\) with \(q,q'\in D_r\). For each fixed pair \(q,q'\), the two translations preserve Haar measure, so integrating the centers produces the same full conditional integral. Integrating \(q,q'\) contributes exactly \(v_r^2\). The upper bound \(v_r\le(4/\pi^2)8r^3\) retains the original Haar factor and gives, after division and minimization over \(c\),
\[
 \boxed{\operatorname{Var}(F_a\mid y)
 \ge\frac2{8^6}\frac{r^4e^{-16\sqrt3r(s_e+s_f)}}{(1+3r^2)^{10}}|a_p|^2,
 \qquad0<r\le1.}
 \tag{GPR40b}
\]
The factor two is \(128/8^2\); the two local Haar constants cancel only in this exact averaging identity. The power \(r^4\) is the squared degree-two mixed component, with the six-dimensional local volume canceled by \(v_r^2\). No independence of the actual vacuum links or modification of their density is used.

For a simple explicit choice, set
\[
 C_*:=\frac1{e\,2^{37}},\qquad
 t_p(x)=\min_{\substack{e,f\in\partial p\\e\ne f}}(s_e+s_f),\qquad
 \eta_p(x)=\max\left\{e^{-8\pi t_p},\ C_*(1+16\sqrt3t_p)^{-4}\right\}.
 \tag{GPR41}
\]
Choose the minimizing two original edges and \(r=(1+16\sqrt3t_p)^{-1}\) in (GPR40b). Its exponential is at least \(e^{-1}\), and \((1+3r^2)^{10}\le4^{10}\). Since \(2\cdot4^{-10}\cdot8^{-6}=2^{-37}\), this gives exactly the second entry of (GPR41). Combining it with (GPR34), conditional variance and the law of total variance proves \(\operatorname{Var}_{\mu_x}F_a\ge\eta_p|a_p|^2\) for each original face. Summing the resulting inequalities \( |a_p|^2\le\operatorname{Var}(F_a)/\eta_p\) gives
\[
 C(x):=[\operatorname{Cov}_{\mu_x}(W_p,W_q)]_{p,q}\ge c_0(x)I_M,
 \quad
 c_0(x)=\max\left\{e^{-\Omega(x)},\ \left(\sum_p\eta_p(x)^{-1}\right)^{-1}\right\}>0.
 \tag{GPR42}
\]
The first entry follows independently from \(\rho_x\ge e^{-\Omega}\) and Haar orthogonality. Retaining the maximum preserves both bounds. Equation (GPR40b) can be optimized further in \(r\) without changing the proof.

For the response estimate, put \(r_e=\#\{p:e\in\partial p\}\le4\) and
\[
 C_L^{\nabla}=\max_p\sum_{e\in\partial p}r_e\le16.
 \tag{GPR43}
\]
For each link, Cauchy–Schwarz in the actual incident face list and \(|\nabla_eW_p|\le1\) give
\[
 \Gamma(\bar F_a,F_a)\le\sum_er_e\sum_{p:e\in\partial p}|a_p|^2
 \le C_L^{\nabla}\sum_p|a_p|^2.
 \tag{GPR44}
\]
All boundary incidences are present; \(16\) is only an upper bound for the exact maximum. If \(F_a^0=F_a-\mu_xF_a\), spectral Cauchy–Schwarz for \(\mathcal L_x\) gives
\[
 \|F_a^0\|_{L^2(\mu_x)}^4
 \le\langle F_a^0,\mathcal L_x^\dagger F_a^0\rangle_{\mu_x}
       \langle F_a^0,\mathcal L_xF_a^0\rangle_{\mu_x}.
\]
The first factor is \(a^*R^{\rm phys}a\), by (GPR8) and (GPR20); the second is the integral in (GPR44). Thus
\[
 \boxed{R^{\rm phys}(x)\ge\frac{c_0(x)^2}{C_L^{\nabla}}I_M
          \ge\frac{c_0(x)^2}{16}I_M,\qquad
 \|(R^{\rm phys})^{-1}\|_{\ell^2\to\ell^2}
          \le16c_0(x)^{-2}.}
 \tag{GPR45}
\]
For an upper bound, \(|F_a|_\infty\le2\sqrt M\|a\|_2\) gives \(C(x)\le4MI_M\), while Haar comparison gives \(C(x)\le M_\rho I_M\). Consequently
\[
 R^{\rm phys}(x)\le\frac{\min(4M,M_\rho)}{\gamma_x}I_M
 \le\frac{4M}{3}e^\Omega I_M.
 \tag{GPR46}
\]
The distinct scales in (GPR31), (GPR45) and (GPR46) are part of the conclusions: the face response inverse has a polynomial bound from local conditioning; this does not bound the full physical spectral inverse by that same polynomial.

\subsection*{6. The exact joint path and what the bounds decide}

Retain the original sequence, for all its admissible integers \(j\ge2\):
\[
\begin{gathered}
 L_j=j^4,\quad a_j=1/(100j),\quad g_j^2=\kappa_*/(200j),\quad
 \kappa_j=\kappa_*,\quad \xi_j=10000j^2/\kappa_*^2,\\
 N_j=3(2j^4)(2j^4+1)^2,\qquad
 M_j=3(2j^4)^2(2j^4+1),\qquad\kappa_*>0.
\end{gathered}\tag{GPR47}
\]
Every source is \(x_p=\xi_j\). Thus \(s_e=r_e\xi_j\), \(\sum_es_e=4M_j\xi_j\), and
\[
 \Omega_j=32\pi\xi_jM_j
 =\frac{320000\pi}{\kappa_*^2}j^2\,3(2j^4)^2(2j^4+1),\qquad
 S_j=2\xi_j\sqrt{\sum_er_e^2}\le8\xi_j\sqrt{N_j}.
 \tag{GPR48}
\]
Equations (GPR10), (GPR14)–(GPR18) and (GPR22) hold at every one of these points, even though they eventually lie outside the old series disk. Explicitly,
\[
 \delta_j\ge3\kappa_*e^{-\Omega_j},\qquad
 \|\mathscr L_j^{-1}\|_{\dot H^1_H\to\dot H^1_H}
 \le e^{\Omega_j/2}
       \left(1+\frac{16\xi_j\sqrt{N_j}}{\sqrt3}e^{\Omega_j/2}\right).
 \tag{GPR49}
\]
These finite bounds deteriorate along the path.

The local polynomial bound retains a very different dependence. Since \(t_p\le8\xi_j\), put
\[
 \eta_j=\max\left\{e^{-64\pi\xi_j},\ C_*(1+128\sqrt3\xi_j)^{-4}\right\},
 \qquad c_j=\max\{e^{-\Omega_j},\eta_j/M_j\}.
 \tag{GPR50}
\]
All original faces, including boundary faces, obey \(\eta_p\ge\eta_j\). Hence
\[
 \boxed{C(x_j)\ge c_jI_{M_j},\quad
 R^{\rm phys}(x_j)\ge\frac{C_*^2}{16M_j^2(1+128\sqrt3\,10000j^2/\kappa_*^2)^8}I_{M_j},}
 \tag{GPR51}
\]
and
\[
 \boxed{\|(R^{\rm phys}(x_j))^{-1}\|_{\ell^2\to\ell^2}
 \le16C_*^{-2}M_j^2
       (1+128\sqrt3\,10000j^2/\kappa_*^2)^8.}
 \tag{GPR52}
\]
For each fixed \(\kappa_*>0\), the right side is \(O(j^{40})\), with every coefficient retained in (GPR52): \(M_j^2=O(j^{24})\), and the other factor is \(O(j^{16})\). This is an explicit bound for the inverse susceptibility on the unchanged Euclidean face coefficient norm, rather than an assertion of a nonvanishing uniform response. The lower bound itself tends to zero.

An additional actual-vacuum bound retains the original weak-coupling energy calculation. For homogeneous \(\xi>0\), put \(D=\sum_p(2-W_p)\ge0\). A unit comparison vector with independent one-link factors proportional to \(e^{t u_0}\) has kinetic energy at most \(3Nt/8\) and expected \(D\le6M/t\). The latter follows from \(1-\mathbb E_tu_0\le3/(4t)\) and the ordered four-link expectation \(\mathbb E_tW_p=2(\mathbb E_tu_0)^4\). To verify the scalar bound, write \(z=1-u_0\): its tilted density is a gamma density proportional to \(e^{-2tz}z^{1/2}\), with mean \(3/(4t)\), multiplied by the decreasing factor \(\sqrt{2-z}\mathbf1_{[0,2]}(z)\). The two-copy covariance identity for an increasing and a decreasing function proves that this multiplier cannot increase its mean. Integration of \(\operatorname{div}(e^{2tu_0}\nabla u_0)\), using \(|\nabla u_0|^2=(1-u_0^2)/4\) and \(\Delta u_0=-3u_0/4\), gives the stated kinetic bound. Taking \(t=4\sqrt{\xi M/N}\) yields
\[
 E(\xi)\le3\kappa\sqrt{\xi NM},\qquad
 \mu_\xi(D)\le3\sqrt{NM}/\sqrt\xi.
 \tag{GPR53}
\]
The full and physical ground energies agree by Section 1, so using this comparison vector requires no change of physical state space.

Since \((2-W_p)^2\le4(2-W_p)\), Cauchy–Schwarz on the full face list gives
\[
 C(\xi)\le\min\{4M,12\sqrt{NM}/\sqrt\xi\}I_M,
 \quad
 R^{\rm phys}(\xi)\le\frac{e^{32\pi\xi M}}3
          \min\{4M,12\sqrt{NM}/\sqrt\xi\}I_M.
 \tag{GPR54}
\]
In particular at the exact path source the second covariance entry is \(3\kappa_*\sqrt{N_jM_j}/(25j)\). Also (GPR20) proves, at each fixed original box,
\[
 -\frac d{d\xi}\mu_\xi(D)=2\mathbf1^tR^{\rm phys}(\xi)\mathbf1>0,
 \quad
 \int_{\xi}^{\infty}\mathbf1^tR^{\rm phys}(t)\mathbf1\,dt
 =\tfrac12\mu_\xi(D)\le\frac{3\sqrt{NM}}{2\sqrt\xi}.
 \tag{GPR55}
\]
The endpoint limit \(\mu_t(D)\to0\) is proved by (GPR53), so this integral identity has no unproved endpoint assumption. It gives actual integrated homogeneous response; it does not select a new coupling in place of \(\xi_j\), and does not bound its derivative at \(\xi_j\) by the integral average.

The established next step beyond the small-source disk is therefore exact and quantitative: the physical Jacobian and the assembled original quotient are invertible at every path point, the Wilson expectation map has a global inverse onto its exact finite-box image, and the original finite face-response inverse grows at most by the explicit polynomial in (GPR52). These statements alone do not determine the low-energy spectral weight of the actual global phase states, the asymptotic excitation gap, a limiting Hilbert space or Hamiltonian, or continuum theory identification. In particular (GPR49) is a positive lower bound that tends to zero; that fact never proves a vanishing upper bound for the actual gap. The next spectral calculation must control the actual path vacuum observables and their spectral measures in the unchanged original Hamiltonian, using these inverses and response identities as available maps.


\begin{figure}[htbp]\centering\includegraphics[width=\linewidth,height=.72\textheight,keepaspectratio]{GLOBAL_PHYSICAL_RESPONSE.png}\caption{Exact physical source inverse, observation map and all-centres conditional averaging. Proofs GPR1–GPR26 and GPR35–GPR52; original domains, means, scalars and Haar factors retained.}\end{figure}
\clearpage
\section*{Polynomial conditional bounds in the original SU(2) metric}

This note proves an explicit bound for every quaternion polynomial of
degree at most two, retaining its entire affine remainder. The exponent
eight is optimal under the stated logarithmic score hypothesis. Applied
to the original two-link phase calculation, it replaces the exponential
losses in JP43, JP47 and JP48 by polynomial losses on the unchanged
joint path. It does not determine the complete shrinking-window endpoint.
Section 9 strengthens the application further by using a larger
coefficient already present in the original phase, with complete
conjugation and derivative maps.

\subsection*{1. Metric, density and the exact polynomial space}

Write an original group element as
\[
 U=x_0I+2\sum_{\alpha=1}^3x_\alpha T_\alpha,\qquad
 T_\alpha=-i\sigma_\alpha/2,\qquad
 \sum_{\alpha=0}^3x_\alpha^2=1 .
 \tag{PC1}
\]
Thus \(c(U)=x_0=\operatorname{tr}U/2\) and
\(q_\alpha(U)=2x_\alpha=-2\operatorname{tr}(T_\alpha U)\).
The metric making the three original left fields orthonormal is the
round metric of radius two. Its distance from identity is
\(d(I,U)=2\arccos x_0\), its diameter is \(2\pi\), and normalized Haar
measure is denoted by \(dU\). A ball of physical radius \(r\) has volume
\[
 v(r)={r-\sin r\over2\pi}.
 \tag{PC2}
\]
Indeed the polar angle \(\theta=d(I,U)/2\) has density
\((2/\pi)\sin^2\theta\,d\theta\) on \([0,\pi]\).
The radius follows directly because the velocity of \(\exp(tT_\alpha)\)
has Euclidean length \(1/2\) in the unit quaternion sphere. Left
multiplication is orthogonal, so multiplying that sphere metric by
four gives precisely the original orthonormal left fields.
Rotation invariance also gives \(\int x_i^2=1/4\).
Writing \(a=\int x_i^4\) and \(b=\int x_i^2x_j^2\) for \(i\ne j\),
the rotation \(x_i\mapsto(x_i+x_j)/\sqrt2\) gives \(a=3b\).
The identity \((\sum_i x_i^2)^2=1\) gives \(4a+12b=1\).
Thus \(a=1/8\), \(b=1/24\); odd coordinate moments vanish.

Let \(\mathcal P_2\) consist of restrictions to this sphere of complex
polynomials of degree at most two in the four \(x_\alpha\).
Its restriction dimension is fourteen: the fifteen coefficients of an ambient
polynomial have the single restriction relation
\(\sum x_\alpha^2-1=0\). Equivalently its harmonic dimensions are
\(1+4+9\). To verify uniqueness of the restriction relation,
a degree-two polynomial vanishing on the sphere has zero linear
part by comparing \(x\) and \(-x\). Its symmetric quadratic form
is constant on all unit vectors, hence is a scalar multiple of the
identity; comparison with the constant term gives the stated relation.
A positive probability density \(\rho\) is assumed to satisfy
\[
 \int\rho\,dU=1,\qquad
 |\nabla\log\rho|\le B,\qquad B\ge0.
 \tag{PC3}
\]
Smooth densities suffice for all applications below. The proof also
applies whenever \(\log\rho\) is \(B\)-Lipschitz. In particular,
integration along a shortest original geodesic gives
\[
 e^{-Bd(U,V)}\rho(V)\le\rho(U)\le e^{Bd(U,V)}\rho(V).
 \tag{PC4}
\]
No probability density or polynomial is replaced by a reference state.

Define the explicit constant
\[
 c_{\rm cap}
 ={ \cos^{14}(1/4)\over1024\cdot105041\cdot\pi\cdot3^{9/2}}
 >2^{-37}.
 \tag{PC5}
\]
The principal bound, proved in Sections 2–4, is
\[
 \boxed{\displaystyle
 \int|P(U)|^2\rho(U)\,dU
 \ge c_{\rm cap}(1+B)^{-8}\int|P(U)|^2\,dU
 \ge2^{-37}(1+B)^{-8}\int|P(U)|^2\,dU
 \quad(P\in\mathcal P_2).}
 \tag{PC6}
\]
This applies componentwise to vector-valued polynomials, with all
components summed. Its constants do not depend on any coefficient,
affine term, cap centre, box size or coupling.

\subsection*{2. An exact polynomial Gram estimate on a cube}

Use probability measure \(dt/2\) on \([-1,1]\). The orthonormal
polynomials through degree four are
\[
 \begin{array}{ll}
 L_0=1,&L_1=\sqrt3\,t,\\
 L_2={\sqrt5\over2}(3t^2-1),&
 L_3={\sqrt7\over2}(5t^3-3t),\\
 L_4={3\over8}(35t^4-30t^2+3).
 \end{array}
 \tag{PC7}
\]
Their orthonormality follows by integrating each monomial:
\(\int_{-1}^1t^kdt/2=0\) for odd \(k\) and \(1/(k+1)\) for even
\(k\). Their coefficient squared norms in the monomial basis are
respectively
\[
 t_0=1,\quad t_1=3,\quad t_2={25\over2},\quad
 t_3={119\over2},\quad t_4={9603\over32}.
 \tag{PC8}
\]

Consider all monomials \(z^\alpha\) in three variables with
\(|\alpha|\le4\). Their number is \(\binom73=35\). Their Gram
matrix \(G\), for probability measure \(dz/8\) on \([-1,1]^3\),
has entries
\[
 G_{\alpha\beta}
 =\prod_{i=1}^3
 \begin{cases}
 0,&\alpha_i+\beta_i\text{ odd},\\
 (\alpha_i+\beta_i+1)^{-1},&\alpha_i+\beta_i\text{ even}.
 \end{cases}
 \tag{PC9}
\]
It is positive definite: a polynomial with zero integral squared norm
vanishes on an open cube, hence all its coefficients vanish.
The thirty-five products
\(L_{\ell_1}(z_1)L_{\ell_2}(z_2)L_{\ell_3}(z_3)\),
\(\ell_1+\ell_2+\ell_3\le4\), form an orthonormal basis of exactly
this space, by their triangular leading coefficients.
If \(C\) is their coefficient matrix, \(CGC^*=I\), so
\(G^{-1}=C^*C\). Therefore
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 \operatorname{tr}G^{-1}
 &=\sum_{\ell_1+\ell_2+\ell_3\le4}t_{\ell_1}t_{\ell_2}t_{\ell_3}\\
 &=1+9+{129\over2}+{861\over2}+{88881\over32}
   ={105041\over32}.
 \end{aligned}\tag{PC10}
\]
The displayed five contributions are the coefficients of degrees
zero through four in
\((1+3z+(25/2)z^2+(119/2)z^3+(9603/32)z^4)^3\).
The largest eigenvalue of the positive \(G^{-1}\) is at most its
trace. Thus every total-degree-four polynomial
\(Q(z)=\sum_{|\alpha|\le4}a_\alpha z^\alpha\) satisfies
\[
 \int_{[-1,1]^3}|Q(z)|^2{dz\over8}
 \ge {32\over105041}\sum_{|\alpha|\le4}|a_\alpha|^2.
 \tag{PC11}
\]
This estimate is proved from the complete Gram matrix, including
every lower-degree monomial. No affine coefficient has been omitted.

\subsection*{3. The exact chart and a cap-average lower bound}

Use the stereographic coordinate map into the original quaternion sphere:
\[
 x_0={1-|y|^2\over1+|y|^2},\qquad
 (x_1,x_2,x_3)={2y\over1+|y|^2},
 \qquad
 Q(y)=(1+|y|^2)^2P(x(y)).
 \tag{PC12}
\]
This map covers \(SU(2)\setminus\{-I\}\), with inverse
\(y=(x_1,x_2,x_3)/(1+x_0)\). The equality
\(P(x(y))=Q(y)/(1+|y|^2)^2\) retains its entire denominator.
The numerator \(Q\) has total degree at most four, and no coefficient
of \(P\) is discarded. The missing point is handled by continuity.

Write \(Q=\sum_{|\alpha|\le4}a_\alpha y^\alpha\), \(t=|y|^2\).
For each degree \(d\), the multinomial formula gives
\(\sum_{|\alpha|=d}y^{2\alpha}\le t^d\). Since the coefficients of
\((1+t)^4\) dominate those of \(\sum_{d=0}^4t^d\), Cauchy–Schwarz
proves the exact comparison
\[
 |P(x(y))|^2
 \le\left(\sum_\alpha|a_\alpha|^2\right)
       {\sum_{|\alpha|\le4}y^{2\alpha}\over(1+|y|^2)^4}
 \le\sum_\alpha|a_\alpha|^2,\qquad
 \|P\|_{\rm Haar}^2\le\sum_\alpha|a_\alpha|^2.
 \tag{PC13}
\]
The same bound holds at the missing point by continuity.

In these coordinates normalized Haar measure is exactly
\[
 dU={4\over\pi^2}{dy\over(1+|y|^2)^3}.
 \tag{PC14}
\]
The Jacobian follows from the conformal length factor
\(2/(1+|y|^2)\) of the unit sphere and its volume \(2\pi^2\).
Normalization of Haar does not change when the metric is expressed
at radius two; that metric remains in the ball radius.

For \(0<r\le1\), the original radius-\(r\) cap is
\(|y|\le a_r=\tan(r/4)\). It contains the cube
\([-\delta_r,\delta_r]^3\), where \(\delta_r=a_r/\sqrt3<1\).
The coefficient vector of \(Q(\delta_r z)\) has squared norm
\(\sum_\alpha|a_\alpha|^2\delta_r^{2|\alpha|}
\ge\delta_r^8\sum_\alpha|a_\alpha|^2\).
Combining (PC11)–(PC14), with the full denominator retained, yields
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 \int_{d(I,U)\le r}|P(U)|^2dU
 &={4\over\pi^2}\int_{|y|\le a_r}
          {|Q(y)|^2\over(1+|y|^2)^7}dy\\
 &\ge {1024\over105041\pi^2}\,
       {\delta_r^{11}\over(1+a_r^2)^7}\,
                       \|P\|_{\rm Haar}^2.
 \end{aligned}\tag{PC15}
\]
The exponent eleven is eight from the worst coefficient scaling and
three from volume. Since
\(a_r\ge r/4\),
\((1+a_r^2)^{-7}\ge\cos^{14}(1/4)\), and
\(v(r)\le r^3/(12\pi)\), division by the actual cap volume gives
\[
 {1\over v(r)}\int_{d(I,U)\le r}|P(U)|^2dU
 \ge c_{\rm cap}r^8\|P\|_{\rm Haar}^2.
 \tag{PC16}
\]
For the volume inequality, \(\sin r\ge r-r^3/6\) on \([0,1]\);
substitute this in (PC2). All constants in (PC16) follow by
substituting \(\delta_r\ge r/(4\sqrt3)\) in (PC15):
\(12288/(4^{11}3^{11/2})=1/(1024\,3^{9/2})\).

Left multiplication by any cap centre is an orthogonal transformation
of the four quaternion coordinates. It preserves \(\mathcal P_2\),
the original metric and Haar norm. Thus (PC16) holds with the cap
centred at every \(V\), for the original \(P\). This is the precise
coordinate map giving uniformity in the centre.

The dyadic inequality in (PC5) follows without decimal estimates.
One has
\(\cos(1/4)\ge31/32\), hence
\(\cos^{14}(1/4)\ge1-14/32=9/16\);
also \(\pi<4\) and \(3^{9/2}=81\sqrt3<162\).
Finally
\((9/16)2^{37}>1024\cdot105041\cdot4\cdot162\).
These inequalities prove \(c_{\rm cap}>2^{-37}\).

\subsection*{4. Averaging removes the volume loss}

Fix \(0<r\le1\), and integrate (PC16) over cap centres against the
actual unit density \(\rho(V)dV\). The score comparison (PC4) and
Fubini's theorem give
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 v(r)c_{\rm cap}r^8\|P\|_{\rm Haar}^2
 &\le \int\rho(V)\!
             \int_{d(U,V)\le r}|P(U)|^2dU\,dV\\
 &\le e^{Br}\int |P(U)|^2\rho(U)
                  \int_{d(U,V)\le r}dV\,dU\\
 &=e^{Br}v(r)\int|P(U)|^2\rho(U)dU .
 \end{aligned}\tag{PC17}
\]
Every cap has the same Haar volume. It therefore cancels, and
\[
 \int|P|^2\rho\,dU
 \ge c_{\rm cap}e^{-Br}r^8\|P\|_{\rm Haar}^2.
 \tag{PC18}
\]
This is stronger than using only a ball about a density maximum.

Choose \(r=2/(B+2)\) and \(t=B/(B+2)\in[0,1)\).
Then \(Br=2t\) and \(r(1+B)=1+t\).
Since \(\log(1+t)\ge t/2\),
\((1+t)^8e^{-2t}\ge1\). Hence
\(e^{-Br}r^8\ge(1+B)^{-8}\), proving (PC6).
An explicit stronger coefficient, useful when \(B\) is known, is
\[
 \Lambda_2(B)=c_{\rm cap}
 \begin{cases}
 e^{-B},&0\le B\le8,\\
 (8/(eB))^8,&B\ge8.
 \end{cases}
 \qquad
 \Lambda_2(B)\ge c_{\rm cap}(1+B)^{-8}.
 \tag{PC19}
\]
Indeed \(r=\min(1,8/B)\) maximizes \(e^{-Br}r^8\) on \((0,1]\).
The preceding radius choice proves the final inequality for this
maximum as well.

\subsection*{5. Exponent eight is optimal under the score bound alone}

For \(B\ge1\), define a smooth positive density using the unchanged
metric and coordinates:
\[
 f_B(U)=\sqrt{B^{-2}+8(1-c(U))},\qquad
 \rho_B(U)={e^{-Bf_B(U)}\over Z_B},\qquad
 Z_B=\int e^{-Bf_B(U)}dU,\qquad P_*(U)=(1-c(U))^2.
 \tag{PC20}
\]
The density is smooth on the whole compact group because the radicand
is strictly positive. Since
\(|\nabla c|^2=(1-c^2)/4\),
\[
 |\nabla f_B|^2
 ={4(1-c^2)\over B^{-2}+8(1-c)}
 \le{1+c\over2}\le1.
\]
Thus \(|\nabla\log\rho_B|\le B\), with exactly the same score
parameter as (PC3). The polynomial \(P_*\) belongs to
\(\mathcal P_2\) and \(\|P_*\|_{\rm Haar}^2=21/8\):
expand \((1-c)^4\), use the odd moments zero and the sphere
moments \(\int c^2=1/4,\int c^4=1/8\).

Let \(t=\sqrt{8(1-c)}\in[0,4]\). The exact Haar density of \(t\)
is
\[
 {t^2\over4\pi}\sqrt{1-t^2/16}\,dt,\qquad
 |P_*|^2={t^8\over4096}.
 \tag{PC21}
\]
This follows by substituting \(c=1-t^2/8\) into the polar measure
in Section 1. For \(I_k=\int_0^\infty u^k e^{-\sqrt{1+u^2}}du\),
which is finite and strictly positive, the substitution \(u=Bt\)
and dominated convergence give
\[
 B^3Z_B\longrightarrow {I_2\over4\pi},\qquad
 B^{11}\int|P_*|^2e^{-Bf_B}dU
                  \longrightarrow {I_{10}\over16384\pi},
 \qquad
 B^8\int|P_*|^2\rho_B\,dU
                  \longrightarrow {I_{10}\over4096I_2}>0.
 \tag{PC22}
\]
The dominators are \(u^2e^{-u}\) and \(u^{10}e^{-u}\),
respectively, after extending each integral by zero past \(4B\).
Thus no bound of the form \(c(1+B)^{-k}\|P\|_{\rm Haar}^2\),
with a fixed positive \(c\) and \(k<8\), holds for every density
and polynomial in (PC3) and \(\mathcal P_2\).
This obstruction identifies the exact fourth-order vanishing direction
\((1-c)^2\), with a smooth admissible family realizing its concentration.

\subsection*{6. A stronger receiving bound for the original colour vector}

The even-mass application below needs \(|q(U)|^2\), which has
additional structure. Let the original radius-\(r\) cap, \(r\le1\),
be centred at \(V\), and write \(U=VZ\), with \(Z\) in the identity
cap. Set \(t_r\) equal to the cap average of \(c(Z)^2\).
Rotation invariance in the three vector coordinates gives
\[
 \operatorname{av}_{Z}|c(VZ)|^2
 =c(V)^2t_r+[1-c(V)^2]{1-t_r\over3}.
 \tag{PC23}
\]
The cross terms have zero integral. Since
\(c(Z)^2\ge\cos^2(1/2)\ge49/64>1/4\), the right side is at
most \(t_r\). Therefore the cap average of \(|q(U)|^2\) is
smallest at an identity or negative-identity centre, and is at least
\[
 4{\int_0^{r/2}\sin^4\theta\,d\theta
       \over\int_0^{r/2}\sin^2\theta\,d\theta}
 \ge {3\over5}\left({23\over24}\right)^4r^2 .
 \tag{PC24}
\]
For the inequality, use
\(\sin\theta\ge\theta-\theta^3/6\ge(23/24)\theta\)
and \(\sin\theta\le\theta\) for \(0\le\theta\le1/2\).
Integrate the resulting powers \(\theta^4\) and \(\theta^2\).

The same averaging argument (PC17), followed by
\(r=\min(1,2/B)\), now proves
\[
 \boxed{\displaystyle
 \int|q(U)|^2\rho(U)dU
 \ge3c_q(1+B)^{-2},\qquad
 c_q={4\over5e^2}\left({23\over24}\right)^4>{1\over16}.}
 \tag{PC25}
\]
To check the uniform constant, for \(B\le2\) the quantity
\(e^{-B}(1+B)^2\) is at least one: its only interior critical
point is its maximum at \(B=1\), and its endpoint values are
\(1\) and \(9/e^2>1\). For \(B\ge2\), the chosen radius gives
\(e^{-Br}r^2=4e^{-2}B^{-2}\ge4e^{-2}(1+B)^{-2}\).
Finally \(e<3\) and \(64(23/24)^4>45\) prove \(c_q>1/16\).
The full Haar norm here is \(\int|q|^2dU=3\).
The densities (PC20) also give
\(B^2\int|q|^2\rho_BdU\to I_4/I_2>0\), by (PC21) and
\(|q|^2=t^2-t^4/16\). Thus exponent two is optimal for this
particular receiving observable.

\subsection*{7. Application to the unchanged original phase and vacuum}

Retain \(m=2L\), \(n=m+1\), \(h=\pi/(2n)\), and \(L\ge2\).
The original chord sets are
\[
 \mathcal C_2=\{(i,k,l;2):1\le i\le m,\ 0\le k<m,\ 0\le l\le m\},
 \quad
 \mathcal C_3=\{(i,k,l;3):0\le i,k\le m,\ (i,k)\ne(0,0),\
                                      0\le l<m\}.
\]
The original tree has every direction-one edge, direction-two
edges at first coordinate zero, and direction-three edges at first
two coordinates zero. Root paths start at \((0,0,0)\), proceeding
in directions three, two and one. Every coordinate remains the full
word \(Z_c=t_sU_ct_t^{-1}\). The original physical vertex indexed
by \((i,k,l)\) is \(o+a(i-L,k-L,l-L)\).

The complete original rows, with all excluded tree edges retained as
exclusions, are
\[
 \begin{gathered}
 v_0(l)=n^{-1/2},\quad
 v_1(l)=\sqrt{2/n}\cos{\pi(l+1/2)\over n},\quad
 w_1(l)=-\sqrt{2/n}\sin{\pi(l+1)\over n},\\
 a_{(i,k,l;2)}=-v_1(i)w_1(k)v_0(l)/\sqrt2,\qquad
 a_{(i,k,l;3)}=0,\\
 b_{(i,k,l;2)}=v_0(i)w_1(k)v_1(l)/\sqrt2,\qquad
 b_{(i,k,l;3)}=-v_0(i)v_1(k)w_1(l)/\sqrt2,\\
 u_1=\sum_ca_cq(Z_c),\quad u_3=\sum_cb_cq(Z_c),\quad
 \mathcal S=u_1\cdot u_3,\quad
 \mathcal S_{\mathcal R}=\mathcal S\circ\mathcal R,\\
 D=\mathcal S+\mathcal S_{\mathcal R},\quad
 \sigma_*=\sqrt8\sin h,\quad \beta_0={\sigma_*\over2g^2},\quad
 F=p-r=\beta_0(\mathcal S-\mathcal S_{\mathcal R}).
 \end{gathered}\tag{PC26}
\]
Here \(\mathcal R\) is the original direction-one graph reflection,
including every inverse orientation and reflected root.

The full Hamiltonian and actual positive unit vacuum are
\[
 H=\kappa H_0+b_{\rm mag}W,\quad
 H_0=-\sum_{e,\alpha}X_{e,\alpha}^2,\quad
 W=\sum_{\text{all faces }p}(2-\operatorname{tr}U_p),\quad
 \kappa={2g^2\over a},\quad b_{\rm mag}={1\over2g^2a},\quad
 \xi={1\over4g^4},\quad H\psi=E_0\psi,\quad d\mu=\psi^2dU.
 \tag{PC27}
\]
The constant \(2b_{\rm mag}M\), \(M=3m^2(m+1)\), remains in
the potential and vacuum equation. Integration by parts in that
complete equation gives
\(C_F=\langle F\psi,(H-E_0)F\psi\rangle
=\kappa\mu(|\nabla F|^2)\), and \(G=\mu(F^2)\).
The original raw vector is not divided by its norm.

The local score bound, in precisely this metric, is
\[
 |\nabla_e\log\psi^2|\le4r_e\xi .
 \tag{PC28}
\]
Its original proof is
\href{https://github.com/KokunoYumeto/yang-mills-interacting-workbench/blob/485b61423c1f4e1805deb453c1b6b342b27842b6/yang-mills/continuations/20260930-s6-ns-moment-map-bridge/sources/spatial/spatial_continuum.tex#L1415}{the retained original TeX, equations (77)–(79) and (113)–(115)}.
For precision, divide the full vacuum equation by \(\psi\), set
\(u=\log\psi\), and use the partial Bochner identity
\[
 (\Delta+2\nabla u\cdot\nabla)|\nabla_eu|^2
 =2\sum_f|\nabla_f\nabla_eu|^2+|\nabla_eu|^2
       +2\xi\langle\nabla_eu,\nabla_eW\rangle.
\]
The radius-two Ricci tensor supplies the displayed coefficient one,
and every incident face has \(|\nabla_e(2-\operatorname{tr}U_p)|\le1\).
At a maximum of \(|\nabla_eu|^2\), all mixed Hessian squared norms
are nonnegative, giving
\(|\nabla_eu|^2\le2r_e\xi|\nabla_eu|\).
This proves (PC28), including boundary face counts.

The exact tree coordinate map takes physical product Haar to product
Haar in tree transports and the full chords. Gauge-invariant functions
are independent of the tree transports. This follows successively
from \(U_{(s,t)}=t_s^{-1}t_t\) on the tree and
\(U_c=t_s^{-1}Z_ct_t\) on each chord, by left and right Haar
invariance. The actual density in chord coordinates is therefore
the same \(\psi^2\), evaluated with tree links identity. A left
variation of an original chord is its physical left variation.
Every actual conditional density of a chord satisfies (PC28) with
its same \(r_e\). A marginal density also does: differentiation of
its logarithm is the density-weighted average of the original score.
These are exact measure maps, with all correlations retained.

Fix the two original chords
\[
 k_*=(1,0,0;2),\quad b_*=(m,0,0;2),\quad k=Z_{k_*},\
 h_b=Z_{b_*},\quad
 A=-{v_1(1)w_1(0)v_0(0)\over\sqrt2},\quad
 d_0={v_0(0)w_1(0)v_1(0)\over\sqrt2},\quad
 d_*=-4Ad_0={8\cos h\cos(3h)\sin^2(2h)\over n^3}>0.
 \tag{PC29}
\]
Their face counts are respectively three and two, so define
\[
 B_k=12\xi,\qquad B_b=8\xi,\qquad
 \eta_2(B)=c_{\rm cap}(1+B)^{-8},\quad
 \eta_q(B)=c_q(1+B)^{-2}.
 \tag{PC30}
\]

To verify the receiving polynomial degree and all remainders directly,
in the tree presentation put
\[
 A_{k,l}=\left(\prod_{r=0}^{l-1}U_3(m,0,r)\right)
                   \left(\prod_{t=0}^{k-1}U_2(m,t,l)\right).
\]
Empty products are identity, each displayed product is in increasing
index order, and original tree values are
\(U_2(0,k,l)=I,\ U_3(0,0,l)=I\).
Reflection of the full root paths gives the words
\[
 Z^{\mathcal R}_{(i,k,l;2)}
       =A_{k,l}U_2(m-i,k,l)A_{k+1,l}^{-1},\qquad
 Z^{\mathcal R}_{(i,k,l;3)}
       =A_{k,l}U_3(m-i,k,l)A_{k,l+1}^{-1}.
 \tag{PC31}
\]
The reversed direction-one root segments are original tree links;
the other two root segments are exactly the displayed endpoint
products. This proves (PC31), including their order.
The bulk chord \(k_*\) occurs as a reflected target only for
\(c_*=(m-1,0,0;2)\); its endpoint products are \(I,h_b\), so
its reflected word is \(kh_b^{-1}\). It occurs in no \(A_{k,l}\).

Define \(U_\mu=\sum_{c\ne k_*}\mathsf M_{\mu c}q(Z_c)\) and
\(V_\mu=\sum_{c\ne c_*}\mathsf M_{\mu c}q(Z_c^{\mathcal R})\),
where the two complete rows are those in (PC26). These keep every
other original and reflected word. Exact multiplication gives
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 \mathcal S={}&U_1\cdot U_3+(d_0U_1+AU_3)\cdot q(k)
                                         +Ad_0|q(k)|^2,\\
 \mathcal S_{\mathcal R}={}&V_1\cdot V_3
       +(d_0V_1-AV_3)\cdot q(kh_b^{-1})
                                 -Ad_0|q(kh_b^{-1})|^2.
 \end{aligned}\tag{PC32}
\]
Every other coefficient is independent of \(k\), but may depend on
all boundary and exterior chords. Since quaternion multiplication is
linear in each factor, the remaining terms are affine-linear in
the four coordinates of \(k\). Derivatives in \(b_*\) preserve that
affine degree; derivatives in \(k_*\) preserve each harmonic degree.
Thus every receiving function below belongs to the full
\(\mathcal P_2\), with its remainder present.

For \(h_b(t)=\exp(tT_\alpha)h_b\),
\(kh_b(t)^{-1}=kh_b^{-1}\exp(-tT_\alpha)\).
The original quaternion product gives
\(\partial c=q_\alpha/4\) and
\(\partial |q|^2=-2cq_\alpha\). Therefore
\[
 X_{b_*,\alpha}D
 =2Ad_0c(kh_b^{-1})q_\alpha(kh_b^{-1})+R_\alpha(k),
 \tag{PC33}
\]
with the entire affine derivative remainder \(R_\alpha\) retained.
The quadratic term is Haar-orthogonal to that remainder, and
\(\int c q_\alpha\,c q_\beta=\delta_{\alpha\beta}/6\).
These moments follow from rotation invariance:
\(\int x_0^2x_\alpha^2=1/24\) for \(\alpha>0\).
Their three squared norms add to \(d_*^2/8\).
Apply (PC6) separately to all three complete polynomials (PC33),
using the actual \(k_*\) conditional density. Then integrate its
unchanged exterior marginal. This proves
\[
 \boxed{\displaystyle
 \mu(|\nabla D|^2)\ge{d_*^2\over8}\eta_2(B_k),\qquad
 \kappa\beta_0^2\mu(|\nabla D|^2)
                \ge{\kappa\beta_0^2d_*^2\over8}\eta_2(B_k).}
 \tag{PC34}
\]
This directly improves JP43 without a boundary condition or
boundary-band penalty.

For the raw odd phase, the quadratic harmonic parts of (PC32) are
\(-Ad_0\chi_1(k)\) and \(Ad_0\chi_1(kh_b^{-1})\), where
\(\chi_1(U)=4c(U)^2-1\). The constant terms
\(+3Ad_0,-3Ad_0\) remain in the respective remainders.
Sphere moments give
\[
 \int\chi_1(k)^2dk=1,\qquad
 \int\chi_1(k)\chi_1(kh_b^{-1})dk={4c(h_b)^2-1\over3}.
\]
Indeed \(c(kh_b^{-1})=x\cdot y\), and
\(\int x_0^2(x\cdot y)^2=(1+2y_0^2)/24\).
It follows that the quadratic harmonic part of the full \(F\) has
squared norm
\(\beta_0^2d_*^2[1+2c(h_b)^2]/12\), at least
\(\beta_0^2d_*^2/12\). Its \(k_*\)-Casimir is exactly two,
by the degree-two sphere eigenvalue \(2(2+2)/4\). Orthogonality
and left differentiation retain the affine terms and prove
\(\int\sum_\alpha|X_{k_*,\alpha}F|^2dk
\ge\beta_0^2d_*^2/6\).
Applying (PC6) to the full \(F\) and its full three derivatives gives
\[
 \boxed{\displaystyle
 G\ge{\beta_0^2d_*^2\over12}\eta_2(B_k),\qquad
 C_F\ge{\kappa\beta_0^2d_*^2\over6}\eta_2(B_k).}
 \tag{PC35}
\]
These are separate bounds, and do not assert \(C_F\ge2\kappa G\).

For \(D\), the same full conditional calculation gives quadratic
harmonic squared norm \(d_*^2|q(h_b)|^2/24\).
Apply (PC6) to the complete \(D\) in the \(k_*\) conditional,
then (PC25) to the actual \(h_b\) marginal, whose score is at most
\(B_b\). All other variables are integrated in the actual measure.
The result is
\[
 \boxed{\displaystyle
 \mu(D^2)\ge{d_*^2\over8}\eta_2(B_k)\eta_q(B_b).}
 \tag{PC36}
\]
This improves JP48 with a degree-two factor for the arbitrary
polynomial in \(k_*\), and the sharper exponent-two factor for the
specific original \(|q(h_b)|^2\). One may replace \(\eta_2\) by
the stronger \(\Lambda_2\) in every displayed receiving inequality.

\subsection*{8. Exact original-path bounds and the remaining endpoint}

Keep precisely
\[
 L_j=j^4,\quad a_j={1\over100j},\quad
 g_j^2={\kappa_*\over200j},\quad s_j={\kappa_*\over j},
 \quad \kappa_j=\kappa_*>0,\quad
 \xi_j={10000j^2\over\kappa_*^2},\quad j\ge2 .
 \tag{PC37}
\]
Consequently
\[
 \eta_2(B_{k,j})
 =c_{\rm cap}\left(1+{120000j^2\over\kappa_*^2}\right)^{-8},
 \qquad
 \eta_q(B_{b,j})
 =c_q\left(1+{80000j^2\over\kappa_*^2}\right)^{-2}.
 \tag{PC38}
\]
These factors replace the exponential losses without changing a
single original coupling or raw vector. The limits obtained directly
from (PC26) and (PC29) are
\[
 j^3\beta_{0,j}\to{50\sqrt2\pi\over\kappa_*},\qquad
 j^{20}d_{*,j}\to{\pi^2\over4}.
 \tag{PC39}
\]
Put \(K_k=(\kappa_*^2/120000)^8\) and
\(K_b=(\kappa_*^2/80000)^2\); these are only explicitly displayed
constants, not replacements for the path. The right sides in (PC35)
are respectively asymptotic to
\[
 {625\pi^6\over24\kappa_*^2}c_{\rm cap}K_k\,j^{-62},
 \qquad
 {625\pi^6\over12\kappa_*}c_{\rm cap}K_k\,j^{-62}.
 \tag{PC40}
\]
The right side of (PC36) is asymptotic to
\[
 {\pi^4\over128}c_{\rm cap}c_qK_kK_b\,j^{-60}.
 \tag{PC41}
\]
These are asymptotics of the proved lower bounds. They do not assert
that the actual vacuum expectations equal these expressions.

Finally retain every term in the original endpoint. Reflection
invariance gives
\(2\mu(\mathcal SD)=\mu(D^2)\) and
\(2\mu(\nabla\mathcal S\cdot\nabla D)=\mu(|\nabla D|^2)\).
They follow by adding the equal reflected integrals and using
\(D=\mathcal S+\mathcal S_{\mathcal R}\), including reflection
invariance of the original physical metric. Thus the full expansion is
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 \mathscr E(s)
 =\beta_0^2[&
 4\mu(\mathcal S^2)-4\mu(\mathcal SD)+\mu(D^2)\\
 &-{\kappa\over s}
   \{4\mu(|\nabla\mathcal S|^2)
      -4\mu(\nabla\mathcal S\cdot\nabla D)+\mu(|\nabla D|^2)\}]\\
 ={}&4\beta_0^2[\mathcal Q_4-(\kappa/s)\mathcal K_1]
                 -\beta_0^2\mu(D^2)
                 +(\kappa\beta_0^2/s)\mu(|\nabla D|^2),
 \end{aligned}\tag{PC42}
\]
where \(\mathcal Q_4=\mu(\mathcal S^2)\) and
\(\mathcal K_1=\mu(|\nabla\mathcal S|^2)\).
Inserting (PC34) at the exact path proves
\[
 \boxed{\displaystyle
 \mathscr E_j(s_j)\ge
 4\beta_{0,j}^2[\mathcal Q_{4,j}-j\mathcal K_{1,j}]
 -\beta_{0,j}^2\mu_j(D_j^2)
 +{j\beta_{0,j}^2d_{*,j}^2\over8}\,
       c_{\rm cap}\left(1+{120000j^2\over\kappa_*^2}\right)^{-8}.}
 \tag{PC43}
\]
The proved positive term is asymptotic to
\[
 {625\pi^6\over16\kappa_*^2}c_{\rm cap}K_k\,j^{-61}.
 \tag{PC44}
\]
This is a polynomial improvement of the exponential term in JP44.
The actual values of the full four-loop and gradient expectations
in (PC42) remain unknown on the joint path. A lower bound for the
negative even-mass term cannot be substituted as a lower bound for
its negative contribution. Nor do separate lower bounds for total
odd mass and energy determine their difference. Accordingly this
calculation does not settle the sign of \(\mathscr E_j(s_j)\) or
the raw spectral mass inside \((0,s_j]\).

\subsection*{9. A stronger receiving chord inside the same original state}

The coefficient \(w_1(0)\) in (PC29) is small. The original phase
already contains a larger coefficient that supports the same exact
conditional calculation. Nothing in the raw function needs to change.
For any original direction-two bulk chord
\[
 v=(i,k,l;2),\qquad 1\le i\le m-1,\quad
 0\le k<m,\quad0\le l\le m,
\]
let its right-wall partner be \(e_\partial(v)=(m,k,l;2)\).
Its unique reflected target source is \((m-i,k,l;2)\).
Put \(K=Z_v\), \(H_\partial=Z_{e_\partial(v)}\), and \(\mathcal T=A_{k,l}\)
from (PC31). The ordered endpoint products satisfy, exactly,
\[
 A_{k+1,l}=A_{k,l}H_\partial,\qquad
 Z^{\mathcal R}_{(m-i,k,l;2)}
       =\mathcal T K H_\partial^{-1}\mathcal T^{-1}.
 \tag{PC45}
\]
The transport \(\mathcal T\) is independent of both \(K\) and
\(H_\partial\): its direction-two product ends at index \(k-1\), and
its direction-three factors are different links. The bulk \(K\)
occurs in no boundary transport. Its original row coefficients
are \(a_v,b_v\), and its reflected target source coefficients are
\(-a_v,b_v\), since \(v_1(m-i)=-v_1(i)\).

Conjugation is an orthogonal action on the colour vector and preserves
the scalar component:
\[
 q(\mathcal T K H_\partial^{-1}\mathcal T^{-1})
   =\operatorname{Ad}_{\mathcal T}q(KH_\partial^{-1}),\qquad
 c(\mathcal T K H_\partial^{-1}\mathcal T^{-1})=c(KH_\partial^{-1}).
 \tag{PC46}
\]
This follows directly from
\(\mathcal TT_\alpha\mathcal T^{-1}
=\sum_\beta(\operatorname{Ad}_{\mathcal T})_{\beta\alpha}T_\beta\);
the trace inner product
\(-2\operatorname{tr}(T_\alpha T_\beta)=\delta_{\alpha\beta}\)
proves orthogonality. In particular the complete quadratic terms
of \(\mathcal S,\mathcal S_{\mathcal R}\) in \(K\) are
\(a_vb_v|q(K)|^2\) and \(-a_vb_v|q(KH_\partial^{-1})|^2\).
Their full remainders are the two expansions (PC32), with the
remaining coefficient sums and the displayed adjoint action retained.
Every remainder is affine-linear in all four quaternion coordinates
of \(K\).

Under the original left variation
\(H_\partial(t)=\exp(tT_\alpha)H_\partial\), the inverse factor remains
\(KH_\partial^{-1}\exp(-tT_\alpha)\). Equation (PC46) and independence
of \(\mathcal T\) from \(H_\partial\) show that the quadratic part of
\(X_{e_\partial(v),\alpha}D\) is exactly
\(2a_vb_vc(KH_\partial^{-1})q_\alpha(KH_\partial^{-1})\).
The complete differentiated remainder remains affine. Thus every
projection, Casimir and conditional estimate in (PC33)–(PC36)
holds with \(d_*^2\) replaced by
\[
 d_v^2=16a_v^2b_v^2,\qquad
 B_K=4\xi[2+\mathbf1_{l>0}+\mathbf1_{l<m}],\qquad
 B_H=4\xi[1+\mathbf1_{l>0}+\mathbf1_{l<m}].
 \tag{PC47}
\]
These are the actual original incident-face counts. The exact
conjugation map proves the generalization; no reflected or original
word has been replaced.

Now choose
\[
 v^\circ=(1,L-1,0;2),\qquad
 e_\partial^\circ=(m,L-1,0;2),\qquad m=2L,\quad n=m+1.
\]
Since
\(\sin(\pi L/n)=\cos h\), the complete coefficients are
\[
 a^\circ={\sqrt2\over n^{3/2}}\cos(3h)\cos h,\qquad
 b^\circ=-{\sqrt2\over n^{3/2}}\cos^2h,\qquad
 a^\circ b^\circ=-{2\over n^3}\cos(3h)\cos^3h,
 \qquad
 d^\circ=-4a^\circ b^\circ
       ={8\over n^3}\cos(3h)\cos^3h>0 .
 \tag{PC48}
\]
The two selected links have face counts three and two. The exact coefficient improvement over (PC29) is
\(d^\circ/d_*=1/(4\sin^2h)\), obtained from
\(\sin(2h)=2\sin h\cos h\). Thus the squared coefficient gain
is exactly \(1/(16\sin^4h)\), with no change in the original state.
This coefficient is maximal
in absolute value among the entire receiving family (PC45).
Indeed
\[
 |a_vb_v|={2\over n^3}
 \left|\cos{\pi(i+1/2)\over n}
             \cos{\pi(l+1/2)\over n}\right|
             \sin^2{\pi(k+1)\over n}.
\]
Over \(1\le i\le m-1\), the first absolute cosine is at most
\(\cos3h\), attained at \(i=1,m-1\).
Over \(0\le l\le m\), the second is at most \(\cos h\), attained
at \(l=0,m\). Since \(n=2L+1\), the sine is at most \(\cos h\),
attained at \(k=L-1,L\). Their product is (PC48). These same
choices \(l=0,m\) also minimize both face counts in (PC47).
This is an exact optimization within the stated receiving family,
not a claim about every possible proof or every original observable.

Applying the proven estimates to these two existing chords gives the
stronger actual-vacuum bounds
\[
 \boxed{\begin{gathered}
 \mu(|\nabla D|^2)\ge{(d^\circ)^2\over8}\eta_2(12\xi),\\
 G\ge{\beta_0^2(d^\circ)^2\over12}\eta_2(12\xi),\qquad
 C_F\ge{\kappa\beta_0^2(d^\circ)^2\over6}\eta_2(12\xi),\\
 \mu(D^2)\ge{(d^\circ)^2\over8}
                         \eta_2(12\xi)\eta_q(8\xi).
 \end{gathered}}\tag{PC49}
\]
All earlier bounds with \(d_*\) remain valid. This strengthening
selects a receiving conditional coordinate already present in the
unchanged full \(F,D,\psi\).

On the exact path (PC37),
\(j^{12}d^\circ_j\to1\). Hence the two odd bounds in (PC49) are
respectively asymptotic to
\[
 {1250\pi^2\over3\kappa_*^2}c_{\rm cap}K_k\,j^{-46},
 \qquad
 {2500\pi^2\over3\kappa_*}c_{\rm cap}K_k\,j^{-46},
 \tag{PC50}
\]
and the even-mass bound is asymptotic to
\[
 {1\over8}c_{\rm cap}c_qK_kK_b\,j^{-44}.
 \tag{PC51}
\]
These remain asymptotics of the explicit bounds, with no assertion
that the actual vacuum values have those asymptotics.
Finally the original endpoint receives
\[
 \boxed{\begin{aligned}
 \mathscr E_j(s_j)\ge&
 4\beta_{0,j}^2[\mathcal Q_{4,j}-j\mathcal K_{1,j}]
 -\beta_{0,j}^2\mu_j(D_j^2)\\
 &+{j\beta_{0,j}^2(d^\circ_j)^2\over8}\,
       c_{\rm cap}\left(1+{120000j^2\over\kappa_*^2}\right)^{-8}.
 \end{aligned}}\tag{PC52}
\]
The last, strictly positive term is asymptotic to
\((625\pi^2/\kappa_*^2)c_{\rm cap}K_kj^{-45}\).
This strengthens (PC43) by sixteen powers of \(j\), while preserving
every original state, coupling, physical link, word orientation,
boundary contribution, and remainder. The complete cancellation in
(PC42), and therefore the low-window spectral mass, remain unresolved.


\begin{figure}[htbp]\centering\includegraphics[width=\linewidth,height=.72\textheight,keepaspectratio]{POLYNOMIAL_CONDITIONAL_BOUNDS.png}\caption{Exact score-bound sharpness family and the original receiving-link coefficients. The left panel is numerical quadrature of the explicitly proved family PC20–PC22; the right panel retains the original coefficient row in PC45–PC52.}\end{figure}
\clearpage
\section*{All real-source inverses of the full original union-labelled Jacobian}

11 October 2026. This note retains the entire original labelled space, including its assembly relation kernel and labels that strictly contain their active spin support. It proves a full labelled inverse by a finite Boolean-lattice transformation. The completed \href{https://github.com/KokunoYumeto/yang-mills-interacting-workbench/blob/main/yang-mills/continuations/20261011-central-signs-shrinking-window/union_label_global/../GLOBAL_PHYSICAL_RESPONSE.md}{physical response note, GPR1–GPR55} is a stable input and is not changed. The original labelled definitions are \href{https://github.com/KokunoYumeto/yang-mills-interacting-workbench/blob/fa79faff2cd697e16cfa3a7953f36810c22c8922/yang-mills/continuations/20260915-gauge-native-band/RESEARCH_NOTE.md}{G1–G13}. The finite inclusion–exclusion calculation is proved here; no novelty claim is made for Boolean Möbius inversion.

\subsection*{1. Every original edge, face, gauge constraint and supported space}

Fix \(L\ge2\), the original vertices \(V_L=\{-L,\ldots,L\}^3\), all contained positive coordinate edges \(E=E_L\), and all contained oriented elementary faces \(\mathcal P=\mathcal P_L\). Set
\[
 N=|E|=3(2L)(2L+1)^2,\quad M=|\mathcal P|=3(2L)^2(2L+1),\quad
 Q_L=SU(2)^E.
 \tag{UL1}
\]
Product Haar measure \(dU\) is a probability measure. Keep the original definitions
\[
\begin{aligned}
 W_{(n;i,j)}(U)
 &=\operatorname{Tr}[U_i(n)U_j(n+e_i)U_i(n+e_j)^{-1}U_j(n)^{-1}],\\
 T_\alpha&=-i\sigma_\alpha/2,\qquad
 X_{e,\alpha}f=\left.\frac d{dt}f(\ldots,e^{tT_\alpha}U_e,\ldots)\right|_{t=0},\\
 K&=-\sum_{e\in E,\alpha=1}^3X_{e,\alpha}^2,\qquad
 \Gamma(f,h)=\sum_{e\in E,\alpha=1}^3(X_{e,\alpha}f)(X_{e,\alpha}h),\\
 H(x)&=\kappa K+\kappa\sum_{p\in\mathcal P}x_p(2-W_p),\qquad
 \kappa>0,\quad x\in\mathbb R^{\mathcal P},\\
 P_Hf&=\int f\,dU,\qquad Q_H=I-P_H.
\end{aligned}\tag{UL2}
\]
Physical functions are invariant under every original vertex transformation \(U_e\mapsto q_sU_eq_t^{-1}\), including transformations at boundary vertices. Denote their orthogonal Haar projection by \(\Pi\).

For \(A\subseteq E\), let \(\mathsf P_A\) integrate precisely the links outside \(A\). It is a Haar-orthogonal projection, commutes with \(K\), with \(P_H\), and with every vertex gauge action. The last assertion follows by changing the integrated link variables by their left and right vertex factors; their Haar measure is preserved. It acts on Fourier blocks by keeping exactly those with \(j_e=0\) for \(e\notin A\).

The full original product-spin convention is
\[
 c(j)=\sum_{e\in E}j_e(j_e+1),\quad
 \pi_j(U)=\bigotimes_{e\in E}\pi_{j_e}(U_e),\quad
 f_j(U)=\operatorname{Tr}(A_j\pi_j(U)),\quad
 A_j=d_j\int f(U)\pi_j(U)^*dU.
 \tag{UL3}
\]
Here \(d_j=\prod_e(2j_e+1)\) is part of the Fourier coefficient convention. Let
\[
 Y_1(A)=
 \left\{f:\Pi f=f,\ P_Hf=0,\ \mathsf P_Af=f,\
            \|f\|_{Y_1}:=\sum_{j\ne0}c(j)\|A_j\|_1<\infty\right\}.
 \tag{UL4}
\]
This is a closed subspace of the physical Fourier Banach space \(Y_1(E)\). In particular \(Y_1(\varnothing)=\{0\}\). For a real Sobolev index \(s\), also write
\[
 H^s_{\mathrm{ph},0}(A)
 =\{f\in H^s(Q_L):\Pi f=f,\ P_Hf=0,\ \mathsf P_Af=f\},
 \qquad
 \|f\|_{H^s}=\|(I+K)^{s/2}f\|_2.
 \tag{UL5}
\]
The mean for negative \(s\) is pairing with the smooth function \(1\). These definitions keep the full \(K\) and all original gauge constraints. A spin outside \(A\) is the original zero spin, with its original Casimir contribution zero.

Every nonconstant physical block has \(c(j)\ge3\): at a vertex, a single incident nontrivial irreducible representation has no invariant vector; a nonempty active finite graph therefore contains a cycle; the original cubic graph has no cycle shorter than four; and each nonzero link spin costs at least \(3/4\). A fundamental face trace attains \(3\). In particular \(K^{-1}Q_H\) preserves the spaces with support \(A\). The estimate
\[
 \|f\|_{H^2}\le \tfrac43\|f\|_{Y_1}
 \tag{UL6}
\]
follows from \((1+c)/c\le4/3\) and the trace-norm bound on each represented function. Absolute Fourier convergence also gives \(f\in C^2\): two original derivatives of a block have norm at most \(j_ej_f\|A_j\|_1\le c(j)\|A_j\|_1\), including repeated derivatives; the corresponding zeroth and first derivative sums converge as well.

The full original labelled Banach space is
\[
 \mathfrak B
 =\prod_{\varnothing\ne S\subseteq E}Y_1(S),\qquad
 \|F\|_{\mathrm{loc}}
 =\max_{e\in E}\sum_{S\ni e}\|F_S\|_{Y_1}.
 \tag{UL7}
\]
There are finitely many label positions, each of which is an infinite-dimensional function space when it is nonzero. The product is therefore complete. Every nonempty edge label is retained. The space is not restricted to connected labels. In particular an active spin support strictly contained in \(S\) remains in that same \(S\) component.

\subsection*{2. The bounded cumulative and Möbius transformations}

Set \(F_\varnothing=0\). Define, for every \(A,S\subseteq E\),
\[
 (\zeta F)_A=\sum_{S\subseteq A}F_S,\qquad
 (\mathfrak mG)_S=\sum_{A\subseteq S}(-1)^{|S\setminus A|}G_A,
 \quad G_\varnothing=0.
 \tag{UL8}
\]
These maps have the same supported domains as (UL7): every summand of \((\zeta F)_A\) depends only on links in \(A\), and every summand of \((\mathfrak mG)_S\) depends only on links in \(S\). Both preserve physical invariance and the original Haar mean zero.

For fixed \(T\subseteq S\), the coefficient in \(\mathfrak m\zeta\) is
\[
 \sum_{A:T\subseteq A\subseteq S}(-1)^{|S\setminus A|}
 =(1-1)^{|S\setminus T|}
 =\mathbf1_{\{T=S\}}.
 \tag{UL9}
\]
The same summation with the lower intermediate index gives \(\zeta\mathfrak m=I\). This proves that (UL8) is an actual bounded change of coordinates on the full labelled space, once its norm is estimated; it is not a quotient.

For a fixed anchor \(e\), put \(b_S=\|F_S\|_{Y_1}\). The full finite counting formula is
\[
 \sum_{A\ni e}\|(\zeta F)_A\|_{Y_1}
 \le\sum_{\varnothing\ne S\subseteq E}
        2^{N-|S\cup\{e\}|}b_S.
 \tag{UL10}
\]
Each original \(F_S\) is counted once for each superset of \(S\cup\{e\}\). The same bound holds for \(\mathfrak m\), since its coefficient at each contained pair has absolute value one. Define
\[
 C_N=(N+1)2^{N-2}.
 \tag{UL11}
\]
To prove the claimed bound with this constant, assign weight \(2^{N-1}\) to the singleton anchor \(\{e\}\) and weight \(2^{N-2}\) to each other singleton. For every nonempty \(S\),
\[
 2^{N-|S\cup\{e\}|}
 \le 2^{N-1}\mathbf1_{\{e\in S\}}
       +2^{N-2}\sum_{\substack{f\in S\\f\ne e}}1.
\]
For \(e\in S\) the first term already bounds the left side; for \(e\notin S\) any one of the terms bounds it. Multiplication by \(b_S\), summation, and the original anchor norm give
\[
 \boxed{\|\zeta F\|_{\mathrm{loc}}\le C_N\|F\|_{\mathrm{loc}},\qquad
        \|\mathfrak mG\|_{\mathrm{loc}}\le C_N\|G\|_{\mathrm{loc}}.}
 \tag{UL12}
\]
No claim of sharpness is needed; some singleton supported spaces are in fact zero because of the original gauge constraints. Formula (UL10) retains the exact counting before the displayed bound. The identical estimates hold when each coordinate carries the norm in (UL5), or for a real scalar family with \(\max_e\sum_{S\ni e}|c_S|\). Every constant here is finite at the fixed original volume, and its dependence on \(N\) is explicit.

Keep the original union-label bilinear map
\[
 \mathcal B(F,G)_S
 =K^{-1}Q_H\sum_{\substack{S_1,S_2\ne\varnothing\\S_1\cup S_2=S}}
                       \Gamma(F_{S_1},G_{S_2}).
 \tag{UL13}
\]
The full original estimate is \(\|\mathcal B(F,G)\|_{\mathrm{loc}}\le(2/3)\|F\|_{\mathrm{loc}}\|G\|_{\mathrm{loc}}\), as proved in original G11–G13. In particular all terms are well-defined in (UL7).
The function \(\Gamma(F_{S_1},G_{S_2})\) depends only on \(S_1\cup S_2\). It is physical because
\(2\Gamma(f,h)=fKh+hKf-K(fh)\) and every term on the right commutes with the original gauge action. Both \(Q_H\) and \(K^{-1}Q_H\) preserve that support and those constraints. Thus the full union label in (UL13) is also its exact allowed target space. The exact Boolean identity is
\[
 \boxed{(\zeta\mathcal B(F,G))_A
     =K^{-1}Q_H\Gamma((\zeta F)_A,(\zeta G)_A).}
 \tag{UL14}
\]
Indeed \(S_1\cup S_2\subseteq A\) is equivalent to both \(S_1\subseteq A\) and \(S_2\subseteq A\). Reorder the finite label sums in (UL13); the bilinearity of \(\Gamma\) gives (UL14). Fourier completion is legitimate by the original bilinear bound and (UL6). Every intermediate \(Q_H\), the inverse Casimir and the two ordered inputs are present in this identity.

\subsection*{3. Every subset vacuum with the full original Hamiltonian}

For every original edge subset \(A\subseteq E\), define the source restriction
\[
 (r_Ax)_p=\mathbf1_{\{\partial p\subseteq A\}}x_p,\qquad
 H_A(x)=H(r_Ax)
       =\kappa K+\kappa\sum_{p\in\mathcal P}
                \mathbf1_{\{\partial p\subseteq A\}}x_p(2-W_p).
 \tag{UL15}
\]
Thus the kinetic operator is the same full \(K\). Each source term, including its scalar \(2\kappa x_p\), is either retained with its displayed indicator or has source zero. The compact product, measure and gauge group are unchanged.

For completeness the real smooth bounded potential in (UL15) has a smooth strictly positive unit ground vector \(\psi_A\) and a simple ground eigenvalue \(E_A\). Minimize the compact-resolvent Rayleigh form, take the modulus, apply the elliptic equation and the maximum principle, and use the identity
\[
 \langle\psi_Ah,(H_A-E_A)\psi_Ah\rangle
 =\kappa\int\psi_A^2\Gamma(\bar h,h)dU
 \tag{UL16}
\]
to prove uniqueness: another ground vector divided by \(\psi_A\) has zero derivatives, hence is constant. These arguments work for all finite real \(x\), including mixed signs. The vertex transformations preserve positivity and norm and commute with \(H_A\), so \(\psi_A\) is physical.

Every arbitrary left translation of an original link outside \(A\) also commutes with \(H_A\). It preserves the positive unit ground vector by the same uniqueness. Therefore
\[
 \mathsf P_A\psi_A=\psi_A,\qquad
 \psi_A(U)=\psi_A(U_A)\prod_{e\notin A}1.
 \tag{UL17}
\]
This does not change \(K\): its outside terms act on the explicitly displayed constant factors and contribute zero. Define
\[
 \rho_A=\psi_A^2,\quad d\mu_A=\rho_AdU,\quad
 v_A=\log\psi_A-P_H\log\psi_A,\quad
 c_A=P_H\log\psi_A=-\tfrac12\log\int e^{2v_A}dU.
 \tag{UL18}
\]
Then \(v_A\in C^\infty\cap Y_1(A)\). The membership in \(Y_1\) follows from the original Peter–Weyl estimate (GPR17), applied with any sufficiently high Sobolev order. These functions depend real analytically on \(x\) in every Sobolev norm and in \(Y_1(A)\): the isolated simple ground projection has a local resolvent Neumann expansion, its positive unit phase is uniquely fixed, the elliptic equation gives every Sobolev order, and the logarithm is analytic on a sufficiently small positive neighbourhood. These local branches agree everywhere on the real source space by uniqueness. At \(A=\varnothing\), the full free vacuum is \(1\), so \(E_\varnothing=v_\varnothing=c_\varnothing=0\).

Dividing the full equation by \(e^{v_A+c_A}\) and taking its Haar mean gives
\[
\begin{aligned}
 Kv_A&=\sum_{\partial p\subseteq A}x_pW_p
        +\Gamma(v_A,v_A)-C_A,\\
 C_A&=\int\Gamma(v_A,v_A)dU,\qquad
 E_A=2\kappa\sum_{\partial p\subseteq A}x_p-\kappa C_A,\\
 v_A&=\tfrac13\sum_{\partial p\subseteq A}x_pW_p
             +K^{-1}Q_H\Gamma(v_A,v_A).
\end{aligned}\tag{UL19}
\]
The last formula uses the original equality \(KW_p=3W_p\). Both the potential scalar and the removed Haar scalar are retained.

We will need the complete physical inverse on the same supported space:
\[
 \mathscr L_A=I-2K^{-1}Q_H\Gamma(v_A,\cdot).
 \tag{UL20}
\]
It is a bounded bijection on every \(H^s_{\mathrm{ph},0}(A)\) and on \(Y_1(A)\). Here is the supporting argument, including the support issue. On the full physical Sobolev space, the perturbation of the identity gains one derivative and is compact. A kernel element bootstraps to a smooth solution of
\(\mathcal L_Ah=b\), where \(\mathcal L_A=K-2\Gamma(v_A,\cdot)\) and \(b\) is constant. Its \(\mu_A\) mean forces \(b=0\), and (UL16) makes \(h\) constant; its Haar mean is zero. The index-zero compact perturbation is therefore a bounded bijection. In the Fourier norm, the exact GPR18 estimate maps its perturbation \(Y_1\to Y_{3/2}\); the latter embeds compactly into \(Y_1\) by a finite Casimir cutoff and its \(R^{-1/2}\) tail. Its kernel embeds in the already trivial \(H^2\) kernel. This proves the \(Y_1\) assertion.

The projection \(\mathsf P_A\) commutes with \(H_A\) and the rank-one projection onto \(\psi_A\), and hence with the vacuum-complement inverse. It also commutes with multiplication by \(\psi_A^{\pm1}\), with \(K\), and with \(Q_H\). Thus the inverse preserves precisely the functions supported on \(A\). In full detail, for \(f\in Y_1(A)\), or \(f\in H^s_{\mathrm{ph},0}(A)\),
\[
 \boxed{\mathscr L_A^{-1}f
 =Q_H\kappa\psi_A^{-1}(H_A-E_A)^\dagger\psi_A
                     [Kf-\mu_A(Kf)].}
 \tag{UL21}
\]
The dagger is the actual inverse on the Haar-orthogonal complement of \(\psi_A\), extended by zero on that vector. Its Sobolev extension has domain \(H^{s-2}\) and range \(H^s\). For negative indices the scalar is the exact pairing \(\mu_A(Kf)=\langle f,K\rho_A\rangle\). The bracket multiplied by \(\psi_A\) is orthogonal to \(\psi_A\), the factor \(\kappa\) is retained, and the final \(Q_H\) imposes the required Haar mean. Multiplication by \(\psi_A\) intertwines \(\mathcal L_A\) with \((H_A-E_A)/\kappa\); hence (UL21) solves (UL20), and the previous uniqueness proves it is the inverse. The same construction proves real analytic dependence of these inverses in their fixed supported operator norms.

\subsection*{4. The unique real full labelled vacuum and every scalar}

Define the original source insertion and the new exact labelled vacuum by
\[
 T(x)_S=\tfrac13\sum_{\substack{p\in\mathcal P\\\partial p=S}}x_pW_p,
 \qquad
 V(x)_S=(\mathfrak m v)_S
       =\sum_{A\subseteq S}(-1)^{|S\setminus A|}v_A(x).
 \tag{UL22}
\]
All labels and functions in this formula have the original meaning. A summand \(v_A\) placed in the larger label \(S\) retains the explicit distinction between that label and its active spin support. There are only finitely many summands; (UL12) and the established \(Y_1(A)\) regularity prove \(V\in\mathfrak B\), with real analytic dependence on all finite real sources.

Equations (UL8)–(UL9) give
\[
 (\zeta V)_A=v_A,\qquad
 (\zeta T)_A=\tfrac13\sum_{\partial p\subseteq A}x_pW_p.
 \tag{UL23}
\]
Apply (UL14) to (UL19), and then the inverse \(\mathfrak m\). The result is the full original labelled nonlinear equation
\[
 \boxed{V(x)=T(x)+\mathcal B(V(x),V(x))}
 \quad\text{in }\mathfrak B,\quad x\in\mathbb R^{\mathcal P}.
