Its positive edges and faces have counts \(N=3m(m+1)^2\), \(M=3m^2(m+1)\). The link manifold is \(Q_L=SU(2)^{E_L}\) with product Haar probability. At every vertex, including the boundary, impose the original gauge action \(U_{(s,t)}\mapsto q_sU_{(s,t)}q_t^{-1}\). Put
\[
 \begin{gathered}
 T_\alpha=-i\sigma_\alpha/2,\quad
 \langle v,w\rangle_{\mathfrak{su}(2)}=-2\operatorname{tr}(vw),\quad
 v=\sum_{\alpha=1}^3v_\alpha T_\alpha,\quad |v|^2=\sum_\alpha v_\alpha^2,\\
 X_{e,\alpha}f(U)=
  \left.\frac d{dh}f(\ldots,e^{hT_\alpha}U_e,\ldots)\right|_{h=0},
 \quad K=-\sum_{e,\alpha}X_{e,\alpha}^2,\quad
 \Delta=-K .
 \end{gathered}\tag{ESE1}
\]
These three fields are orthonormal for the radius-two sphere metric, whose diameter is \(2\pi\). The complete face word, trace, potential and Hamiltonian are
\[
 \begin{gathered}
 W_{(z;i,j)}
 =\operatorname{tr}\!\left[
 U_i(z)U_j(z+e_i)U_i(z+e_j)^{-1}U_j(z)^{-1}\right],\\
 \kappa=\frac{2g^2}{a}>0,\quad b_{\rm mag}=\frac1{2g^2a},\quad
 \xi=\frac{b_{\rm mag}}{\kappa}=\frac1{4g^4},\quad
 V(U)=\kappa\xi\sum_{p}(2-W_p(U)),\quad H=\kappa K+V,\\
 H\psi=E_0\psi,\qquad \psi>0,\quad\int_{Q_L}\psi^2dU=1,\quad
 \rho=\psi^2,\quad u=\log\psi.
 \end{gathered}\tag{ESE2}
\]
The normalized positive vacuum is unique, smooth, and physical, by positivity of the heat kernel and elliptic regularity on the compact connected link manifold. Every scalar \(2\kappa\xi M\), \(E_0\), and the unit normalization remains part of (ESE2).

Fix an original edge \(e\). Its actual number of incident faces is \(r_e\in\{2,3,4\}\), including boundary incidences, and write
\[
 \beta=r_e\xi,\qquad
 V=V_e+V_{\bar e},\quad
 V_e=\kappa\xi\sum_{p:e\in\partial p}(2-W_p),\quad
 V_{\bar e}=\kappa\xi\sum_{p:e\notin\partial p}(2-W_p).
 \tag{ESE3}
\]
Here \(V_{\bar e}\) is independent of \(U_e\); \(V_e\) retains all outside links in its incident face words. In particular
\[
 0\le V_e\le4\kappa\beta,\qquad
 |\nabla_eV|=|\nabla_eV_e|\le\kappa\beta.
 \tag{ESE4}
\]
For the gradient constant, write an incident face as \(A U_e B\) or \(A U_e^{-1}B\). Bi-invariance and inversion reduce its trace gradient to that of \(2c(U)\), where \(c(U)=\operatorname{tr}U/2\); its squared norm on the radius-two sphere is \(1-c(U)^2\le1\). Summing every incident face proves (ESE4).

For each original edge let \(B^f=(B^{f,1},B^{f,2},B^{f,3})\) be independent standard Euclidean Brownian motions. Brownian motion on \(Q_L\) with generator \(\kappa\Delta\), starting from the arbitrary full configuration \(x\), satisfies the matrix equations
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 dX_s^f&=\sqrt{2\kappa}\sum_\alpha T_\alpha X_s^f\circ dB_s^{f,\alpha},\\
 dX_s^f&=\sqrt{2\kappa}\sum_\alpha T_\alpha X_s^f\,dB_s^{f,\alpha}
                     -\frac{3\kappa}{4}X_s^f\,ds,\qquad X_0^f=x_f.
 \end{aligned}\tag{ESE5}
\]
The second line follows from \(\sum_\alpha T_\alpha^2=-3I/4\); it is the exact Itô correction in the original metric. The matrix equation has a unique global strong solution: its real matrix coefficients are linear and therefore globally Lipschitz, while the Stratonovich product rule preserves \(X^*X=I\) and determinant one. Thus it remains in \(SU(2)\), and its generator on smooth functions is \(\kappa\sum_\alpha X_{f,\alpha}^2\).

The full vacuum equation gives the exact positive martingale and normalization
\[
 Z_s^{\rm vac}
  =\frac{e^{E_0s-\int_0^s V(X_r)\,dr}\psi(X_s)}{\psi(x)},
 \qquad \mathbb E_x Z_t^{\rm vac}=1,\qquad
 d\mathbb Q_x=Z_t^{\rm vac}\,d\mathbb P_x .
 \tag{ESE6}
\]
Indeed Itô differentiation of \(\psi(X_s)\) gives drift
\(\kappa\Delta\psi=(V-E_0)\psi\), cancelling the displayed exponential drift. The stochastic integrand is bounded on every finite time interval, since the configuration space is compact and \(V,\psi,\nabla\psi\) are bounded there. Hence the local martingale is a true martingale, with initial value one. Equation (ESE6) proves exactly the Feynman–Kac identity needed below, including its terminal factor and energy scalar.

\subsection*{2. The complete endpoint-preserving path transformation}

Fix a finite time \(t>0\), a unit \(v\in\mathfrak{su}(2)\), and a real parameter \(\epsilon\). Keep every outside Brownian motion and every outside link path unchanged. For the selected edge put
\[
 a_s=1-\frac{s}{t},\quad
 g_s^\epsilon=\exp(\epsilon a_s v),\quad
 Y_s^{e,\epsilon}=g_s^\epsilon X_s^e,\quad
 Y_s^{f,\epsilon}=X_s^f\ (f\ne e).
 \tag{ESE7}
\]
Thus \(Y_0^{e,\epsilon}=e^{\epsilon v}x_e\) and
\(Y_t^{e,\epsilon}=X_t^e\) exactly. This is a transformation of the whole original edge path with a fixed terminal point, not conditioning on a Brownian bridge.

Write the deterministic adjoint matrix in the original orthonormal Lie basis as
\[
 g_s^\epsilon T_\alpha(g_s^\epsilon)^{-1}
       =\sum_\gamma R_s^\epsilon{}_{\gamma\alpha}T_\gamma,\qquad
 R_s^\epsilon\in SO(3),\quad
 R_s^\epsilon v=v,\quad (R_s^\epsilon)^{\mathsf T}v=v.
 \tag{ESE8}
\]
Conjugation preserves \(-2\operatorname{tr}(vw)\), proving orthogonality; continuity from the identity gives determinant one. Since \(g_s^\epsilon\) is an exponential of \(v\), it fixes \(v\). Define the rotated noise
\[
 B_s^{e,\epsilon}=\int_0^sR_r^\epsilon\,dB_r^e.
 \tag{ESE9}
\]
This is a standard three-dimensional Brownian motion, independent of all outside Brownian motions. To verify the law directly, deterministic stochastic integrals against Brownian motion are Gaussian; for disjoint time intervals their underlying increments are independent, their mean is zero, and
\[
 d\langle B^{e,\epsilon,\alpha},B^{e,\epsilon,\gamma}\rangle_s
       =\delta_{\alpha\gamma}\,ds,\quad
 d\langle B^{e,\epsilon,\alpha},B^{f,\gamma}\rangle_s=0\ (f\ne e),
 \qquad v\cdot B_s^{e,\epsilon}=v\cdot B_s^e .
 \tag{ESE10}
\]
The final equality follows by \(v^{\mathsf T}R_r^\epsilon=v^{\mathsf T}\) inside the integral. In particular no dimension factor multiplies the variance of this unit directional noise.
The inverse deterministic stochastic integral is \(B_s^e=\int_0^s(R_r^\epsilon)^{\mathsf T}dB_r^{e,\epsilon}\), so the two noises generate the same completed filtration. This also makes the subsequent change-of-measure statement a Brownian statement in the full filtration used by the original matrix equation.

The deterministic derivative is
\((g_s^\epsilon)'(g_s^\epsilon)^{-1}=-\epsilon v/t\).
Applying Itô's product rule to (ESE5), with this deterministic matrix, therefore gives
\[
 dY_s^{e,\epsilon}
 =\sqrt{2\kappa}\sum_\alpha T_\alpha Y_s^{e,\epsilon}
                                  \,dB_s^{e,\epsilon,\alpha}
   -\frac{3\kappa}{4}Y_s^{e,\epsilon}\,ds
   -\frac{\epsilon}{t}vY_s^{e,\epsilon}\,ds.
 \tag{ESE11}
\]
There is no extra derivative of \(R_s^\epsilon\) in this equation: (ESE9) is a deterministic orthogonal rotation inside a stochastic integral, and (ESE11) already includes the entire derivative of \(g_s^\epsilon\). This records the adjoint rotation, curvature correction and drift sign separately.

The exact likelihood which removes the final drift is
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 \Lambda_s^\epsilon
 &=\exp\left[
   \frac{\epsilon}{\sqrt{2\kappa}\,t}\,v\cdot B_s^{e,\epsilon}
                      -\frac{\epsilon^2s}{4\kappa t^2}\right],\\
 \Lambda_t^\epsilon
 &=\exp\left[
   \frac{\epsilon}{\sqrt{2\kappa}\,t}\,v\cdot B_t^e
                      -\frac{\epsilon^2}{4\kappa t}\right],\qquad
 d\mathbb P_\epsilon=\Lambda_t^\epsilon\,d\mathbb P_x .
 \end{aligned}\tag{ESE12}
\]
The stochastic exponent has quadratic variation
\(\epsilon^2s/(2\kappa t^2)\), so its compensator is exactly the negative half shown above. The exponential has expectation one.

Here the required change of measure can be proved by finite-dimensional Gaussian integration. For a partition of \([0,t]\), the increments of \(B^{e,\epsilon}\) are independent centered Gaussians with covariance \(\Delta s\,I_3\). Multiplication of their joint density by
\(\exp[\theta v\cdot\sum\Delta B-\theta^2t/2]\), with
\(\theta=\epsilon/(\sqrt{2\kappa}\,t)\), completes the square in each increment and shifts its mean to \(\theta v\Delta s\), keeping its covariance and independence unchanged. Cylinder sets on all finite partitions generate the continuous-path sigma field. Consequently under \(\mathbb P_\epsilon\)
\[
 \widetilde B_s^{e,\epsilon}
       =B_s^{e,\epsilon}
                    -\frac{\epsilon s}{\sqrt{2\kappa}\,t}v
 \tag{ESE13}
\]
is standard Brownian motion, independent of the unchanged outside Brownian motions. The latter assertion also follows directly because (ESE12) depends only on the selected edge noise, whose original law is independent of the outside noises.

Substituting (ESE13) into (ESE11) adds
\(+(\epsilon/t)vY_s^{e,\epsilon}ds\), cancelling the negative drift exactly. Strong uniqueness of (ESE5) then shows that the full path \(Y^\epsilon\), under \(\mathbb P_\epsilon\), has exactly the original full Brownian law starting at
\(x^\epsilon=(x_f)_{f\ne e},e^{\epsilon v}x_e\).
This proves the required Cameron–Martin/Girsanov formula with its sign and all metric constants; no bounded-drift estimate or suppressed noise rotation substitutes for the exact law.

\subsection*{3. Differentiation with every outside path and terminal factor}

Apply (ESE6) at \(x^\epsilon\), then use the just-proved change of law. Terminal preservation in (ESE7) gives
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 \psi(x^\epsilon)
 &=e^{E_0t}\,
   \mathbb E_x\!\left[
     \Lambda_t^\epsilon
       e^{-\int_0^t V(Y_s^\epsilon)\,ds}\psi(X_t)\right],\\
 \frac{\psi(x^\epsilon)}{\psi(x)}
 &=\mathbb E_{\mathbb Q_x}\!\left[
     \Lambda_t^\epsilon
       e^{-\int_0^t[V(Y_s^\epsilon)-V(X_s)]\,ds}\right].
 \end{aligned}\tag{ESE14}
\]
The original terminal factor \(\psi(X_t)\) appears explicitly in the first line and in the full tilted law of the second. Every outside path is present in \(V(X_s)\), \(V(Y_s^\epsilon)\), and \(\psi(X_t)\). The constant potential \(2\kappa\xi M\) remains in the first line; it cancels in the potential difference of the second because the same full potential is used at both paths. The vacuum energy \(E_0\) cancels only through the exact normalization (ESE6).

The derivative at \(\epsilon=0\) can be passed through the expectation. Indeed (ESE4) bounds the potential difference by
\(\kappa\beta|\epsilon|\int_0^t a_sds=\kappa\beta|\epsilon|t/2\).
Its derivative is bounded by the same constant. The likelihood and its derivative, for \(|\epsilon|\le\epsilon_0\), are bounded by a constant times
\((1+|v\cdot B_t^e|)
\exp[\epsilon_0|v\cdot B_t^e|/(\sqrt{2\kappa}\,t)]\).
This is integrable under the original Gaussian law. The tilt \(Z_t^{\rm vac}\) is bounded above and below by finite positive constants at this fixed box, configuration, and time, because the vacuum and potential are smooth on the compact manifold. The same dominating function is therefore integrable under \(\mathbb Q_x\).

Differentiating (ESE14) now proves the exact directional formula
\[
 \boxed{\displaystyle
 X_{e,v}\log\psi(x)=
 \mathbb E_{\mathbb Q_x}\!\left[
  \frac{v\cdot B_t^e}{\sqrt{2\kappa}\,t}
  -\int_0^t\left(1-\frac{s}{t}\right)
                         X_{e,v}V(X_s)\,ds\right].}
 \tag{ESE15}
\]
Here \(X_{e,v}=\sum_\alpha v_\alpha X_{e,\alpha}\). The potential derivative includes every incident face with its actual inverse orientations; the estimate (ESE4) merely bounds their complete sum. There is no terminal derivative because \(Y_t^\epsilon=X_t\) holds for every \(\epsilon\), before differentiation.

\subsection*{4. Conditional entropy and the exact outside mixture}

Let \(\mathbb P_e\) be the driving Brownian law of the selected edge and \(\mathbb P_{\bar e}\) the joint law of all outside link paths, starting at the fixed original outside configuration. The original law factors as \(\mathbb P_e\otimes\mathbb P_{\bar e}\). For an outside path \(y=(y_s)_{0\le s\le t}\), keep
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 R_y(B^e)
   &=\exp\left[-\int_0^t
                  V_e(X_s^e,y_s)\,ds\right]\psi(X_t^e,y_t),\\
 Z_y&=\mathbb E_{\mathbb P_e}R_y>0,\qquad
 q_y=\frac{R_y}{Z_y},\qquad
 d\mathbb Q_e^y=q_y\,d\mathbb P_e.
 \end{aligned}\tag{ESE16}
\]
All expressions are measurable; Brownian path spaces are standard Borel, and these explicit positive densities give their conditional laws. Compactness bounds \(R_y\) above and below uniformly by positive finite constants at fixed \(t\). In particular every normalization and conditional expectation here is well-defined.

The actual tilted outside law is
\[
 d\mathbb Q_{\bar e}(y)
 =\frac{e^{E_0t}}{\psi(x)}
   \exp\left[-\int_0^tV_{\bar e}(y_s)\,ds\right]
        Z_y\,d\mathbb P_{\bar e}(y),
 \qquad
 d\mathbb Q_x=q_y\,d\mathbb P_e\,d\mathbb Q_{\bar e}(y).
 \tag{ESE17}
\]
The normalization of the first line follows from (ESE6) and Fubini. Equation (ESE17) retains the terminal vacuum through \(Z_y\) and retains all nonincident potentials through \(V_{\bar e}\). It is not the original outside Brownian marginal in general.

Define the actual edge oscillation and maximal score by
\[
 \omega_e=\max_{z\in SU(2)^{E_L\setminus\{e\}}}
       \left[\max_{U_e}\log\psi(U_e,z)
                         -\min_{U_e}\log\psi(U_e,z)\right],
 \quad
 M_e=\max_{x\in Q_L}|\nabla_e\log\psi(x)|.
 \tag{ESE18}
\]
These are finite actual-vacuum numbers, since \(\psi\) is smooth and positive on the compact product. No estimated oscillation is substituted in their definition.

For every fixed outside path \(y\), (ESE4) and the terminal slice of (ESE18) give
\[
 \operatorname{osc}_{B^e}\log R_y
     \le\omega_e+4\kappa\beta t=:D_t,\qquad
 \operatorname{Ent}(\mathbb Q_e^y\mid\mathbb P_e)
       =\mathbb E_{\mathbb P_e}(q_y\log q_y)\le D_t.
 \tag{ESE19}
\]
The first inequality keeps the potential and terminal terms separately: the integral of \(V_e\) ranges within an interval of length \(4\kappa\beta t\), and the logarithm of \(\psi(X_t^e,y_t)\) ranges within an interval of length at most \(\omega_e\). For the second, \(Z_y\) lies between the minimum and maximum of \(R_y\), so \(q_y\le e^{D_t}\). Integrating \(\log q_y\le D_t\) against its probability density proves the bound. Nonnegativity of entropy follows from \(q\log q-q+1\ge0\), integrated against \(\mathbb P_e\).

The full entropy, by contrast, has the exact chain rule
\[
 \operatorname{Ent}(\mathbb Q_x\mid\mathbb P_e\otimes\mathbb P_{\bar e})
 =\operatorname{Ent}(\mathbb Q_{\bar e}\mid\mathbb P_{\bar e})
    +\int\operatorname{Ent}(\mathbb Q_e^y\mid\mathbb P_e)
                                  \,d\mathbb Q_{\bar e}(y).
 \tag{ESE20}
\]
To prove it, take the logarithm of the product density in (ESE17) and integrate first in the edge variable. All logarithms are bounded at fixed \(t\), so Fubini applies directly. The outside term in (ESE20) is retained. No bound by \(D_t\) for the full entropy is used or follows from (ESE19) alone.

Under the original conditional law \(\mathbb P_e\), the scalar \(Z=v\cdot B_t^e\) is Gaussian with mean zero and variance \(t\), for every unit \(v\). For any probability density \(q\) of finite entropy \(I\), Jensen's inequality gives
\[
 \lambda\,\mathbb E_qZ-I
  =\mathbb E_q\log\frac{e^{\lambda Z}}q
  \le\log\mathbb E_{\mathbb P_e}e^{\lambda Z}
  =\frac{\lambda^2t}{2},\qquad
 |\mathbb E_qZ|\le\sqrt{2tI}.
 \tag{ESE21}
\]
The final inequality follows by minimizing \(I/|\lambda|+|\lambda|t/2\) and choosing the sign of \(\lambda\); if \(I=0\), take \(\lambda\to0\). The Gaussian moment generating function follows by completing the square. These steps apply to \(q_y\), which is bounded and strictly positive.

Apply (ESE21) to each entire conditional density (ESE16), then integrate using the actual outside law (ESE17). Equations (ESE19) and (ESE21) imply
\[
 \left|\mathbb E_{\mathbb Q_x}
       \frac{v\cdot B_t^e}{\sqrt{2\kappa}\,t}\right|
 \le\int\sqrt{\frac{\operatorname{Ent}(\mathbb Q_e^y\mid\mathbb P_e)}
                         {\kappa t}}\,d\mathbb Q_{\bar e}(y)
 \le\sqrt{\frac{\omega_e}{\kappa t}+4\beta}.
 \tag{ESE22}
\]
This is the required conditioning-and-mixture step. The global differentiation was already performed in (ESE15); no derivative of an outside marginal has been ignored by conditioning prematurely.

Finally (ESE4), \(\int_0^t(1-s/t)ds=t/2\), and (ESE15) give, for every \(x\), unit \(v\), and \(t>0\),
\[
 \boxed{\displaystyle
 |X_{e,v}\log\psi(x)|
 \le\sqrt{\frac{\omega_e}{\kappa t}+4\beta}
                          +\frac{\kappa\beta t}{2},
 \qquad
 M_e\le\sqrt{\frac{\omega_e}{\tau}+4\beta}+\frac{\tau\beta}{2}
 \quad(\tau=\kappa t>0).}
 \tag{ESE23}
\]
Taking the supremum over unit directions gives the original three-component metric gradient norm, without an added factor \(\sqrt3\).

\subsection*{5. Closing and optimizing the bound without a heat-kernel oscillation claim}

The diameter in the original metric gives the unconditional relation
\[
 \omega_e\le2\pi M_e.
 \tag{ESE24}
\]
For each fixed exterior configuration, integrate the derivative of \(\log\psi\) along a shortest one-link geodesic of length at most \(2\pi\), then maximize over the exterior. This proves (ESE24) directly for the actual vacuum.

If \(M_e=0\), every following score bound is immediate. For \(\beta>0\) and \(M_e>0\), choose the permitted time \(\tau=cM_e/\beta\), with any \(0<c<2\). Substitution of (ESE24) into (ESE23) yields
\[
 M_e\le\sqrt{\beta(2\pi/c+4)}+\frac c2M_e,\qquad
 M_e^2\le\beta\,\Phi(c),\qquad
 \Phi(c)=\frac{2\pi/c+4}{(1-c/2)^2}.
 \tag{ESE25}
\]
The unknown positive \(M_e\) is used only to select a time in an inequality already proved for every positive time. This does not assume the desired estimate or change the Hamiltonian.

In particular \(c=1/2\) gives the completely explicit bounds
\[
 M_e\le\frac83\sqrt{1+\pi}\sqrt{\beta},\qquad
 |\nabla_e\log\rho|
       \le\frac{16}{3}\sqrt{1+\pi}\sqrt{\beta}.
 \tag{ESE26}
\]
The exact optimal constant obtainable from (ESE24)–(ESE25) is also explicit:
\[
 c_*=\frac{\sqrt{9\pi^2+32\pi}-3\pi}{8},\quad
 C_\psi=\sqrt{\Phi(c_*)},\quad C_\rho=2\sqrt{\Phi(c_*)},\qquad
 \boxed{M_e\le C_\psi\sqrt{\beta},\quad
             |\nabla_e\log\rho|\le C_\rho\sqrt{\beta}.}
 \tag{ESE27}
\]
Indeed
\[
 \Phi'(c)=
   \frac{4c^2+3\pi c-2\pi}{c^2(1-c/2)^3}.
 \tag{ESE28}
\]
The numerator is strictly increasing for \(c>0\), is negative at zero and positive at two, and has exactly the positive root \(c_*\) displayed in (ESE27). The denominator is positive on \((0,2)\), and \(\Phi(c)\) tends to infinity at each endpoint. Thus this root is the unique global minimizer on the exact allowed interval.

The former score estimate is retained as well. With \(w_e=|\nabla_eu|^2\), the full vacuum equation and the radius-two Ricci tensor give
\[
 (\Delta+2\nabla u\cdot\nabla)w_e
   =2\sum_f|\nabla_f\nabla_eu|_{\rm HS}^2+w_e
                   +2\kappa^{-1}\langle\nabla_eu,\nabla_eV\rangle.
 \tag{ESE29}
\]
This follows by differentiating
\(\Delta u+|\nabla u|^2=(V-E_0)/\kappa\); commuting the factor-\(e\) derivatives contributes twice its Ricci tensor, namely \(w_e\), and retains every mixed Hessian square. At a maximum, (ESE4) implies \(0\ge w_e-2\beta\sqrt{w_e}\), hence \(M_e\le2\beta\). Combining with (ESE27) proves
\[
 \boxed{\displaystyle
 |\nabla_e\log\rho|
    \le \min\{\,4r_e\xi,\ C_\rho\sqrt{r_e\xi}\,\}.}
 \tag{ESE30}
\]
At \(\beta=0\), the potential is independent of the chosen edge. Left translations of that edge commute with the full Hamiltonian. Uniqueness of the positive unit vacuum makes it invariant under those translations, so \(M_e=\omega_e=0\) and (ESE30) still holds.

The same proof applies, with every original scalar retained, to the all-real face-source Hamiltonian
\[
 H(x)=\kappa K+\kappa\sum_px_p(2-W_p),\qquad
 \beta_e(x)=\sum_{p:e\in\partial p}|x_p|,\qquad
 |\nabla_e\log\rho_x|
       \le\min\{4\beta_e(x),C_\rho\sqrt{\beta_e(x)}\}.
 \tag{ESE31}
\]
To verify the extension, the incident potential need not be nonnegative, but its range has length at most \(4\kappa\beta_e(x)\) and its gradient norm is at most \(\kappa\beta_e(x)\). These are the only two potential bounds used in (ESE14)–(ESE30). The complete real potential is bounded at every finite source, so the martingale and domination arguments are unchanged; all constant \(2\kappa x_p\) terms remain in their original positions. If \(\beta_e(x)=0\), the same translation argument applies.

\subsection*{6. Status of the sharper proposed constant}

The requested numerical substitution is exact:
\[
 \sqrt{\frac{4\pi\sqrt{\beta}}{\,2/\sqrt{\beta}\,}+4\beta}
       +\frac{(2/\sqrt{\beta})\beta}{2}
       =\bigl(1+\sqrt{4+2\pi}\bigr)\sqrt{\beta}.
 \tag{ESE32}
\]
Thus the pathwise derivative and entropy portion of that proposed calculation has all its stated constants. The separate oscillation assertion
\(\omega_e\le4\pi\sqrt{\beta}\) is not proved in this note and is not used in (ESE27), (ESE30), or (ESE31). Its independent verification is needed before the smaller density-score constant \(2+2\sqrt{4+2\pi}\) is recorded as proved. The unconditional square-root improvement above already follows from the actual full vacuum and needs no such assertion.

\subsection*{7. Receiving improvement for the original raw phase, with the unchanged path}

Keep precisely the original \(F=p-r\), all coefficients, rooted words and affine remainders defined in PRE10–PRE16. PRE32–PRE36 prove degree at most two along every original physical edge subgroup, including all tree links. PRE37–PRE40 then prove, directly in any smooth density with edge score bound \(B_e\),
\[
 \mu(|X_{e,\alpha}F|^2)
       \le b_2(B_e)\,\mu(F^2),\qquad
 b_2(B)=\frac{75}{64}(1+r_B^2)^5e^{4B\arctan r_B}
                  \frac{\arctan r_B}{r_B^3},\quad
 r_B=\frac1{2(B+2)},\quad
 \frac{b_2(B)}{B^2}\longrightarrow\frac{75e^2}{16}.
 \tag{ESE33}
\]
The proof uses the exact coefficient Gram functional \(75/8\), integrates along the full original subgroup interval \([-4\arctan r_B,4\arctan r_B]\), and averages every center in the actual vacuum. It therefore applies to (ESE30) with every outside correlation present.

For \(r=2,3,4\) put
\[
 B_r(\xi)=\min\{4r\xi,C_\rho\sqrt{r\xi}\}.
 \tag{ESE34}
\]
The exact edge multiplicities are \(12m\), \(12m(m-1)\), \(3m(m-1)^2\), respectively. Consequently the original raw quantities
\(G=\mu(F^2)\), \(C_F=\kappa\mu(|\nabla F|^2)\) obey the stronger direct estimate
\[
 \boxed{\displaystyle
 \frac{C_F}{G}\le\kappa\,\widetilde{\mathcal B}_L(\xi),\qquad
 \widetilde{\mathcal B}_L(\xi)=
 3\left[
 12m\,b_2(B_2(\xi))
 +12m(m-1)b_2(B_3(\xi))
 +3m(m-1)^2b_2(B_4(\xi))\right].}
 \tag{ESE35}
\]
This uses the unchanged factor three for the three colour fields. It is a bound for the complete raw quotient, not a ratio of separate lower estimates.

For the original simultaneous path
\[
 L_j=j^4,\quad a_j=\frac1{100j},\quad
 g_j^2=\frac{\kappa_*}{200j},\quad \kappa=\kappa_*,\quad
 \xi_j=\frac{10000j^2}{\kappa_*^2},\quad
 s_j=\frac{\kappa_*}{j},\quad m_j=2j^4,\qquad j\ge2,
 \tag{ESE36}
\]
the new upper bound has the evaluated asymptotic constant
\[
 \boxed{\displaystyle
 \lim_{j\to\infty}
 \frac{\kappa_*\widetilde{\mathcal B}_{L_j}(\xi_j)}{j^{14}}
       =\frac{13500000\,e^2 C_\rho^2}{\kappa_*}.}
 \tag{ESE37}
\]
Indeed \(B_r(\xi_j)=C_\rho\sqrt{r\xi_j}\) for all sufficiently large \(j\). In the leading interior term there are \(3m_j(m_j-1)^2\sim24j^{12}\) edges, with \(r=4\), while its score square is
\(40000C_\rho^2j^2/\kappa_*^2\). Multiplying by the three colour directions and the limit \(75e^2/16\) in (ESE33) gives
\(3\cdot24\cdot(75e^2/16)\cdot40000
=13500000e^2\).
The boundary terms have strictly smaller orders and their exact finite expressions remain in (ESE35).

The corresponding raw endpoint estimate is
\[
 G_j-\frac{C_{F,j}}{s_j}
       \ge[1-j\widetilde{\mathcal B}_{L_j}(\xi_j)]G_j,\qquad
 \frac{j\widetilde{\mathcal B}_{L_j}(\xi_j)}{j^{15}}
       \longrightarrow\frac{13500000e^2C_\rho^2}{\kappa_*^2}.
 \tag{ESE38}
\]
Thus the unconditional score strengthens the proved quotient order from PRE's \(j^{16}\) to \(j^{14}\), while the evaluated endpoint coefficient still eventually becomes negative. This does not decide the actual shrinking-window endpoint or prove gaplessness. All original phase and Hamiltonian factors remain intact.


The separate oscillation estimate discussed in ESE32 is now proved in \href{https://github.com/KokunoYumeto/yang-mills-interacting-workbench/blob/main/yang-mills/continuations/20261011-central-signs-shrinking-window/HEAT_RATIO_PROOF.md}{HR1–HR25} and \href{https://github.com/KokunoYumeto/yang-mills-interacting-workbench/blob/main/yang-mills/continuations/20261011-central-signs-shrinking-window/SQUARE_ROOT_LOCAL_SCORE.md}{LS7–LS18}. Together with this exact entropy derivation it gives the smaller constant in LS32. \href{https://github.com/KokunoYumeto/yang-mills-interacting-workbench/blob/main/yang-mills/continuations/20261011-central-signs-shrinking-window/INDEPENDENT_EDGE_ENTROPY_PROOF.md}{ID53–ID57} retain the exact edge-marginal information identity and prove the optimal finite-range entropy bound.
\clearpage
\section*{Independent derivation of the conditional edge score}

11 October 2026. The result of this derivation is that ESE1–ESE38 in \href{https://github.com/KokunoYumeto/yang-mills-interacting-workbench/blob/main/yang-mills/continuations/20261011-central-signs-shrinking-window/CONDITIONAL_EDGE_ENTROPY_AND_SCORE.md}{the complete edge-entropy proof} are correct, with the scope stated there. In particular, the stochastic argument proves the unconditional constant \(C_\rho=10.8333216102394742\ldots\). It does not prove the separate proposed oscillation bound \(\omega_e\le4\pi\sqrt\beta\). The proof below reconstructs the stochastic law, every factor in the entropy estimate, the closure, and the receiving phase calculation. Two additional entropy identities are proved after the core calculation.

Source versions and actual reading coverage are recorded in the source-use ledger. Fundamental Itô integration and the Brownian Gaussian increment law are the stochastic framework.

\subsection*{1. Original metric, Hamiltonian, and edge potential}

Retain \(L\ge2\), \(m=2L\), \(n=m+1\), the vertices \(o+a(i-L,k-L,l-L)\), \(0\le i,k,l\le m\), and every positively oriented original edge and face. Their counts are
\[
 N=3m(m+1)^2,\qquad M=3m^2(m+1),\qquad Q_L=SU(2)^{E_L}.
 \tag{ID1}
\]
The measure is product Haar probability. At every vertex, including the boundary, the gauge action on an edge \((s,t)\) is \(U_e\mapsto q_sU_eq_t^{-1}\). The three Lie generators and metric are
\[
 T_\alpha=-i\sigma_\alpha/2,\quad
 \langle v,w\rangle=-2\operatorname{tr}(vw),\quad
 v=\sum_\alpha v_\alpha T_\alpha,\quad |v|^2=\sum_\alpha v_\alpha^2,
 \quad X_{e,\alpha}f(U)=\left.\frac d{dh}f(\ldots,e^{hT_\alpha}U_e,\ldots)\right|_{h=0}.
 \tag{ID2}
\]
In quaternion coordinates \(U=cI+\sum_\alpha q^\alpha T_\alpha\), the equation for \(SU(2)\) is \(c^2+|q|^2/4=1\). Each \(T_\alpha\) has Euclidean quaternion length \(1/2\), while its length in (ID2) is one. Thus the given metric is the round three-sphere metric of radius two. Its diameter is \(2\pi\), sectional curvature \(1/4\), and Ricci tensor \((1/2)g\). The Laplacian and complete Hamiltonian are
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 K&=-\sum_{e,\alpha}X_{e,\alpha}^2,\qquad \Delta=-K,\\
 W_{(z;i,j)}&=\operatorname{tr}[U_i(z)U_j(z+e_i)U_i(z+e_j)^{-1}U_j(z)^{-1}],\\
 \kappa&=2g^2/a>0,\quad b_{\rm mag}=1/(2g^2a),\quad\xi=b_{\rm mag}/\kappa=1/(4g^4),\\
 V&=\kappa\xi\sum_p(2-W_p),\qquad H=\kappa K+V,\\
 H\psi&=E_0\psi,\qquad \psi>0,\qquad \int\psi^2dU=1,\qquad u=\log\psi,\quad\rho=\psi^2.
 \end{aligned}\tag{ID3}
\]
The finite product is compact and connected. For completeness, the lowest Rayleigh minimizer exists by compactness of the Sobolev inclusion; replacing a minimizer by its modulus cannot increase its kinetic form. The smooth bounded potential and the eigen-equation give smoothness by successive applications of \((I+K)^{-1}\) and Sobolev embedding. The nonnegative eigenfunction is strictly positive by the elliptic strong maximum principle. Uniqueness can then be checked without a positivity-improving assertion: writing any other ground vector as \(\psi F\), integration by parts and (ID3) give
\[
 \langle\psi F,(H-E_0)\psi F\rangle
       =\kappa\int\psi^2\sum_{e,\alpha}|X_{e,\alpha}F|^2dU.
 \tag{ID4}
\]
The right side vanishes only for constant \(F\). Gauge actions preserve the operator, positivity, and unit norm, and therefore fix \(\psi\). This establishes that this is the actual physical unit vacuum. Its positive minimum, finite maximum, and bounded derivatives will be used only at fixed finite volume.

For one original edge \(e\), let \(r_e\in\{2,3,4\}\) count its incident faces and put
\[
 \beta=r_e\xi,\quad
 V_e=\kappa\xi\sum_{p:e\in\partial p}(2-W_p),\quad
 V_{\bar e}=\kappa\xi\sum_{p:e\notin\partial p}(2-W_p),\quad V=V_e+V_{\bar e}.
 \tag{ID5}
\]
The full scalar \(2\kappa\xi M\) remains in \(V\). Since each face trace lies in \([-2,2]\), \(0\le V_e\le4\kappa\beta\). To compute its derivative constant, isolate an occurrence as \(A U_e B\) or \(A U_e^{-1}B\). Multiplication and inversion are isometries, so the gradient of that trace has the norm of the gradient of \(2c\) at the resulting group element. On the radius-two sphere the squared norm is \(1-c^2\). Each face contributes at most one, including its inverse occurrence. The full sum therefore satisfies
\[
 0\le V_e\le4\kappa\beta,\qquad
 |\nabla_eV|=|\nabla_eV_e|\le\kappa\beta.
 \tag{ID6}
\]

\subsection*{2. Product Wiener space and the exact vacuum tilt}

Use one independent standard three-dimensional Brownian motion \(B^f\) per original edge. All calculations are on this product Wiener space with its completed natural filtration. Let \(X_0=x\) be an arbitrary full configuration and solve
\[
 dX_s^f=\sqrt{2\kappa}\sum_\alpha T_\alpha X_s^f\,dB_s^{f,\alpha}
                       -\frac{3\kappa}{4}X_s^f\,ds.
 \tag{ID7}
\]
The matrix coefficients are linear, giving a global strong solution. The equivalent Stratonovich equation has no drift, because \(\sum_\alpha T_\alpha^2=-3I/4\) and the conversion contributes \(\kappa\sum T_\alpha^2X\). Its product rule preserves \(X^*X=I\) and determinant one. On smooth functions its generator is exactly \(\kappa\Delta\). In particular each edge solution is a measurable function of that edge's Wiener noise and its initial edge value; independence is retained across edges.

Define, for \(0\le s\le t<\infty\),
\[
 Z_s=\frac{e^{E_0s-\int_0^sV(X_r)dr}\psi(X_s)}{\psi(x)}.
 \tag{ID8}
\]
The eigen-equation reads \(\kappa\Delta\psi=(V-E_0)\psi\). Applying Itô's product formula to (ID8) gives
\[
 dZ_s=\frac{\sqrt{2\kappa}\,e^{E_0s-\int_0^sV(X_r)dr}}{\psi(x)}
                  \sum_{f,\alpha}(X_{f,\alpha}\psi)(X_s)dB_s^{f,\alpha}.
 \tag{ID9}
\]
Every displayed coefficient is bounded by a deterministic finite constant on \([0,t]\); this follows from compactness and boundedness of \(E_0,V,\nabla\psi\), and \(\psi(x)>0\). Thus the stochastic integrals have zero expectation and are square-integrable. Consequently \(Z_s\) is a genuine positive martingale, \(\mathbb E Z_s=Z_0=1\), and
\[
 d\mathbb Q_x=Z_t\,d\mathbb P_x,\qquad
 \psi(x)=e^{E_0t}\mathbb E_x[e^{-\int_0^tV(X_s)ds}\psi(X_t)].
 \tag{ID10}
\]
This proves the needed vacuum Feynman–Kac identity while keeping \(E_0\), the terminal factor, and every potential term.

\subsection*{3. Derivation of the controlled group path law}

Fix \(t>0\), a unit \(v=\sum v_\alpha T_\alpha\), and \(\epsilon\in\mathbb R\). Set
\[
 a_s=1-s/t,\quad g_s^\epsilon=e^{\epsilon a_sv},\quad
 Y_s^{e,\epsilon}=g_s^\epsilon X_s^e,\quad
 Y_s^{f,\epsilon}=X_s^f\ (f\ne e).
 \tag{ID11}
\]
The initial point is \(x_e^\epsilon=e^{\epsilon v}x_e\), while \(Y_t^\epsilon=X_t\) exactly as full configurations. This transformation preserves the terminal point without replacing the original edge path measure by a bridge.

Write \(\operatorname{Ad}_{g_s^\epsilon}T_\alpha=\sum_\gamma R_{s,\gamma\alpha}^\epsilon T_\gamma\). Invariance of the trace metric proves \(R_s^\epsilon(R_s^\epsilon)^{\mathsf T}=I\), and continuity from \(\epsilon=0\) proves determinant one. Since \(g_s^\epsilon\) is an exponential of \(v\),
\[
 R_s^\epsilon v=v,\qquad (R_s^\epsilon)^{\mathsf T}v=v.
 \tag{ID12}
\]
Define \(B_s^{e,\epsilon}=\int_0^sR_r^\epsilon dB_r^e\). This is a Gaussian process: its integrands are deterministic, and its increments over disjoint intervals use disjoint original increments. Its covariance is
\[
 d\langle B^{e,\epsilon,\alpha},B^{e,\epsilon,\gamma}\rangle_s
 =\delta_{\alpha\gamma}ds,\quad
 d\langle B^{e,\epsilon,\alpha},B^{f,\gamma}\rangle_s=0\ (f\ne e),
 \quad v\cdot B_s^{e,\epsilon}=v\cdot B_s^e.
 \tag{ID13}
\]
It is thus standard Brownian motion and independent of all outside noises. The inverse is \(B_s^e=\int_0^s(R_r^\epsilon)^{\mathsf T}dB_r^{e,\epsilon}\); deterministic stochastic-integral composition verifies this identity. The full completed filtrations before and after rotation coincide.

Since \(\partial_sg_s^\epsilon(g_s^\epsilon)^{-1}=-(\epsilon/t)v\), the Itô product rule, with no quadratic covariation against the deterministic \(g\), yields
\[
 dY_s^{e,\epsilon}
 =\sqrt{2\kappa}\sum_\alpha T_\alpha Y_s^{e,\epsilon}dB_s^{e,\epsilon,\alpha}
   -\frac{3\kappa}{4}Y_s^{e,\epsilon}ds
   -\frac\epsilon t vY_s^{e,\epsilon}ds.
 \tag{ID14}
\]
There is no \(\partial_sR\) correction. The integral defining \(B^{e,\epsilon}\) already contains its deterministic time dependence; the entire time derivative of \(g\) is the last term of (ID14).

Let \(\theta=\epsilon/(\sqrt{2\kappa}\,t)\) and
\[
 \Lambda_s^\epsilon
 =\exp\{\theta v\cdot B_s^{e,\epsilon}-\tfrac12\theta^2s\},\qquad
 \Lambda_t^\epsilon
 =\exp\left\{\frac{\epsilon v\cdot B_t^e}{\sqrt{2\kappa}\,t}
                      -\frac{\epsilon^2}{4\kappa t}\right\}.
 \tag{ID15}
\]
The quadratic variation of the exponent's martingale part is \(\epsilon^2s/(2\kappa t^2)\). Its compensator is therefore exactly \(-\epsilon^2s/(4\kappa t^2)\), and Gaussian integration gives expectation one.

Under \(d\mathbb P_\epsilon=\Lambda_t^\epsilon d\mathbb P_x\), the process
\[
 \widetilde B_s^{e,\epsilon}=B_s^{e,\epsilon}-\theta sv
 \tag{ID16}
\]
is standard Brownian motion. A direct verification needs no assumed change-of-measure theorem. On any finite partition, multiply the independent increment density by \(\prod_j\exp\{\theta v\cdot\Delta B_j-\theta^2\Delta s_j/2\}\). Completing the square shifts the mean of each increment to \(\theta v\Delta s_j\), retains covariance \(\Delta s_jI_3\), and retains independence. These partition distributions determine the continuous-path law. The likelihood depends only on the edge noise, so the outside Wiener noises keep their original joint law and remain independent of (ID16). The filtration equality after (ID13) gives the Brownian increment property in the full filtration as well.

Substitute \(dB^{e,\epsilon}=d\widetilde B^{e,\epsilon}+\theta vds\) into (ID14). The additional drift is \(+\sqrt{2\kappa}\theta vYds=+(\epsilon/t)vYds\), cancelling its negative counterpart. The remaining equation is exactly (ID7), started at \(x^\epsilon\). The strong solution law therefore proves
\[
 \mathcal L_{\mathbb P_\epsilon}(Y^\epsilon)
                  =\mathcal L_{\mathbb P_{x^\epsilon}}(X).
 \tag{ID17}
\]
Every sign, the original Itô correction, and the absence of a factor three in the directional variance have now been determined independently.

\subsection*{4. Global differentiation before disintegration}

Apply (ID10) at \(x^\epsilon\), use (ID17), and then use \(Y_t^\epsilon=X_t\). This gives the exact identities
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 \psi(x^\epsilon)&=e^{E_0t}\mathbb E_x[\Lambda_t^\epsilon
                  e^{-\int_0^tV(Y_s^\epsilon)ds}\psi(X_t)],\\
 \frac{\psi(x^\epsilon)}{\psi(x)}
 &=\mathbb E_{\mathbb Q_x}\left[\Lambda_t^\epsilon
             e^{-\int_0^t[V(Y_s^\epsilon)-V(X_s)]ds}\right].
 \end{aligned}\tag{ID18}
\]
The second line divides by the exact vacuum normalization. The full scalar potential cancels in the difference of the same full function \(V\). It is still present in the first line and in \(Z_t\). Outside paths and the terminal vacuum factor remain inside the full measure \(\mathbb Q_x\).

For all real \(\epsilon\), the path \(\eta\mapsto e^{\eta a_sv}X_s^e\) has speed \(a_s\). By (ID6),
\[
 |V(Y_s^\epsilon)-V(X_s)|\le\kappa\beta|\epsilon|a_s,\qquad
 |\partial_\epsilon V(Y_s^\epsilon)|\le\kappa\beta a_s.
 \tag{ID19}
\]
For \(|\epsilon|\le\epsilon_0\), the absolute value of the derivative of the integrand in the second line of (ID18) is bounded by a finite constant times

\( (1+|v\cdot B_t^e|)\exp\{\epsilon_0|v\cdot B_t^e|/(\sqrt{2\kappa}\,t)\}.\)

This is integrable under the original centered Gaussian law. Also \(Z_t\) is bounded above and below by positive finite deterministic constants at fixed volume, \(x\), and \(t\), because \(V\) and \(\psi\) are bounded and \(\min\psi>0\). It follows that the same bound is integrable under \(\mathbb Q_x\). The dominated difference quotient thus justifies differentiation, with no uniform-in-volume domination asserted or needed. It yields
\[
 X_{e,v}\log\psi(x)
 =\mathbb E_{\mathbb Q_x}\left[
    \frac{v\cdot B_t^e}{\sqrt{2\kappa}\,t}
       -\int_0^t(1-s/t)X_{e,v}V(X_s)ds\right].
 \tag{ID20}
\]
The terminal derivative vanishes because the terminal equality held before differentiation. In particular, no derivative of a conditional normalization or an outside marginal has been discarded: (ID20) is established for the full law first.

\subsection*{5. Exact conditioning, entropy, and mixture}

Let \(\mathbb P_e\) be the edge Wiener measure and \(\mathbb P_{\bar e}\) the law of all outside link paths. The joint law of the edge noise and those outside paths is \(\mathbb P_e\otimes\mathbb P_{\bar e}\), by the edgewise strong solution in (ID7). For an outside continuous link path \(y\) starting at \(x_{\bar e}\), define
\[
 R_y(B^e)=e^{-\int_0^tV_e(X_s^e,y_s)ds}\psi(X_t^e,y_t),\quad
 Z_y=\mathbb E_{\mathbb P_e}R_y,\quad q_y=R_y/Z_y.
 \tag{ID21}
\]
Here \(X^e\) is any measurable strong-solution version, completed arbitrarily on its null set. The explicit expressions define a probability density for each such continuous \(y\); assertions involving a conditional distribution of the full law hold for \(\mathbb Q_{\bar e}\)-almost every \(y\), as usual for disintegration. The stronger numerical bound below holds for every \(y\) in this version. Measurability follows by composing the measurable edge solution with the continuous potential and terminal function. With \(\psi_-=\min\psi>0\) and \(\psi_+=\max\psi\),
\[
 e^{-4\kappa\beta t}\psi_-\le R_y\le\psi_+,\qquad
 e^{-4\kappa\beta t}\psi_-\le Z_y\le\psi_+.
 \tag{ID22}
\]
Thus all the logarithms and all required conditional densities are bounded at fixed \(t\).

Define the outside density and conditional law by
\[
 h(y)=\frac{e^{E_0t}}{\psi(x)}
        e^{-\int_0^tV_{\bar e}(y_s)ds}Z_y,\quad
 d\mathbb Q_{\bar e}=h\,d\mathbb P_{\bar e},\quad
 d\mathbb Q_e^y=q_y\,d\mathbb P_e.
 \tag{ID23}
\]
Fubini and (ID10) give \(\int h\,d\mathbb P_{\bar e}=1\). Multiplying the two displayed densities reproduces \(Z_t\) exactly, so
\[
 d\mathbb Q_x=q_y\,d\mathbb P_e\,d\mathbb Q_{\bar e}(y).
 \tag{ID24}
\]
This explicitly proves the conditional law and its actual mixture. The outside marginal generally differs from its Brownian reference marginal.

Set the actual finite vacuum quantities
\[
 \omega_e=\max_z\{\max_Uu(U,z)-\min_Uu(U,z)\},\qquad
 M_e=\max_{x\in Q_L}|\nabla_eu(x)|.
 \tag{ID25}
\]
For fixed \(y\), the terminal term \(u(X_t^e,y_t)\) has oscillation at most \(\omega_e\). The integral of \(V_e\) takes values between zero and \(4\kappa\beta t\). Their sum in \(\log R_y\) therefore has essential oscillation at most
\[
 D_t=\omega_e+4\kappa\beta t.
 \tag{ID26}
\]
If \(m_y\) and \(M_y\) are its essential infimum and supremum, then \(e^{m_y}\le Z_y\le e^{M_y}\); it follows that \(e^{-D_t}\le q_y\le e^{D_t}\). The conditional relative entropy is consequently
\[
 0\le I_y:=\int q_y\log q_y\,d\mathbb P_e\le D_t.
 \tag{ID27}
\]
The lower bound follows by integrating \(q\log q-q+1\ge0\). The upper bound follows by integrating \(\log q_y\le D_t\) against its own probability density. This is the entropy of the edge noise after conditioning on the entire outside path, with the terminal vacuum present.

The full path entropy has instead the exact formula
\[
 \operatorname{Ent}(\mathbb Q_x\mid\mathbb P_e\otimes\mathbb P_{\bar e})
 =\operatorname{Ent}(\mathbb Q_{\bar e}\mid\mathbb P_{\bar e})
                  +\int I_y\,d\mathbb Q_{\bar e}(y).
 \tag{ID28}
\]
Indeed the full density is \(hq_y\); insert \(\log(hq_y)=\log h+\log q_y\) and integrate using \(\int q_y\,d\mathbb P_e=1\). All logarithms here are bounded, so no integrability qualification is hidden. The outside entropy is not estimated by (ID27).

For \(Z=v\cdot B_t^e\) under \(\mathbb P_e\), the variance is exactly \(t\) and the moment generating function is \(\mathbb E e^{\lambda Z}=e^{\lambda^2t/2}\). For the bounded positive density \(q_y\), Jensen's inequality gives
\[
 \lambda\mathbb E_{q_y}Z-I_y
 =\mathbb E_{q_y}\log(e^{\lambda Z}/q_y)
 \le\log\mathbb E_{\mathbb P_e}e^{\lambda Z}
 =\lambda^2t/2.
 \tag{ID29}
\]
All expectations are finite by the bound on \(q_y\). Choosing the sign of \(\lambda\) to agree with the mean and minimizing \(I_y/|\lambda|+|\lambda|t/2\) gives
\[
 |\mathbb E_{q_y}Z|\le\sqrt{2tI_y}.
 \tag{ID30}
\]
For \(I_y=0\), let \(|\lambda|\downarrow0\); the result still holds. The same inequality for arbitrary finite-entropy densities can be proved by truncation, but that additional generality is unnecessary for this bounded conditional law.

Integrating (ID30) against the actual marginal (ID23), rather than against the Brownian outside marginal, proves
\[
 \left|\mathbb E_{\mathbb Q_x}\frac{v\cdot B_t^e}{\sqrt{2\kappa}\,t}\right|
 \le\int\sqrt{\frac{I_y}{\kappa t}}\,d\mathbb Q_{\bar e}(y)
 \le\sqrt{\frac{\omega_e}{\kappa t}+4\beta}.
 \tag{ID31}
\]
Together with \(\int_0^t(1-s/t)ds=t/2\) and (ID6), this proves
\[
 |X_{e,v}u(x)|\le\sqrt{\frac{\omega_e}{\kappa t}+4\beta}
                       +\frac{\kappa\beta t}{2},\qquad
 M_e\le\sqrt{\frac{\omega_e}{\tau}+4\beta}+\frac{\beta\tau}{2},
 \quad\tau=\kappa t>0.
 \tag{ID32}
\]
Taking the supremum over all unit \(v\) is precisely the norm of the three-component gradient in (ID2). It adds no dimension factor.

\subsection*{6. Closing the bound and retaining the earlier bound}

For fixed outside configuration \(z\), integrate \(du\) on a shortest one-edge geodesic between its minimum and maximum. Its length is at most \(2\pi\), so
\[
 \omega_e\le2\pi M_e.
 \tag{ID33}
\]
The numbers in (ID25) are finite before any estimate. Therefore, when \(\beta>0\) and \(M_e>0\), the choice \(\tau=cM_e/\beta\), \(0<c<2\), is a permitted positive time in (ID32). It gives
\[
 (1-c/2)M_e\le\sqrt{\beta(2\pi/c+4)},\qquad
 M_e^2\le\beta\Phi(c),\qquad
 \Phi(c)=\frac{2\pi/c+4}{(1-c/2)^2}.
 \tag{ID34}
\]
No fixed-time restriction exists in (ID32), and this selection does not assume the desired bound. The derivative is
\[
 \Phi'(c)=\frac{4c^2+3\pi c-2\pi}{c^2(1-c/2)^3}.
 \tag{ID35}
\]
Its numerator is strictly increasing for \(c>0\), negative at zero, positive at two. The function tends to infinity at both endpoints. Thus its unique minimizing point and constants are
\[
 c_* =\frac{\sqrt{9\pi^2+32\pi}-3\pi}{8},\quad
 C_\psi=\sqrt{\Phi(c_*)},\quad C_\rho=2C_\psi,
 \qquad M_e\le C_\psi\sqrt\beta,\quad
 |\nabla_e\log\rho|\le C_\rho\sqrt\beta.
 \tag{ID36}
\]
In particular \(c=1/2\) gives \(M_e\le(8/3)\sqrt{1+\pi}\sqrt\beta\), and the optimized density constant is \(10.8333216102394742\ldots\). If \(M_e=0\), the estimate is immediate. If \(\beta=0\), \(V\) is independent of the edge, left translations in that edge commute with \(H\), and uniqueness of the positive unit vacuum forces their invariance; then \(M_e=\omega_e=0\).

The earlier linear estimate follows directly from the same full equation. Set \(w_e=|\nabla_eu|^2\). In a product of geodesic orthonormal frames, twice differentiating this square gives its Hessian-square terms, the derivative of \(\Delta u\), and the Ricci commutator in factor \(e\):
\[
 \Delta w_e=2\sum_f|\nabla_f\nabla_eu|_{\rm HS}^2
           +2\langle\nabla_eu,\nabla_e\Delta u\rangle+w_e.
 \tag{ID37}
\]
Mixed factors have zero curvature, and \(2\operatorname{Ric}_e(\nabla_eu,\nabla_eu)=w_e\). Differentiating \(\Delta u+|\nabla u|^2=(V-E_0)/\kappa\) gives a term \(-2\langle\nabla_eu,\nabla_e|\nabla u|^2\rangle\). Symmetry of the scalar Hessian identifies this as \(-2\nabla u\cdot\nabla w_e\). Hence
\[
 (\Delta+2\nabla u\cdot\nabla)w_e
 =2\sum_f|\nabla_f\nabla_eu|_{\rm HS}^2+w_e
       +2\kappa^{-1}\langle\nabla_eu,\nabla_eV\rangle.
 \tag{ID38}
\]
At a global maximum of \(w_e\), the left side is nonpositive. By (ID6), \(0\ge M_e^2-2\beta M_e\), so \(M_e\le2\beta\). Keeping both estimates proves
\[
 |\nabla_e\log\rho|\le\min\{4r_e\xi,C_\rho\sqrt{r_e\xi}\}.
 \tag{ID39}
\]

For the full signed-source Hamiltonian \(H(x)=\kappa K+\kappa\sum_px_p(2-W_p)\), define \(\beta_e(x)=\sum_{p:e\in\partial p}|x_p|\). At fixed finite real \(x\), the potential is bounded, its incident part has global range length at most \(4\kappa\beta_e(x)\), and its edge gradient has norm at most \(\kappa\beta_e(x)\). In (ID22) the lower and upper potential endpoints replace zero and \(4\kappa\beta t\); their difference in (ID26) is still at most \(4\kappa\beta_e(x)t\). All other steps remain exactly as written, including the full scalar \(2\kappa\sum_px_p\), energy, and unit normalization. Therefore
\[
 |\nabla_e\log\rho_x|
      \le\min\{4\beta_e(x),C_\rho\sqrt{\beta_e(x)}\}.
 \tag{ID40}
\]

If one separately had \(\omega_e\le4\pi\sqrt\beta\), then substituting \(\tau=2/\sqrt\beta\) in (ID32) would give \((1+\sqrt{4+2\pi})\sqrt\beta\) for \(M_e\). This arithmetic does not establish its oscillation premise. Neither that premise nor its smaller constant is used in (ID36)–(ID40).

\subsection*{7. Exact receiving coefficient and unchanged phase path}

The receiving phase is the complete \(F=\beta_0(\mathcal S-\mathcal S_{\mathcal R})\) of PRE10–PRE16, where
\[
 \begin{gathered}
 h=\pi/(2n),\quad v_0(l)=n^{-1/2},\quad
 v_1(l)=\sqrt{2/n}\cos(\pi(l+1/2)/n),\quad
 w_1(l)=-\sqrt{2/n}\sin(\pi(l+1)/n),\\
 a_{(i,k,l;2)}=-v_1(i)w_1(k)v_0(l)/\sqrt2,\quad a_{(i,k,l;3)}=0,\\
 b_{(i,k,l;2)}=v_0(i)w_1(k)v_1(l)/\sqrt2,\quad
 b_{(i,k,l;3)}=-v_0(i)v_1(k)w_1(l)/\sqrt2,\\
 Z_c=t_sU_ct_t^{-1},\quad q^\alpha(U)=-2\operatorname{tr}(T_\alpha U),\quad
 u_1=\sum_ca_cq(Z_c),\quad u_3=\sum_cb_cq(Z_c),\quad
 \mathcal S=u_1\cdot u_3,\quad \sigma_*=\sqrt8\sin h,\quad\beta_0=\sigma_*/(2g^2).
 \end{gathered}\tag{ID41}
\]
The chord sets remain \(\mathcal C_2=\{(i,k,l;2):1\le i\le m,0\le k<m,0\le l\le m\}\) and \(\mathcal C_3=\{(i,k,l;3):0\le i,k\le m,(i,k)\ne(0,0),0\le l<m\}\). The original tree root is \((0,0,0)\), with root paths in directions three, two, one. The reflection retains its reflected root and all inverse edge orientations. All constants and affine terms in PRE16 are included by these full words; no truncated phase is used.

Here is a verification of the precise degree fact used in ESE33. The quaternion product gives
\[
 q(U)\cdot q(V)=\operatorname{tr}(UV^{-1})-\operatorname{tr}(UV),\quad
 \mathcal S=\sum_{c,d}a_cb_d[\operatorname{tr}(Z_cZ_d^{-1})-\operatorname{tr}(Z_cZ_d)].
 \tag{ID42}
\]
For a chosen tree edge, let \(\epsilon_z\) indicate that its root path uses that edge. Its physical variation gives \(t_z(t)=C(t)^{\epsilon_z}t_z\), with \(C(t)=A_e e^{tT_\alpha}A_e^{-1}\). For an inverse traversal the conjugate subgroup has parameter \(-t\). In either case the complete chord becomes \(Z_c(t)=C(t)^{\epsilon_s}Z_cC(t)^{-\epsilon_t}\). For chords \(c=(s,t)\) and \(d=(u,w)\), cyclicity gives
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 \operatorname{tr}(Z_c(t)Z_d(t)^{-1})
 &=\operatorname{tr}[Z_cC(t)^{\epsilon_w-\epsilon_t}Z_d^{-1}C(t)^{\epsilon_s-\epsilon_u}],\\
 \operatorname{tr}(Z_c(t)Z_d(t))
 &=\operatorname{tr}[Z_cC(t)^{\epsilon_u-\epsilon_t}Z_dC(t)^{\epsilon_s-\epsilon_w}].
 \end{aligned}\tag{ID43}
\]
Every exponent is \(-1,0,1\), so there are at most two factors linear in \(\cos(t/2),\sin(t/2)\). A chord edge appears only in its own word, and at most twice in each trace pair, giving the same degree. Applying this to the reflected full tree proves the same property for \(\mathcal S_{\mathcal R}\). Thus the entire \(F\), including all its remainders, has trigonometric degree at most two in \(t/2\) along each original physical subgroup.

For a real function \(f\) with this degree, set \(z=\tan(t/4)\). Then
\[
 f(e^{tT_\alpha}U_e,\ldots)=\frac{P_U(z)}{(1+z^2)^2},\quad
 \deg P_U\le4,\quad P_U'(0)=4X_{e,\alpha}f(U),\quad dt=\frac{4\,dz}{1+z^2}.
 \tag{ID44}
\]
On \([-1,1]\), the five polynomials \(1,y,(3y^2-1)/2,(5y^3-3y)/2,(35y^4-30y^2+3)/8\) have pairwise zero integrals of products and squared norms \(2,2/3,2/5,2/7,2/9\); direct expansion and \(\int_{-1}^1y^{2k}dy=2/(2k+1)\) verify this. Only the degree-one and degree-three derivatives at zero survive, with values \(1,-3/2\). Cauchy–Schwarz for this derivative functional and rescaling the interval consequently give
\[
 |P'(0)|^2\le\frac{75}{8r^3}\int_{-r}^r|P(z)|^2dz.
 \tag{ID45}
\]
For \(0<r\le1/4\), retain the exact denominator in (ID44), bound \((1+z^2)^5\le(1+r^2)^5\), and divide by \(16\). The result is
\[
 |X_{e,\alpha}f(U)|^2
 \le\frac{75(1+r^2)^5}{512r^3}
        \int_{-4\arctan r}^{4\arctan r}|f(e^{tT_\alpha}U_e,\ldots)|^2dt.
 \tag{ID46}
\]
If the actual density satisfies \(|X_{e,\alpha}\log\rho|\le B\), integration of that derivative gives \(\rho(U)\le e^{B|t|}\rho(e^{tT_\alpha}U_e,\ldots)\). Multiply (ID46) by \(\rho(U)\), integrate the full original product Haar measure, use invariance of its translated edge, and bound the time factor by \(e^{4B\arctan r}\). The interval length is \(8\arctan r\). Hence
\[
 \mu(|X_{e,\alpha}f|^2)\le b_2(B;r)\mu(f^2),\quad
 b_2(B;r)=\frac{75}{64}(1+r^2)^5e^{4B\arctan r}\frac{\arctan r}{r^3}.
 \tag{ID47}
\]
Taking \(r_B=1/[2(B+2)]\), \(b_2(B)=b_2(B;r_B)\), one has \(Br_B\to1/2\), \((\arctan r_B)/r_B\to1\), and \(B^2r_B^2\to1/4\). Therefore
\[
 \lim_{B\to\infty}\frac{b_2(B)}{B^2}=\frac{75e^2}{16}.
 \tag{ID48}
\]
This verifies the entire ESE33 coefficient without relying on an unspecified norm comparison.

Define \(B_r(\xi)=\min\{4r\xi,C_\rho\sqrt{r\xi}\}\). For each of three edge directions there are \(m\) longitudinal positions; the two transverse coordinates have four corners, \(4(m-1)\) noncorner boundary points, and \((m-1)^2\) interior points. Their face-incidence numbers are respectively \(2,3,4\). Thus the multiplicities are exactly \(12m,12m(m-1),3m(m-1)^2\). Summing (ID47) for the three colour fields on every edge, with \(G=\mu(F^2)>0\) and \(C_F=\kappa\mu(|\nabla F|^2)\), proves
\[
 \frac{C_F}{G}\le\kappa\widetilde{\mathcal B}_L(\xi),\qquad
 \widetilde{\mathcal B}_L(\xi)
 =3[12m\,b_2(B_2(\xi))+12m(m-1)b_2(B_3(\xi))
                            +3m(m-1)^2b_2(B_4(\xi))].
 \tag{ID49}
\]
To check \(G>0\) for this specific function, put all links at identity except \(K_\circ=Z_{(1,L-1,0;2)}=e^{\pi T_1}\) in the original tree presentation. Its boundary products are identity; the two coefficients are \(a^\circ=\sqrt2n^{-3/2}\cos(3h)\cos h\) and \(b^\circ=-\sqrt2n^{-3/2}\cos^2h\), while their reflected values are \(-a^\circ,b^\circ\). Hence \(F=8\beta_0a^\circ b^\circ\ne0\), whereas \(F=0\) at the identity configuration. Positivity of \(\rho\) proves the strict mass inequality.

Finally retain precisely
\[
 L_j=j^4,\quad a_j=1/(100j),\quad g_j^2=\kappa_*/(200j),\quad
 \kappa_j=\kappa_*,\quad \xi_j=10000j^2/\kappa_*^2,\quad
 s_j=\kappa_*/j,\quad m_j=2j^4,\qquad j\ge2.
 \tag{ID50}
\]
For each \(r\in\{2,3,4\}\), eventually \(B_r(\xi_j)=C_\rho\sqrt{r\xi_j}\). The multiplicity of the \(r=4\) edges is \(3m_j(m_j-1)^2\sim24j^{12}\), their score square is \(40000C_\rho^2j^2/\kappa_*^2\), and there are three colour fields. Thus
\[
 \lim_{j\to\infty}\frac{\kappa_*\widetilde{\mathcal B}_{L_j}(\xi_j)}{j^{14}}
 =\frac{3\cdot24\cdot(75e^2/16)\cdot40000C_\rho^2}{\kappa_*}
 =\frac{13500000e^2C_\rho^2}{\kappa_*}.
 \tag{ID51}
\]
The \(r=2\) and \(r=3\) contributions have orders \(j^6\) and \(j^{10}\), and their exact values remain in (ID49). Dividing the quotient bound by \(s_j\) now gives
\[
 G_j-C_{F,j}/s_j\ge[1-j\widetilde{\mathcal B}_{L_j}(\xi_j)]G_j,\qquad
 \lim_{j\to\infty}\frac{j\widetilde{\mathcal B}_{L_j}(\xi_j)}{j^{15}}
 =\frac{13500000e^2C_\rho^2}{\kappa_*^2}.
 \tag{ID52}
\]
This upper quotient estimate has order \(j^{14}\); the lower endpoint coefficient in (ID52) eventually becomes negative. No conclusion about the sign of the actual endpoint or gaplessness follows from that negative lower bound.

\subsection*{8. Two additional entropy consequences}

First, the actual unconditional edge-noise marginal has density
\[
 \bar q(B^e)=\int q_y(B^e)\,d\mathbb Q_{\bar e}(y),\qquad
 d\mathbb Q_e=\bar q\,d\mathbb P_e.
 \tag{ID53}
\]
It remains bounded above and below by \(e^{D_t}\) and \(e^{-D_t}\). Directly inserting \(\log q_y=\log\bar q+\log(q_y/\bar q)\) gives the exact additional relation
\[
 \int I_y\,d\mathbb Q_{\bar e}(y)
 =\operatorname{Ent}(\mathbb Q_e\mid\mathbb P_e)
  +\operatorname{Ent}(\mathbb Q_x\mid\mathbb Q_e\otimes\mathbb Q_{\bar e}).
 \tag{ID54}
\]
The last term is nonnegative by \(r\log r-r+1\ge0\). Consequently the unconditional edge-noise entropy, unlike the full path entropy, is also at most \(D_t\). Applying (ID29) to \(\bar q\) is another valid proof of (ID31). Formula (ID54) records the exact information lost by passing from the conditional laws to their mixture.

Second, the entropy estimate from a logarithmic range can be improved at every finite positive range. For any \(R>0\) whose essential logarithmic oscillation is at most \(D>0\), multiply \(R\) by a positive constant so its bounds become \(1\le R\le e^D\); this auxiliary multiplication leaves its probability density \(q=R/\mathbb ER\) exactly unchanged and does not replace any potential or vacuum. Write \(z=\mathbb ER\). The convex graph \(r\log r\) lies below its chord between \(1\) and \(e^D\), so
\[
 R\log R\le\frac{De^D}{e^D-1}(R-1).
 \tag{ID55}
\]
Let \(A(D)=D/(1-e^{-D})\). Dividing the expectation of (ID55) by \(z\) and subtracting \(\log z\) proves
\[
 \operatorname{Ent}(q\mid\mathbb P)
 \le A(D)(1-1/z)-\log z\le A(D)-1-\log A(D)=:J(D).
 \tag{ID56}
\]
Indeed \(1<A(D)<e^D\) follows from \(1-e^{-D}<D<e^D-1\); the derivative of \(A(D)(1-1/z)-\log z\) is \((A(D)-z)/z^2\), so its maximum on \([1,e^D]\) occurs at \(z=A(D)\). Equality is attained by a two-point \(R\) on \(\{1,e^D\}\), assigning upper probability \((A(D)-1)/(e^D-1)\). Hence (ID56) is the sharp range-only bound. Extend \(J(0)=0\) by continuity; its Taylor expansion begins \(D^2/8\). Every such density also obeys the earlier bound \(D\); the displayed maximum and its attaining positive two-point distribution show \(J(D)<D\) for \(D>0\), since \(\log q<D\) on both positive-probability points.

Applying (ID56) to the same \(R_y\) in (ID21) proves \(I_y\le J(D_t)\), and (ID20), (ID24), and (ID30) therefore strengthen (ID32) to
\[
 |X_{e,v}u(x)|\le\sqrt{\frac{J(\omega_e+4\kappa\beta t)}{\kappa t}}
                       +\frac{\kappa\beta t}{2}.
 \tag{ID57}
\]
The full Hamiltonian, outside mixture, and terminal vacuum are identical to those in the core proof. This finite-oscillation improvement is a proved consequence, not a repair required for ESE1–ESE38. No novelty claim is made.

\subsection*{9. Comparison and exact scope}

\begin{figure}[htbp]\centering\includegraphics[width=\linewidth,height=.72\textheight,keepaspectratio]{CONDITIONAL_ENTROPY.png}\caption{Exact conditional-law diagram and entropy bounds}\end{figure}

The figure records the measure maps of ID8–ID10 and ID21–ID24, the entropy chain rule of ID28, and the marginal relation of ID53–ID54. Its graph plots the exact bounds \(D\) and \(J(D)\) proved in ID55–ID56; it does not depict sampled vacuum data. The original potential term and terminal vacuum are displayed in the conditional density. Reproducible sources are retained in entropy\_figure.py and CONDITIONAL\_ENTROPY.svg. These are illustrations of this independent calculation; no external theorem or novelty attribution is inferred from them.

ESE1–ESE6 are reconstructed in ID1–ID10; ESE7–ESE13 in ID11–ID17; ESE14–ESE15 in ID18–ID20; ESE16–ESE23 in ID21–ID32; ESE24–ESE31 in ID33–ID40; the arithmetic and scope of ESE32 follow after ID40; ESE33–ESE38 are reconstructed in ID41–ID52. ID53–ID57 are additional consequences. There is no mathematical correction needed to the candidate formulas in the stated finite-volume scope.

The path-space wording can be made more precise by saying that the displayed conditional-density version is defined and bounded for every continuous outside group path, and represents the actual conditional law almost everywhere. All dominated differentiation is at fixed finite volume and fixed positive time; the resulting score constants are independent of volume because their final formulas depend only on the actual local incidence \(r_e\), \(\xi\), and the retained edge metric. No volume-limit interchange is used.


The separate oscillation estimate discussed after ID40 is proved in \href{https://github.com/KokunoYumeto/yang-mills-interacting-workbench/blob/main/yang-mills/continuations/20261011-central-signs-shrinking-window/HEAT_RATIO_PROOF.md}{HR17–HR23}; \href{https://github.com/KokunoYumeto/yang-mills-interacting-workbench/blob/main/yang-mills/continuations/20261011-central-signs-shrinking-window/SQUARE_ROOT_LOCAL_SCORE.md}{LS20–LS32} give the complete resulting smaller score constant. The independent entropy closure above remains valid with its own stated constant.

\section*{The exact radius-two SU(2) heat-kernel ratio and its one-edge consequence}

This independent derivation proves, for every real \(\tau>0\),
\[
 R(\tau):=\sup_{u,v,w\in SU(2)}
 \frac{p_\tau(u,w)}{p_\tau(v,w)}
 =\frac{p_\tau(0)}{p_\tau(\pi)}
 <\exp(\pi^2/\tau).                                      \tag{HR1}
\]
The constant \(\pi^2\) is the smallest constant \(C\) for which
\(R(\tau)\le e^{C/\tau}\) can hold at every positive time.
Here \(p_\tau\) is the kernel of the original generator
\(\Delta=\sum_{\alpha=1}^3X_\alpha^2\), with
\(T_\alpha=-i\sigma_\alpha/2\) orthonormal, relative to probability
Haar measure. The symbols \(0,\pi\) in the radial notation are the
angles \(d/2\); their physical distances are \(0,2\pi\).

The candidate LS12–LS16 establishes the necessary estimate only on
\(0<\tau\le\pi^2\). That range already suffices for the candidate's
square-root oscillation argument. Section HR5 below proves the additional
all-time assertion without assuming any extension of that range.

\subsection*{HR2. Metric, Haar measure, generator and exact spectral series}

Write
\[
 U=cI+\sum_{\alpha=1}^3q_\alpha T_\alpha,
 \qquad c^2+\tfrac14\sum_\alpha q_\alpha^2=1.
\]
The map \(U\mapsto(2c,q_1,q_2,q_3)\) maps \(SU(2)\) onto the
radius-two sphere in \(\mathbb R^4\). At the identity, the curve
\(\exp(tT_\alpha)\) maps to
\((2\cos(t/2),2\sin(t/2)e_\alpha)\), whose speed is one.
Left and right multiplication by unit quaternions act orthogonally on
these coordinates. Consequently the sphere metric is exactly the
bi-invariant metric making the three stated generators orthonormal.

Put \(c=\cos\theta\), \(q=2\sin\theta\,\omega\), with
\(0\le\theta\le\pi\) and \(\omega\in S^2\). The metric, volume,
probability Haar measure and radial generator are, respectively,
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 ds^2&=4\,d\theta^2+4\sin^2\theta\,ds_{S^2}^2,\\
 d\mathrm{vol}&=8\sin^2\theta\,d\theta\,d\omega,
 \qquad \mathrm{vol}(SU(2))=16\pi^2,\\
 dH&=\frac{\sin^2\theta}{2\pi^2}\,d\theta\,d\omega
   =\frac2\pi\sin^2\theta\,d\theta\,\frac{d\omega}{4\pi},\\
 \Delta_{\mathrm{rad}}&=\frac14
   (\partial_\theta^2+2\cot\theta\,\partial_\theta).
 \end{aligned}                                                   \tag{HR2}
\]
The last formula follows by applying the divergence formula to the
displayed metric and volume. In particular, \(d(I,U)=2\theta\), and
\(p_\tau=16\pi^2p_\tau^{\mathrm{vol}}\); this measure factor has not
been replaced by a different convention.

For each integer \(n\ge1\), set
\(\chi_{n-1}(\theta)=\sin(n\theta)/\sin\theta\), with its continuous
endpoint values. Direct differentiation and the sine orthogonality
integral give
\[
 \Delta\chi_{n-1}=-\frac{n^2-1}{4}\chi_{n-1},\qquad
 \frac2\pi\int_0^\pi\chi_{n-1}\chi_{m-1}\sin^2\theta\,d\theta
 =\delta_{nm},\qquad \chi_{n-1}(0)=n.                       \tag{HR3}
\]
Multiplication by \(\sin\theta\) identifies the radial weighted
\(L^2\) space with ordinary \(L^2(0,\pi)\), up to the displayed
constant \(2/\pi\). Completeness is therefore sine-series completeness.
The heat evolution of a radial smooth test function at the identity
has coefficient \(\chi_{n-1}(0)=n\), hence its exact kernel is
\[
 p_\tau(\theta)=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}
   n e^{-\tau(n^2-1)/4}\frac{\sin(n\theta)}{\sin\theta}.
                                                               \tag{HR4}
\]
The conjugation average of any smooth test function has its original
value at the identity, so the same expression gives the point-source
kernel on the full group. Left translation gives
\(p_\tau(u,w)=p_\tau(d(u,w)/2)\). The finite identity
\[
 \frac{\sin(n\theta)}{\sin\theta}
 =e^{i(n-1)\theta}+e^{i(n-3)\theta}+\cdots+e^{-i(n-1)\theta}
                                                               \tag{HR5}
\]
gives \(|\chi_{n-1}|\le n\) including both endpoints. It also shows
that each angular derivative is bounded by a power of \(n\).
Thus the series and every fixed number of derivatives converge
uniformly when \(\tau\) stays bounded away from zero. The integral
against \(dH\) is one because only \(n=1\) has nonzero integral.

The maximum principle preserves nonnegative initial functions and the
constant function one. The associated heat kernel is therefore
nonnegative. It is strictly positive at each positive time: if the
nonnegative heat kernel vanished at an interior point of spacetime,
the strong parabolic maximum principle, applied from any earlier
positive time on the connected compact manifold, would force it to
vanish identically there, contrary to its integral one. This uses
only the usual local maximum principle for a smooth elliptic
Laplacian; there is no singular radial endpoint in this argument.

\subsection*{HR3. The exact extrema}

For \(\varepsilon>0\), let
\(f_\varepsilon(\theta)=a_\varepsilon e^{\cos\theta/\varepsilon}\),
where \(a_\varepsilon>0\) makes its Haar integral one. This is a
smooth radial density, \(f_\varepsilon'\le0\), and its probability
measures converge to \(\delta_I\). To check the last statement,
the mass outside any angular ball \(\theta<\eta\) is bounded by
a fixed volume ratio times
\(e^{(\cos\eta-\cos(\eta/2))/\varepsilon}\), which tends to zero.

Let \(u_\varepsilon=P_\tau f_\varepsilon\) and
\(v_\varepsilon=\partial_\theta u_\varepsilon\). Smoothness at the
two poles gives \(v_\varepsilon(\tau,0)=v_\varepsilon(\tau,\pi)=0\),
and differentiating HR2 gives
\[
 \partial_\tau v_\varepsilon
 =\frac14(v_\varepsilon''+2\cot\theta\,v_\varepsilon'
                         -2\csc^2\theta\,v_\varepsilon).       \tag{HR6}
\]
On a finite time strip, suppose there were a positive value. A
positive maximum over that strip cannot lie on either spatial
boundary or at initial time. At an interior spatial maximum and
a time of maximum up to that time, \(v'=0\), \(v''\le0\), and
\(\partial_\tau v\ge0\), whereas HR6 gives
\(\partial_\tau v\le-\tfrac12\csc^2\theta\,v<0\).
This is a contradiction. All coefficients used at that maximum are
finite, so the radial coordinate singularities do not require a
boundary assertion about their signs. Hence \(v_\varepsilon\le0\).

The smooth heat kernel and its first derivatives on a fixed
positive-time compact set can be integrated against the weakly
convergent measures \(f_\varepsilon dH\). The integrals converge
uniformly. Thus \(\partial_\theta p_\tau(\theta)\le0\).
In fact the inequality is strict for \(0<\theta<\pi\). If the
derivative vanished at an interior point at positive time, apply
the strong maximum principle to \(-\partial_\theta p\ge0\) on
compact subintervals of \((0,\pi)\), where HR6 has smooth bounded
coefficients. Overlapping such subintervals shows that the
derivative vanished on all of \((0,\pi)\) at earlier positive
times. This would make \(p\) constant, contradicting its nonzero
\(\chi_1\) coefficient \(2e^{-3\tau/4}\) in HR4.

Consequently the maximum is attained exactly when \(u=w\), and
the minimum exactly when \(v=-w\). Their ratio is
\[
 R(\tau)=
 \frac{\displaystyle\sum_{n\ge1}n^2e^{-\tau(n^2-1)/4}}
      {\displaystyle\sum_{n\ge1}(-1)^{n-1}n^2e^{-\tau(n^2-1)/4}}.
                                                               \tag{HR7}
\]
The denominator is strictly positive by the preceding heat-kernel
argument. It is an alternating spectral expression, not a sum
whose terms may be discarded as though all were positive.

\subsection*{HR4. Image formula and both endpoint limits}

The Fourier coefficient at integer \(n\) of
\(\sum_{k\in\mathbb Z}e^{-(\theta+2\pi k)^2/\tau}\) is
\(\sqrt{\pi\tau}\,e^{-\tau n^2/4}/(2\pi)\). Indeed, unfold the
integral over \([0,2\pi]\) to \(\mathbb R\). The Gaussian Fourier
integral follows by differentiating that integral with respect to
the frequency, integrating by parts, and solving
\(F'(s)=-\tau sF(s)/2\), with \(F(0)=\sqrt{\pi\tau}\).
The latter Gaussian integral follows by squaring it and using
polar coordinates. Thus
\[
 \sum_{n\in\mathbb Z}e^{-\tau n^2/4}e^{in\theta}
 =\frac{2\sqrt\pi}{\sqrt\tau}
       \sum_{k\in\mathbb Z}e^{-(\theta+2\pi k)^2/\tau}.
                                                               \tag{HR8}
\]
Both sides and all fixed derivatives converge absolutely. Differentiate
HR8, pair \(n\) with \(-n\), and insert the result in HR4 to obtain
\[
 p_\tau(\theta)=A_\tau
  \frac{S_\tau(\theta)}{\sin\theta},\qquad
 A_\tau=\frac{2\sqrt\pi e^{\tau/4}}{\tau^{3/2}},\qquad
 S_\tau(\theta)=\sum_{k\in\mathbb Z}
      (\theta+2\pi k)e^{-(\theta+2\pi k)^2/\tau}.              \tag{HR9}
\]
For \(\theta=0\), the terms \(k\) and \(-k\) cancel in \(S\).
For \(\theta=\pi\), pair \(k\) with \(-k-1\). Thus both endpoint
values of \(S\) are zero. Its derivative is
\[
 S_\tau'(\theta)=\sum_{k\in\mathbb Z}
 \left(1-\frac{2(\theta+2\pi k)^2}{\tau}\right)
                       e^{-(\theta+2\pi k)^2/\tau}.
\]
L'Hopital's rule at the two simple zeros of \(\sin\theta\) gives
the full limits, including the negative sign \(\cos\pi=-1\):
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 \frac{p_\tau(0)}{A_\tau}
 &=1+2\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}
       \left(1-\frac{8\pi^2k^2}{\tau}\right)e^{-4\pi^2k^2/\tau},\\
 \frac{p_\tau(\pi)}{A_\tau}
 &=2\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}
       \left(\frac{2(2k+1)^2\pi^2}{\tau}-1\right)
                                   e^{-(2k+1)^2\pi^2/\tau}.
 \end{aligned}                                                  \tag{HR10}
\]
For \(0<\tau\le\pi^2\), every correction in the first line is
negative, and every summand in the second line is positive.
The first line is therefore strictly less than one. Retaining
only the \(k=0\) summand of the second line yields
\[
 1<R(\tau)<
  \frac{e^{\pi^2/\tau}}{2(2\pi^2/\tau-1)}
  <e^{\pi^2/\tau}\qquad(0<\tau\le\pi^2).                    \tag{HR11}
\]
The left inequality follows from strict radial decrease; the
right inequality uses \(2(2\pi^2/\tau-1)\ge2\).

\subsection*{HR5. A separate rigorous large-time estimate}

Let
\[
 \delta(\tau)=\sum_{n=2}^{\infty}n^2e^{-\tau(n^2-1)/4}.
                                                               \tag{HR12}
\]
By HR5, \(|p_\tau(\theta)-1|\le\delta(\tau)\). For \(n=j+2\),
\(j\ge0\), one has
\[
 (j+2)^2\le4\,4^j,\qquad (j+2)^2-1\ge3+5j.
\]
The first inequality follows from \(j+2\le2^{j+1}\), whose
induction step is \(j+3\le2(j+2)\). The second inequality is
\(j(j-1)\ge0\) for integer \(j\ge0\). Hence, whenever the
displayed geometric ratio is below one,
\[
 \delta(\tau)\le
 D(\tau):=\frac{4e^{-3\tau/4}}{1-4e^{-5\tau/4}}.              \tag{HR13}
\]
For \(\tau\ge4\), this denominator is positive and
\[
 D(4)<\frac{1/4}{3/4}=\frac13.
\]
Here \(e^3>16\) follows already from
\(1+3+3^2/2+3^3/6+3^4/24=131/8>16\); also \(e^5>e^3>16\).
Every function \(\tau e^{-\tau(n^2-1)/4}\), \(n\ge2\), is
strictly decreasing for \(\tau\ge4\), because its derivative has
the sign of \(1-\tau(n^2-1)/4<0\). Thus
\[
 \delta(\tau)<\frac13,\qquad
 \tau\delta(\tau)\le4\delta(4)<\frac43\qquad(\tau\ge4).
\]
It follows that
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 \log R(\tau)
 &\le\log\frac{1+\delta(\tau)}{1-\delta(\tau)}\\
 &=\int_{-\delta(\tau)}^{\delta(\tau)}\frac{ds}{1+s}
 \le\frac{2\delta(\tau)}{1-\delta(\tau)}
 <3\delta(\tau)<\frac4\tau<\frac{\pi^2}\tau,
 \qquad \tau\ge4.
 \end{aligned}
\tag{HR14}
\]
Since \(4<\pi^2\), HR11 and HR14 cover every \(\tau>0\),
proving HR1. The small-time image series is not used outside
the range where its signs establish HR11.

\subsection*{HR6. Sharp exponential coefficient and the antipodal asymptotic}

In HR10, the omitted numerator terms are
\(O(\tau^{-1}e^{-4\pi^2/\tau})\) as \(\tau\downarrow0\).
To see this with a summable majorant, put \(x=\pi^2/\tau\ge1\),
factor out \(xe^{-4x}\), and bound the remaining series by a
constant times \(\sum_{k\ge1}(1+k^2)e^{-4(k^2-1)}\).
Similarly, the denominator terms with \(k\ge1\), relative to
the \(k=0\) term, are \(O(e^{-8\pi^2/\tau})\): factor out the
first exponent, use \(2x-1\ge x\), and dominate the remaining
sum by a constant times
\(\sum_{k\ge1}(2k+1)^2e^{-((2k+1)^2-9)}\).
Therefore the full endpoint asymptotics are
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 p_\tau(0)&=\frac{2\sqrt\pi e^{\tau/4}}{\tau^{3/2}}
       [1+O(\tau^{-1}e^{-4\pi^2/\tau})],\\
 p_\tau(\pi)&=\frac{2\sqrt\pi e^{\tau/4}}{\tau^{3/2}}
       2(2\pi^2/\tau-1)e^{-\pi^2/\tau}
       [1+O(e^{-8\pi^2/\tau})],\\
 R(\tau)&=\frac{e^{\pi^2/\tau}}{2(2\pi^2/\tau-1)}
       [1+O(\tau^{-1}e^{-4\pi^2/\tau})],\\
 \lim_{\tau\downarrow0}\tau\log R(\tau)&=\pi^2.
 \end{aligned}                                                  \tag{HR15}
\]
The last equality proves the optimality assertion following HR1:
any all-time exponent \(C/\tau\) requires
\(C\ge\lim_{\tau\downarrow0}\tau\log R(\tau)=\pi^2\).
It does not assert equality in HR1 at a positive time.

At large time, retaining the \(n=1,2,3\) terms in HR7 and bounding
the remainder by HR13 with its first terms removed gives
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 p_\tau(0)&=1+4e^{-3\tau/4}+9e^{-2\tau}+O(e^{-15\tau/4}),\\
 p_\tau(\pi)&=1-4e^{-3\tau/4}+9e^{-2\tau}+O(e^{-15\tau/4}),\\
 \log R(\tau)&=8e^{-3\tau/4}+O(e^{-9\tau/4}).
 \end{aligned}                                                  \tag{HR16}
\]
For the last line, set \(a=4e^{-3\tau/4}\),
\(b=9e^{-2\tau}\); expand
\(\log(1+a+b+\cdots)-\log(1-a+b+\cdots)\).
The quadratic even term cancels, the cubic term is
\(O(a^3)\), and \(ab=O(e^{-11\tau/4})\). All these bounds have
strictly smaller order than the displayed leading term.

\subsection*{HR7. The original one-edge Feynman–Kac consequence}

Retain the complete finite-volume Hamiltonian and all constants:
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 H&=-\kappa\sum_f\Delta_f+V,\qquad
 \kappa=2g^2/a,\quad b_{\rm mag}=1/(2g^2a),\quad a,g>0,\\
 V&=b_{\rm mag}\sum_p(2-\operatorname{tr}U_p),\qquad
 \xi=b_{\rm mag}/\kappa=1/(4g^4),\\
 H\psi&=E_0\psi,\qquad \psi>0,\qquad
 \int\psi^2\,dH^{\otimes N}=1.
 \end{aligned}                                                  \tag{HR17}
\]
Fix a physical link \(e\), write \(x=U_e\),
\(z=(U_f)_{f\ne e}\), and let \(r_e\) be the actual number of
incident faces. Split the original sum exactly into
\(V_e=b_{\rm mag}\sum_{p\ni e}(2-\operatorname{tr}U_p)\)
and \(V_{\bar e}=b_{\rm mag}\sum_{p\not\ni e}(2-\operatorname{tr}U_p)\).
Every \(SU(2)\) trace is in \([-2,2]\), so
\(0\le V_e\le4b_{\rm mag}r_e\). No scalar has been removed from
either potential or from \(E_0\).

Let independent link Brownian motions have generator
\(\kappa\sum_f\Delta_f\). The full eigenvalue equation and Ito's
formula give, for every physical time \(t>0\),
\[
 \psi(x,z)=e^{E_0t}\mathbb E_{x,z}
  \left[e^{-\int_0^tV(X_s,Z_s)ds}\psi(X_t,Z_t)\right].       \tag{HR18}
\]
Indeed the drift of the integrand with its time-dependent
exponential is \((E_0-V)\psi+\kappa\sum_f\Delta_f\psi=0\).
Compactness bounds the integrand on each finite time interval,
so the local martingale is a true martingale.

Condition on the complete outside path \(\zeta=(Z_s)_{0\le s\le t}\)
and define the common positive function
\[
 G_{t,z}(w)=\mathbb E_z^{\bar e}
 \left[e^{-\int_0^tV_{\bar e}(Z_s)ds}\psi(w,Z_t)\right].
\]
Independence before weighting, the bound on \(V_e\), and Tonelli's
theorem imply
\[
 e^{E_0t-4b_{\rm mag}r_et}P_{\kappa t}G_{t,z}(x)
 \le\psi(x,z)\le e^{E_0t}P_{\kappa t}G_{t,z}(x).             \tag{HR19}
\]
Thus the outside Brownian motion and every outside potential are
retained in the same \(G_{t,z}\). Dividing its positive integrals
gives the pointwise comparison
\[
 \frac{\psi(x,z)}{\psi(y,z)}
 \le e^{4b_{\rm mag}r_et}
       \sup_w\frac{p_{\kappa t}(x,w)}{p_{\kappa t}(y,w)}
 \le e^{4\beta\tau}R(\tau),\qquad
 \beta=r_e\xi,\quad \tau=\kappa t.                         \tag{HR20}
\]
The \(E_0\) factors cancel in this ratio of the full inequalities;
the scalar terms have not been omitted before obtaining HR19.
Consequently the exact consequence of this kernel comparison is
\[
 \sup_z\operatorname{osc}_x\log\psi(x,z)
 \le\inf_{\tau>0}\{4\beta\tau+\log R(\tau)\}
 \le4\pi\sqrt\beta.                                       \tag{HR21}
\]
For \(\beta>0\), the last bound follows by substituting
\(\tau=\pi/(2\sqrt\beta)\) into HR1. In original physical time,
\[
 t=\frac{\pi}{2\kappa\sqrt{r_e\xi}}
   =\frac{\pi a}{2\sqrt{r_e}}\qquad(r_e>0).                 \tag{HR22}
\]
The last equality uses exactly \(\kappa=2g^2/a\) and
\(\xi=1/(4g^4)\). If \(r_e=0\), let \(\tau\to\infty\) in
HR20; HR16 gives ratio at most one in either direction, so the
vacuum is independent of that link and HR21 gives zero.

For the physical density \(\rho_{\rm full}=\psi^2\), HR21 gives
\(\sup_z\operatorname{osc}_x\log\rho_{\rm full}\le8\pi\sqrt{r_e\xi}\).
For its conditional probability density
\(h_z(x)=\psi(x,z)^2/\int\psi(y,z)^2dH(y)\), probability Haar
measure and the ratio bound therefore give, pointwise,
\[
 e^{-8\pi\sqrt{r_e\xi}}\le h_z(x)
 \le e^{8\pi\sqrt{r_e\xi}}.                                \tag{HR23}
\]
To verify this last step, integrate
\(\psi(y,z)^2\le e^{8\pi\sqrt{r_e\xi}}\psi(x,z)^2\)
and its reverse inequality over \(y\); both Haar integrals have
total mass one.

\subsection*{HR8. Source comparison and the source's unused second image formula}

Fabrice Baudoin and Michel Bonnefont, *The subelliptic heat kernel
on SU(2): Representations, Asymptotics and Gradient bounds*,
\href{https://arxiv.org/src/0802.3320v1}{original author TeX, arXiv:0802.3320v1},
Section 2 and Section 3.2, formula `spectralQ` and the first image
formula, supply an independent primary-source comparison for HR4
and HR9. The original author source was read at lines 155–200 and
414–496; its section structure was inspected. No PDF was used.
The source's basis is
\(X=-2T_2\), \(Y=-2T_1\), \(Z=-2T_3\), whence its complete
Riemannian Laplacian is exactly \(4\Delta\). The precise map is
\[
 p_\tau(\theta)=q_{\tau/4}(\cos\theta),\qquad
 \frac{\sqrt\pi e^{\tau/4}}{4(\tau/4)^{3/2}}
 =\frac{2\sqrt\pi e^{\tau/4}}{\tau^{3/2}}.                  \tag{HR24}
\]
The source's two-field subelliptic kernel is a different object
and is not used anywhere in HR1–HR23.

The immediately following, unused second image expression in
this v1 TeX has a sign error. Pairing the terms \(k\) and \(-k\)
in HR9 gives exactly, for \(0<\theta<\pi\),
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 S_\tau(\theta)
 =\theta e^{-\theta^2/\tau}
 \bigg[1+2\sum_{k\ge1}e^{-4\pi^2k^2/\tau}
 \bigg\{\cosh\frac{4\pi k\theta}{\tau}
 -\frac{2\pi k}{\theta}\sinh\frac{4\pi k\theta}{\tau}\bigg\}\bigg].
 \end{aligned}                                                  \tag{HR25}
\]
Indeed the paired unfactored terms are
\(e^{-\theta^2/\tau-4\pi^2k^2/\tau}
[(\theta+2\pi k)e^{-4\pi k\theta/\tau}
+(\theta-2\pi k)e^{4\pi k\theta/\tau}]\).
The coefficient of the hyperbolic sine is therefore negative.
The v1 source displays a plus sign in that second expression.
HR9 uses its first expression, independently derived in HR8–HR9;
none of HR1–HR24 uses the erroneous rewrite. This is a bounded
source correction to v1, with no assertion about another edition.

\subsection*{HR9. Scope and reproducibility}

`verify\_heat\_ratio.py` evaluates both complete series at high
precision on their stable ranges, checks overlapping evaluations,
checks the two endpoint limits and the exact Haar integral, and
produces `HEAT\_RATIO\_CHECKS.json`. Its finite samples support the
calculation; HR2–HR25 are the proof. The two original-source forms
retain the full multiplier and every image or spectral term in
the mathematical statements. Truncation is confined to the
numerical checks and explicitly recorded there.

`HEAT\_RATIO\_FIGURE.png` illustrates the original probability-Haar
kernel against the physical distance \(d=2\theta\), and the exact
ratio against HR1. The plotted curves are numerical samples of
HR4, HR9 and HR10; their inequalities are proved in HR11–HR14.
The geometry is already described exactly by HR2, so a three-dimensional
scene would add no mathematical information to these plots.

This note establishes the heat-ratio and one-edge oscillation
claims. It does not assert a continuum mass gap, a sign for a
complete spectral endpoint, or a score bound derived from
additional stochastic differentiation.


\begin{figure}[htbp]\centering\includegraphics[width=\linewidth,height=.72\textheight,keepaspectratio]{HEAT_RATIO_FIGURE.png}\caption{The original radius-two heat kernel and the sharp all-time ratio bound, HR2–HR16. Curves are numerical evaluations of the retained complete series.}\end{figure}
\clearpage
\section*{A square-root local score for the original vacuum}

The complete original finite-box vacuum satisfies the volume-independent
bound
\[
 \boxed{\|\nabla_e\log\psi^2\|_\infty
 \le \min\{4r_e\xi,\ C_*\sqrt{r_e\xi}\},\qquad
 C_*=2+2\sqrt{4+2\pi}.}
 \tag{LS1}
\]
Here the metric is the original radius-two \(SU(2)\) metric,
\(\xi=1/(4g^4)\), and \(r_e\) is the actual number of incident
faces of the physical link \(e\). Every original potential term and
all outside-link dynamics remain in the proof. The argument first
proves the proposed Feynman–Kac comparison, then bounds a one-link
logarithmic oscillation, and finally differentiates an exact
terminal-point-preserving path transformation. That last step
retains the edge displacement in the potential term.

The accepted
\href{https://github.com/KokunoYumeto/yang-mills-interacting-workbench/blob/442786391ab942ad0c829c0ebf32e952585915e0/yang-mills/continuations/20261011-central-signs-shrinking-window/POLYNOMIAL_SCORE_AND_ORIGINAL_ENDPOINT.md}{PS1–PS76 proof}
is unchanged. Section 7 below applies (LS1) to its complete
fourteen-dimensional polynomial bound and to the exact original
boundary derivative, with all affine terms and physical factors.
The all-row positive endpoint correction on the prescribed path
then has a proved lower-bound function of order \(j^{-27}\).

\subsection*{1. Original operator, local splitting and the first score bound}

Let the original open box have \(L\ge2\),
\(N=3(2L)(2L+1)^2\) physical links and
\(M=12L^2(2L+1)\) elementary faces. Retain
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 H&=\kappa H_0+V,\qquad
 H_0=-\sum_{f,\alpha}X_{f,\alpha}^2,\qquad
 V=b_{\rm mag}\sum_p(2-\operatorname{tr}U_p),\\
 \kappa&={2g^2\over a},\qquad
 b_{\rm mag}={1\over2g^2a},\qquad
 \xi={b_{\rm mag}\over\kappa}={1\over4g^4},\qquad a,g>0,\\
 H\psi&=E_0\psi,\qquad \psi>0,\qquad \|\psi\|_{L^2(dU)}=1,
 \qquad \rho_{\rm full}=\psi^2.
 \end{aligned}
 \tag{LS2}
\]
The original fields are generated by
