\section*{Independent ordered Wilson-action derivation and source review}

This review checks OW1–OW23, including the preceding subsequent zero-circulation and positive-defect strengthenings. It reads Sourav Chatterjee's original author TeX, arXiv:1602.01222v6, especially lines 230–248, 781–811 and 1756–1888. It neither reads nor applies the full free-energy proof. The exact downloaded source is identified in ACTUAL\_LIMIT\_SOURCE\_USE\_LEDGER.json. The complete original source version and reading coverage are retained in ACTUAL\_LIMIT\_SOURCE\_USE\_LEDGER.json.

The coefficients, signs, global integral remainders, exponential chart, full CW configuration potential, and original-path constants of OW1–OW20 are correct in their stated scopes. The original author source has two printed path defects; their correction is included in OW1–OW4. The complete derivations and two further bounds follow.

\subsection*{1. What the author source actually prints}

Write \(z=x\), \(u=x+e_j\), \(v=x+e_j+e_k\), \(w=x+e_k\). The original Wilson face in author lines 235–238 is the ordered cycle \(z,u,v,w,z\). However, its printed additive definition at lines 1761–1762 is
\[
 H(z,u)+H(u,v)+H(v,u)+H(u,z)=0.
 \tag{UR1}
\]
This is a backtracking path and vanishes by the stipulated antisymmetry of every reverse edge. Its printed Taylor product at line 1832 follows the same backtracking sequence. In contrast, the four matrices at lines 1848–1851 use the genuine face:
\[
 H_1=H(z,u),\quad H_2=H(u,v),\quad
 H_3=H(v,w),\quad H_4=H(w,z).
 \tag{UR2}
\]
Thus the intended map is determined internally by the author's original Wilson definition and the later four matrices. Both printed occurrences of the penultimate vertex \(u\) must be \(w\). This is a correction of the written path, not a commutation or approximation.

For an exact counterexample to the lemma with its literal printed additive definition, set \(H(z,u)=\operatorname{diag}(x,-x)\), let all other positive face matrices be zero, and impose the original reverse-edge rule. The genuine Wilson defect is \(2-2\cos x\), while (UR1) is zero. The maximum Hilbert–Schmidt edge norm is \(\eta=\sqrt2|x|\). Consequently
\[
 \frac{2-2\cos x}{\eta^3}
 \sim\frac{1}{2\sqrt2|x|}\longrightarrow+\infty
       \quad(x\longrightarrow0,\ x\ne0).
 \tag{UR3}
\]
The displayed source lemma therefore cannot hold with the backtracking definition. This identifies a local written defect; it does not assert failure of the intended theorem or of the paper's main free-energy result.

There is also the separate nonunitarity issue at lines 1843–1844. For any square matrix \(R\),
\[
 \frac12\|I-R\|_{\rm HS}^2
 =r-\operatorname{Re}\operatorname{Tr}R
          +\frac12\operatorname{Tr}(R^*R-I).
 \tag{UR4}
\]
For the literal backtracking Taylor product in the example above,
\(R_{\rm back}=(1+x^4/4)I\). Hence
\(\phi(R_{\rm back})=-x^4/2\) and
\(\frac12\|I-R_{\rm back}\|_{\rm HS}^2=x^8/16\).
The missing defect is \(x^4/2+x^8/16\). This verifies the nonunitarity criticism even before the path is repaired. After the path is repaired, the intended four-factor example \(R=I+iH_1-H_1^2/2\) gives precisely OW3's different defect \(x^4/4\).

\subsection*{2. Exact positive defect for every ordered quadratic Taylor product}

Set \(Q_a=I+iH_a-H_a^2/2\) for the original four Hermitian matrices, and \(R=Q_1Q_2Q_3Q_4\), with no rearrangement. Let \(S_a=Q_{a+1}\cdots Q_4\), where \(S_4=I\). Direct multiplication gives \(Q_a^*Q_a=I+H_a^4/4\). Peeling off the first factor and iterating therefore proves
\[
 R^*R-I=\frac14\sum_{a=1}^4S_a^*H_a^4S_a,\qquad
 \Delta(R):=\frac12\operatorname{Tr}(R^*R-I)
     =\frac18\sum_{a=1}^4\|H_a^2S_a\|_{\rm HS}^2\ge0.
 \tag{UR5}
\]
Every suffix is in its original order. In particular every quadratic factor and their full product are expansive in the sense \(R^*R\ge I\). This is the exact object measured by the source's failed unitary identity.

For \(\|H_a\|_{\rm HS}\le\eta\), the elementary eigenvalue bounds give
\(\|H_a^2\|_{\rm HS}^2=\operatorname{Tr}H_a^4\le\eta^4\) and
\(\|Q_a\|_{\rm op}^2\le1+\eta^4/4\). Inserting them individually in (UR5), then summing the four suffix lengths, proves
\[
 0\le\Delta(R)\le
 \frac{\eta^4}{8}\sum_{k=0}^3(1+\eta^4/4)^k
 =\frac12[(1+\eta^4/4)^4-1].
 \tag{UR6}
\]
The final equality holds by continuity at \(\eta=0\). For a unitary \(P\), the exact repaired signed equation is
\[
 \phi(P)-\phi(R)
 =\tfrac12(\|I-P\|_{\rm HS}^2-\|I-R\|_{\rm HS}^2)+\Delta(R).
\]
Each \(Q_a\) is invertible because \(Q_a^*Q_a\ge I\). Thus each suffix is invertible, and the sum in (UR5) vanishes exactly when all four \(H_a\) are zero. This also checks the preceding final characterization in OW23. Thus, once the path is repaired, the additional defect is nonnegative and fourth order at small radius. This confirms that the intended cubic bound survives. OW4's direct trace bound is also valid and needs none of this intermediate unitary reasoning.

\subsection*{3. Ordered coefficients and a dimension-independent remainder bound}

Retain \(P(t)=\prod_{a=1}^4e^{itH_a}\), in order, and \(\Phi(t)=r-\operatorname{Re}\operatorname{Tr}P(t)\). Differentiating each exponential gives exactly the multi-index sum OW5. The scalar multinomial formula proves OW6. Selecting total derivative degrees one, two and three gives
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 \Phi(0)&=\Phi'(0)=0,\\
 \frac{\Phi''(0)}2&=\frac12\operatorname{Tr}\Big(\sum_aH_a\Big)^2,\\
 \frac{\Phi'''(0)}6&=-\sum_{a<b<c}
                     \operatorname{Im}\operatorname{Tr}(H_aH_bH_c).
 \end{aligned}\tag{UR7}
\]
For the last line, \(P\)'s cubic coefficient is \(-iB_3\) with exactly OW7's full ordered polynomial. Hermitian-cube and repeated-factor traces are real; \(\operatorname{Re}(-iz)=\operatorname{Im}z\), and the minus sign in \(\Phi=r-\operatorname{Re}\operatorname{Tr}P\) gives the displayed negative sign. Four integrations of the fourth derivative give the exact OW8 remainder and the factor \(1/24\); three integrations give OW9 and \(1/6\). These bounds hold globally for all four Hermitian matrices.

There is a useful improvement for the general matrix statement. Define, retaining both norms,
\[
 q_{\rm op}=\sum_a\|H_a\|_{\rm op},\qquad
 q_{\rm HS}=\sum_a\|H_a\|_{\rm HS}.
\]
For \(k\ge2\), in each ordered differentiated product choose two of its \(k\) differentiated factors and use their Hilbert–Schmidt norms; use operator norms for the other \(k-2\) factors and all intervening unitary matrices. The inequality needed is
\(|\operatorname{Tr}(AUBV)|\le\|A\|_{\rm HS}\|B\|_{\rm HS}\|U\|_{\rm op}\|V\|_{\rm op}\), proved by the Hilbert–Schmidt inner-product inequality and submultiplicativity. Cyclicity places the chosen two factors in this form without interchanging any factors.

Index the product rule by its \(k\) derivative labels \(a_1,\ldots,a_k\), which gives each multi-index its correct multiplicity. Choose derivative labels one and two for the two Hilbert–Schmidt factors in every term. Summing their bounds now factors exactly, yielding
\[
 |\Phi^{(k)}(t)|
 \le\min\{r q_{\rm op}^k,\ q_{\rm HS}^2q_{\rm op}^{k-2}\},
 \qquad k\ge2,\quad t\in\mathbb R.
 \tag{UR8}
\]
If all four original Hilbert–Schmidt norms are at most \(\eta\), the quadratic remainder is bounded by \(32\eta^3/3\), independently of the matrix size, rather than \(32r\eta^3/3\). The fourth remainder is bounded by \(q_{\rm HS}^2q_{\rm op}^2/24\). In the traceless \(2\times2\) programme specialization, \(q_{\rm HS}=\sqrt2q_{\rm op}\), so this reproduces, and does not improve, OW14–OW15's stated constants.

\subsection*{4. Original SU(2) signs and the exact zero-circulation sector}

Retain \(T_\alpha=-i\sigma_\alpha/2\), \(X(y)=\sum y^\alpha T_\alpha\), and \(H(y)=-y\cdot\sigma/2\). Thus \(iH(y)=X(y)\). Pauli multiplication directly gives
\[
 \operatorname{Tr}(H(y)H(z))=y\cdot z/2,\quad
 \operatorname{Tr}(H(y)H(z)H(w))=-i\,y\cdot(z\times w)/4.
 \tag{UR9}
\]
This verifies OW13 and the positive cubic coefficient in OW14. Since \(q_{\rm op}=\sum_a|y_a|/2\), the two remainder constants are
\(2q_{\rm op}^4/24=(\sum_a|y_a|)^4/192\) and
\(2q_{\rm op}^3/6=(\sum_a|y_a|)^3/24\).

Keep the original \(C=\sum_{a=1}^4y_a\), and put \(B=\sum_{a<b}y_a\times y_b\), in that order. Expanding \(C\cdot B\), contributions with a repeated index vanish. For each \(a<b<c\), its three contributions have signs \(+,-,+\), so
\[
 \sum_{a<b<c}y_a\cdot(y_b\times y_c)=C\cdot B.
 \tag{UR10}
\]
Under whole-face reversal \(y'_a=-y_{5-a}\), one has \(C'=-C\) and \(B'=-B\), preserving this scalar. A transposition of only two entries is different and changes the ordered triple sign in OW18. Multiplying the three original exponentials in OW18 gives scalar quaternion parts \(\cos^3(t/2)\mp\sin^3(t/2)\); their defects are exactly the two displayed expressions there.

On the precise subspace \(C=0\), set \(P(t)=\prod_ae^{tX(y_a)}\). Then \(P'(0)=0\). Expanding the second derivative, and using
\((\sum_aX_a)^2=0\), gives
\[
 P''(0)=\sum_aX_a^2+2\sum_{a<b}X_aX_b
       =\sum_{a<b}[X_a,X_b]=X(B).
 \tag{UR11}
\]
The original Lie-bracket convention is \([X(y),X(z)]=X(y\times z)\), as follows from the same Pauli identity. Hence \(P(t)=I+t^2X(B)/2+O(t^3)\). Because the full product is unitary, its exact defect is \(\frac12\|I-P(t)\|_{\rm HS}^2\). The fourth coefficient is therefore
\(\frac18\|X(B)\|_{\rm HS}^2=|B|^2/16\), with all lower coefficients zero. The exact fifth-order Taylor identity is
\[
 D(t)=\frac{t^4|B|^2}{16}
       +\frac1{24}\int_0^t(t-u)^4D^{(5)}(u)\,du,\qquad
 \left|D(t)-\frac{t^4|B|^2}{16}\right|
       \le\frac{|t|^5(\sum_a|y_a|)^5}{1920}.
 \tag{UR12}
\]
For either sign of \(t\), integrate the absolute fifth-derivative bound
\(2q_{\rm op}^5\) over the interval between zero and \(t\). This proves the preceding strengthening with its exact sign, norm, and constant. It is a deterministic statement on the specified original-coordinate subspace; it asserts no probability or vacuum concentration there.

If \(B=0\) as well, the matrix product itself starts at degree three. Its third-order integral bound gives the additional fully proved estimate
\[
 0\le D(t)\le\frac{|t|^6q_{\rm op}^6}{36}
      =\frac{|t|^6(\sum_a|y_a|)^6}{2304}.
 \tag{UR13}
\]
Indeed \(\|P(t)-I\|_{\rm op}\le|t|^3q_{\rm op}^3/6\), and
\(\frac12\|P(t)-I\|_{\rm HS}^2\le\|P(t)-I\|_{\rm op}^2\) for two-dimensional matrices. This follows from the exact two vanishing derivative conditions, not from replacing the original product by a logarithmic approximation.

\subsection*{5. Full CW configuration and the unchanged path}

The retained CW42 field has commuting link matrices
\(U_1=I\), \(U_2(i,k,l)=e^{\pi iT/m}\),
\(U_3(i,k,l)=e^{\pi kT/m}\).
There are \(m^2n\) faces of each orientation, including all boundary faces. The full 12 and 23 products are \(e^{\pi T/m}\); every 13 product is \(I\). The face half-angle is \(\alpha=\pi/(2m)\). This gives exactly
\[
 W=4m^2n(1-\cos\alpha),\quad
 W_{\rm quad}=2m^2n\alpha^2,\quad
 -m^2n\alpha^4/6\le W-W_{\rm quad}\le0.
 \tag{UR14}
\]
Both scalar inequalities hold for all real \(\alpha\): use the second-order and fourth-order cosine integral bounds. Substituting the unchanged
\(m=2j^4\), \(n=2j^4+1\), \(b=10000j^2/\kappa_*\) gives
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 bW_{\rm quad}&=5000\pi^2j^2(2j^4+1)/\kappa_*,\\
 -10000\pi^4j^2(2j^4+1)/(384\kappa_*j^8)
        &\le b(W-W_{\rm quad})\le0,\\
 bW/j^6&\longrightarrow10000\pi^2/\kappa_*,\\
 j^2b(W-W_{\rm quad})&\longrightarrow-625\pi^4/(12\kappa_*).
 \end{aligned}\tag{UR15}
\]
The sixth cosine remainder contributes at most a constant times
\(j^2b\,m^2n\,\alpha^6=O(j^{-8})\) in the last limit, so it vanishes. This also verifies the precise full-boundary constants in OW20. They are configuration values, not actual-vacuum expectations.

The chart and expectation claims require no change to the Hamiltonian. The map
\(x\mapsto e^{X(x)}\) is injective for \(|x|<2\pi\), with image
\(SU(2)\setminus\{-I\}\), by the radius-two sine/cosine formula. The principal representatives are measurable and bounded, and their exceptional link sets are Haar-null and therefore actual-vacuum-null. Thus every term in OW17 is integrable with that choice. More generally the phrase “any selected representatives” in its expectation statement is read with measurability and finite fourth moments; arbitrary unbounded or nonmeasurable selections need not define those expectations. This qualification does not affect the explicitly supplied principal-chart construction.

No further result about the author's Euclidean Wilson Gibbs measure or the programme's Hamiltonian vacuum is used. The source corrections concern exact printed paths and a matrix identity, and the strengthening concerns ordered deterministic products only.


\begin{figure}[htbp]\centering\includegraphics[width=\linewidth,height=.72\textheight,keepaspectratio]{ORDERED_WILSON_REVIEW.png}\caption{The dimension-independent derivative bound and the exact sixth-order product estimate; UR8 and UR13.}\end{figure}

The reproducible source draw\_ordered\_review.py records the exact norms and coordinate locus. The complete receiving calculation is \href{https://github.com/KokunoYumeto/yang-mills-interacting-workbench/blob/main/yang-mills/continuations/20261011-central-signs-shrinking-window/ORDERED_WILSON_ACTION.md}{OW1–OW25}.
\clearpage
\section*{Continuous time histories of the actual Wilson observables}

This note strengthens ASL38 from mean-square continuity to a continuous version with every local Hölder exponent below one half, for each specified countable family of fixed finite Wilson words. It uses the actual finite-volume ground-state process, with the original metric, full drift and coupling. The original growing physical loops and raw phase retain their full gradient constants; no uniform estimate for them is inferred.

The provider is \href{https://github.com/KokunoYumeto/yang-mills-interacting-workbench/blob/main/yang-mills/continuations/20261011-central-signs-shrinking-window/ACTUAL_SEMIGROUP_LIMIT.md}{ASL1–ASL66}, especially ASL3–ASL4, ASL23–ASL29, ASL33–ASL38 and ASL46–ASL66. The forward/backward identity is the symmetric Lyons–Zheng mechanism. The original author TeX of Masayoshi Takeda and Gerald Trutnau, \href{https://arxiv.org/abs/1007.3900v1}{arXiv:1007.3900v1}, Section 2, equation (1) and Remark (i), was read at source lines 228–263. Its general nonsymmetric theorem is not imported. The identity required here is derived below for the actual smooth compact diffusion. Their bibliography identifies T. J. Lyons and W. Zheng, *A crossing estimate for the canonical process on a Dirichlet space and a tightness result*, Astérisque 157–158 (1988), 249–271; that historical reference has not been read here. This is an application and complete specialized calculation, with no novelty claim.

\subsection*{1. The original reversible process}

Retain the path and all finite-volume objects:
\[
\begin{gathered}
 L_j=j^4,\quad m_j=2j^4,\quad n_j=2j^4+1,\quad
 a_j=(100j)^{-1},\quad g_j^2=\kappa_*/(200j),\\
 \xi_j=10000j^2/\kappa_*^2,\quad s_j=\kappa_*/j,\quad
 N_j=3m_jn_j^2,\quad M_j=3m_j^2n_j,\\
 H_j=\kappa_*K_j+\kappa_*\xi_j\sum_p(2-W_p),\qquad
 K_j=-\sum_{e,\alpha}X_{e,\alpha}^2,\\
 H_j\psi_j=E_j^{\rm vac}\psi_j,\quad \psi_j>0,\quad
 \|\psi_j\|_{L^2(dU_j)}=1,\quad A_j=H_j-E_j^{\rm vac}I,
 \quad d\mu_j=\psi_j^2dU_j.
\end{gathered}\tag{TR1}
\]
The configuration space is the full compact product of one radius-two SU(2) per positive edge of the open box, including every boundary edge. Haar measure is probability measure. The fields use exactly \(T_\alpha=-i\sigma_\alpha/2\). The actual Markov generator, obtained by the complete product rule and the vacuum equation, is
\[
 \mathcal L_j f=-\psi_j^{-1}A_j(\psi_j f)
 =\kappa_*\sum_{e,\alpha}X_{e,\alpha}^2f
   +2\kappa_*\sum_{e,\alpha}(X_{e,\alpha}\log\psi_j)X_{e,\alpha}f.
 \tag{TR2}
\]
Both the potential scalar and the vacuum energy in (TR1) cancel by that equation. The full nonconstant logarithmic drift in (TR2) remains.

At each finite j this smooth generator is realized by the Stratonovich equation with diffusion fields \(\sqrt{2\kappa_*}X_{e,\alpha}\) and drift \(2\kappa_*\sum(X_{e,\alpha}\log\psi_j)X_{e,\alpha}\). Smooth coefficients on the compact product give a global continuous diffusion: solve the usual local Lipschitz stochastic equations in charts up to exit, concatenate the solutions on the finite chart cover, and use compactness and bounded coefficients to exclude finite lifetime. This uses the foundational existence and uniqueness theorem for finite-dimensional smooth stochastic differential equations and Itô's formula. No uniform drift bound as j grows is used. Its generator agrees with (TR2); uniqueness of the smooth parabolic problem on the compact product identifies its semigroup with \(S_j(t)f=\psi_j^{-1}e^{-tA_j}(\psi_jf)\).

For smooth real f,g, integration by parts gives the exact identity
\[
 \int f\mathcal L_jg\,d\mu_j
 =-\kappa_*\int\sum_{e,\alpha}(X_{e,\alpha}f)(X_{e,\alpha}g)\,d\mu_j.
 \tag{TR3}
\]
The divergence contribution from differentiating \(\psi_j^2\) cancels precisely the drift in (TR2). Thus \(\mu_j\) is invariant and the Markov semigroup is self-adjoint in \(L^2(\mu_j)\). Start the diffusion \(U_j(t)\) in \(\mu_j\). Its stationary finite-time laws are invariant under time reversal: write their integrals as alternating multiplication and semigroup operators, take the adjoint, and use the real symmetric transition kernels. This proves the reversal property used below, rather than substituting Haar measure for the vacuum. Gauge-invariant observables have exactly the physical correlations of ASL33.

\subsection*{2. Exact cancellation of the two drift integrals}

Fix j, a real smooth gauge-invariant observable b, and a time interval [0,T]. Retain
\[
 D^2=\sup_U\sum_{e,\alpha}|X_{e,\alpha}b(U)|^2,
 \qquad c=2\kappa_*D^2.
 \tag{TR4}
\]
Itô's formula gives the forward martingale
\[
 M_t=b(U_j(t))-b(U_j(0))-\int_0^t\mathcal L_jb(U_j(r))\,dr,
 \quad
 \langle M\rangle_t=2\kappa_*\int_0^t|\nabla b(U_j(r))|^2dr\le ct.
 \tag{TR5}
\]
The reversed stationary path \(\widehat U_j(r)=U_j(T-r)\) has the same generator and law. In its own forward filtration its martingale is
\[
 N_r=b(U_j(T-r))-b(U_j(T))
       -\int_0^r\mathcal L_jb(U_j(T-v))\,dv,
 \qquad d\langle N\rangle_r\le c\,dr.
 \tag{TR6}
\]
The variables M and N need not be independent or adapted to a common forward filtration. No such property is used. Subtracting (TR6) at T and T-t, with the exact substitution r=T-v in its integral, gives
\[
 N_T-N_{T-t}=b(U_j(0))-b(U_j(t))-\int_0^t\mathcal L_jb(U_j(r))\,dr.
\]
Consequently every original drift contribution cancels in the exact identity
\[
 b(U_j(t))-b(U_j(0))
   =\tfrac12 M_t-\tfrac12(N_T-N_{T-t}),\qquad 0\le t\le T.
 \tag{TR7}
\]
This is the required map from the full drift process to two martingale increments. For an estimate at the endpoint of any interval of length h, use that interval as the reversal horizon: its increment is exactly \((M_h-N_h)/2\).

\subsection*{3. All even moments with explicit constants}

For a real continuous martingale starting at zero and satisfying \(d\langle M\rangle_t\le c\,dt\), Itô's formula for its even powers yields, for every integer p at least one,
\[
 \mathbb E|M_h|^{2p}\le (2p-1)!!\,c^p h^p.
 \tag{TR8}
\]
Here \((2p-1)!!=1\cdot3\cdots(2p-1)\), with the empty product one. To justify expectations, first stop at \(|M|=R\). The martingale integral then has zero mean. For p=1 the assertion follows from the bracket. Inductively,
\[
 \mathbb E M_{h\wedge\tau_R}^{2p}
 \le p(2p-1)c\int_0^h
      \mathbb E\bigl[\mathbf1_{r<\tau_R}M_r^{2p-2}\bigr]dr
 \le (2p-1)(2p-3)!!\,c^ph^p.
\]
The stopped lower moment is bounded by its inductive estimate; the indicator only decreases its nonnegative integrand. Fatou as R tends to infinity proves (TR8). Finite moments for the unstopped formula can then be obtained by the same localization. Stationarity, (TR7) with horizon h, and convexity of the even power now prove
\[
 \boxed{\quad
 \mathbb E|b(U_j(t))-b(U_j(u))|^{2p}
 \le (2p-1)!!(2\kappa_*D^2)^p|t-u|^p.
 \quad}\tag{TR9}
\]
Indeed \(|(x-y)/2|^{2p}\le(|x|^{2p}+|y|^{2p})/2\). Apply (TR8) to the two martingales separately; their dependence creates no omitted covariance assumption.

For an elementary face trace, ASL26 proves \(D^2\le4\) by differentiating its four distinct original oriented edges. Thus
\[
 \mathbb E|W_{p_j}(U_j(t))-W_{p_j}(U_j(u))|^{2p}
 \le (2p-1)!!(8\kappa_*)^p|t-u|^p.
 \tag{TR10}
\]
For p=1 this is the original ASL38 coefficient. For p=2 its coefficient is exactly \(192\kappa_*^2\). For a product of k face traces, including all repetitions and overlap derivatives, ASL28 gives \(D\le k2^k\); insert \(D^2\le k^24^k\) in (TR9). A real polynomial combination uses the sum of its absolute coefficients times the corresponding gradient bounds. Real and imaginary parts give the corresponding result for complex polynomial observables, without deleting cross terms from their definitions.

For a complete growing original loop, keep \(\nu_j=\sum_e m_{e,j}^2\) with its actual occurrence multiplicities. ASL40 gives \(D_j^2\le\nu_j\), so its proved coefficient is
\[
 (2p-1)!!(2\kappa_*\nu_j)^p.
 \tag{TR11}
\]
For the physical rectangles of ASL43, \(\nu_j/j\to200(r+s)\); this bound is not uniform. The raw phase keeps its full gradient and all beta and row factors as well. Neither observable is silently replaced by a divided or truncated version in (TR9).

\subsection*{4. Continuous versions and convergence on path space}

Take the actual limiting history state and a specified countable real Wilson family from ASL33–ASL38 and ASL64–ASL66. Every coordinate is bounded and its original moment difference is a polynomial of two time coordinates. Therefore its common limiting finite-dimensional laws satisfy exactly (TR9), with the same finite constant C for that coordinate and p:
\[
 \mathbb E|X(t)-X(u)|^{2p}\le C|t-u|^p,
 \quad C=(2p-1)!!(2\kappa_*D^2)^p.
 \tag{TR12}
\]
Here is a direct continuity proof that retains constants. Fix T>0 and \(0<\alpha<(p-1)/(2p)\). On the dyadic times \(t_{n,k}=kT2^{-n}\), put
\[
 H_{\alpha,T}=\sup_{n\ge0,\,0\le k<2^n}
  2^{n\alpha}|X(t_{n,k+1})-X(t_{n,k})|,\quad
 d=p-1-2p\alpha>0.
\]
The union bound and (TR12), with every one of the \(2^n\) edges retained, give
\[
 \mathbb P(H_{\alpha,T}>R)
 \le\frac{CT^p}{R^{2p}(1-2^{-d})},\qquad R>0.
 \tag{TR13}
\]
Thus H is finite almost surely. For two dyadic times at separation at most \(T2^{-n}\), approximate both from below by level-n grid points. The grid points are equal or adjacent; their increment is at most \(H2^{-n\alpha}\). Each finer chain contributes at most \(H\sum_{k>n}2^{-k\alpha}\). Choose n with \(T2^{-n-1}<|t-u|\le T2^{-n}\). Summing the two chains proves the explicit bound
\[
 |X(t)-X(u)|\le C_\alpha H_{\alpha,T}
       (|t-u|/T)^\alpha,\qquad
 C_\alpha=\frac{2^{\alpha+1}}{1-2^{-\alpha}}.
 \tag{TR14}
\]
The displayed constant bounds \(2^\alpha[1+2/(2^\alpha-1)]\); it is not an asserted sharp constant. The dyadic function therefore has a unique continuous extension. For any fixed real t, dyadic approximants converge to the original X(t) in probability by (TR12), so the extension agrees almost surely with X(t). This proves a modification preserving all finite-dimensional distributions. Apply the argument to integer bounded time intervals, a countable sequence of exponents increasing to one half with p chosen sufficiently large, and the countably many chosen coordinates. Their common probability-one intersection gives simultaneous locally Hölder paths for every exponent strictly below one half. The extensions agree on overlapping intervals by agreement on the rational times.

The same constants apply before taking the limit. For each coordinate on each compact time interval, (TR13)–(TR14), together with its uniform value bound, provide compact sets of continuous paths carrying uniformly high probability by Arzelà–Ascoli. For countably many coordinates and time intervals, choose the individual error budgets with summable total. Their product compact set gives tightness on the countable product of continuous path spaces with uniform convergence on compact time intervals. The usual tightness theorem for probability measures on complete separable metric spaces yields subsequential weak limits. Evaluation at any finite set of times is continuous there, and ASL64–ASL66 already identify all such finite-time laws on one ordinary subsequence. Rational-time cylinder sets generate this path-space Borel algebra, so two weak limits with those laws coincide. Hence the entire selected ASL subsequence converges weakly on this path space, rather than only at individual times.

Original polynomial trace relations hold at each finite set of rational times, pass to the limiting laws, and then hold at all times for the continuous versions. Reflection positivity is unchanged because the finite-time correlations are unchanged. This proves temporal continuity for the stated observable family. It supplies no spatial derivative, differentiability in time, local four-dimensional field, or identification of the intended interacting theory.

\subsection*{5. Orientation-average collapse on whole time intervals}

Retain the exact original orientation average and its ASL constants:
\[
 \overline W_{de,j}=(K_j^0)^{-1}\sum_{p:\operatorname{type}p=de}W_p,
 \quad K_j^0=m_j^2n_j,\qquad
 \epsilon_j=\min\{2,(3\kappa_*/100j)\sqrt{n_j/m_j}\},
\]
\[
 \mathbb E(2-\overline W_{de,j}(U_j(0)))^2\le4\epsilon_j,
 \quad D_j^2\le\frac{4m_jn_j(2m_j-1)}{(K_j^0)^2}\le\frac8{K_j^0}.
 \tag{TR15}
\]
The forward/backward identity also controls the maximum over the whole interval. With \(M_*\) and \(N_*\) the absolute suprema on [0,T], (TR7) gives
\(\sup_t|b(U_j(t))-b(U_j(0))|\le M_*/2+N_*\).
The L² martingale maximal inequality and (TR5)–(TR6) give \(\mathbb EM_*^2,\mathbb EN_*^2\le4cT\). To recall that inequality, the stopping-time bound \(\lambda\mathbb P(M_*>\lambda)\le\mathbb E[|M_T|\mathbf1_{M_*>\lambda}]\), after division by \(\lambda\) and integration against \(2\lambda\,d\lambda\), gives \(\mathbb EM_*^2\le2\mathbb E|M_T|M_*\); Cauchy–Schwarz proves the coefficient four, with localization if needed. Therefore
\[
 \mathbb E\sup_{0\le t\le T}|b(U_j(t))-b(U_j(0))|^2
 \le 2\mathbb E(M_*^2/4+N_*^2)
 \le10cT=20\kappa_*D^2T.
 \tag{TR16}
\]
All three terms of the backward increment remain in this estimate. Combining it with (TR15) proves
\[
 \boxed{\quad
 \mathbb E\sup_{0\le t\le T}|2-\overline W_{de,j}(U_j(t))|^2
 \le8\epsilon_j+40\kappa_*D_j^2T
 \le8\epsilon_j+\frac{320\kappa_*T}{m_j^2n_j}
 \longrightarrow0.\quad}\tag{TR17}
\]
Thus the three original orientation-average histories converge to the constant 2 uniformly in time in probability, and in the displayed squared maximum mean, on every bounded interval. This strengthens ASL52's scalar action and finite-time correlations. No equality of individual-face expectations or collapse of growing-loop histories is asserted. The nonconstant quantities still requiring actual calculation are their spatially resolved and raw-phase correlations and spectral weights, as retained in ASL39–ASL45 and ASL53–ASL63.

\begin{figure}[htbp]\centering\includegraphics[width=\linewidth,height=.72\textheight,keepaspectratio]{WILSON_TIME_REGULARITY.png}\caption{Exact forward/backward drift cancellation, unchanged moment constants, and continuous-path receiving maps}\end{figure}

The figure shows the proved maps (TR2), (TR7), (TR9), (TR13)–(TR14) and (TR17). It depicts no simulated vacuum trajectories. Its reproducible source is draw\_wilson\_time.py; the complete proof and original human-source coverage accompany it.
\clearpage
\section*{Physical soft fields and the actual vacuum variance}

This is an independent continuation of CW1–CW49. The original observable,
reflection, Hamiltonian, root, open boundaries and simultaneous path are
retained. The calculation gives a divergence-free physical conjugate
operator, its full commutator and double commutator, and a quantitative
variance bound in the actual vacuum. It also computes the terms that stop
this particular bound from proving the proposed spectral endpoint.
No alternative state or observable is used as the receiving object.

\subsection*{CV1. Original data and conventions}

Let \(L\ge2\), \(m=2L\), \(n=m+1\), and let a vertex with indices
\((i,k,l)\in\{0,\ldots,m\}^3\) have original physical coordinate
\(o+a(i-L,k-L,l-L)\). Every positive link has its starting coordinate in
its direction less than \(m\). Every elementary face is included. Their
counts, the probability Haar measure, and the full Hamiltonian are
\[
 N=3mn^2,\quad M=3m^2n,\quad dH=\bigotimes_e dH_e,
 \quad H=\kappa K+bW,
 \quad K=-\sum_{e,\alpha}X_{e\alpha}^2,
 \quad W=\sum_pD_p,
 \quad D_p=2-\operatorname{tr}P_p,                         \tag{CV1}
\]
where \(T_\alpha=-i\sigma_\alpha/2\),
\(X_{e\alpha}f(U)=\partial_t f(\ldots,e^{tT_\alpha}U_e,\ldots)|_0\),
\(\kappa=2g^2/a\), \(b=1/(2g^2a)\), and \(\xi=b/\kappa=1/(4g^4)\).
The metric has radius two on every link. In particular
\([X_{e\alpha},X_{e\beta}]=-\epsilon_{\alpha\beta\gamma}X_{e\gamma}\):
applying two derivatives to the matrix \(U_e\) gives the products in
the order \(T_\beta T_\alpha U_e\). Different-link fields commute.
We write \(U=c(U)I+q(U)\cdot T\); hence
\(c^2+|q|^2/4=1\), \(q^\alpha=-2\operatorname{tr}(T_\alpha U)\),
and \(0\le D_p\le4\).

The unit vacuum is the positive smooth solution
\(H\psi=E_0\psi\), and \(d\mu=\psi^2dH\). Its gauge invariance and
reflection invariance follow from uniqueness: those transformations
preserve \(H\), positivity, and the unit norm. Put \(v=\log\psi\).

The tree and all its products are
\[
 t_{i,k,l}=
 \left(\prod_{r=0}^{l-1}U_3(0,0,r)\right)
 \left(\prod_{b=0}^{k-1}U_2(0,b,l)\right)
 \left(\prod_{a'=0}^{i-1}U_1(a',k,l)\right),
 \qquad Z_e=t_{s(e)}U_et_{t(e)}^{-1}.                    \tag{CV2}
\]
Products increase from left to right; empty products are \(I\).
Tree \(Z_e\)'s equal \(I\). The chord classes are
\(C_2=\{(i,k,l;2):1\le i\le m,0\le k<m,0\le l\le m\}\) and
\(C_3=\{(i,k,l;3):(i,k)\ne(0,0),0\le i,k\le m,0\le l<m\}\).

Put \(h_0=\pi/(2n)\), \(s_0=\sin h_0\), \(c_0=\cos h_0\),
\(C_0=\cot h_0\), and retain the original counting-space functions
\[
 v_0(r)=n^{-1/2},\quad
 v_1(r)=\sqrt{2/n}\cos(\pi(r+1/2)/n),\quad
 w_1(r)=-\sqrt{2/n}\sin(\pi(r+1)/n).                    \tag{CV3}
\]
The vertex index runs from 0 to \(m\); the edge index runs from 0 to
\(m-1\). Set \(w_1(-1)=w_1(m)=0\). The two full physical cochains,
with their zero components displayed, are
\[
\begin{array}{lll}
 V_{1,1}=w_1(i)v_1(k)/\sqrt{2n},&
 V_{1,2}=-v_1(i)w_1(k)/\sqrt{2n},&V_{1,3}=0,\\
 V_{3,1}=0,&V_{3,2}=w_1(k)v_1(l)/\sqrt{2n},&
 V_{3,3}=-v_1(k)w_1(l)/\sqrt{2n}.
\end{array}                                                    \tag{CV4}
\]
These are the source's \(V_{12},V_{23}\). They are orthonormal,
divergence free in the open-box counting inner product, and
\(\|d_1V_3\|^2=\|d_1V_1\|^2=\sigma_*^2=8s_0^2\).
For verification, \(v_1(r+1)-v_1(r)=2s_0w_1(r)\),
\(w_1(r-1)-w_1(r)=2s_0v_1(r)\), including both endpoints;
their squared counting norms are one and their sums against \(v_0\)
are zero. Substituting these four finite identities in the divergence,
inner product, and curl gives the stated equalities. In the curl of
\(V_3\), the 23 component has squared norm \(8s_0^2\), and the 12 and
13 components vanish. No periodic boundary convention is involved.

The original chord rows are \(a_c=V_{1,c}\), \(b_c=V_{3,c}\), and
\[
 u_1=\sum_ca_cq(Z_c),\quad u_3=\sum_cb_cq(Z_c),\quad
 S=u_1\cdot u_3,\quad S_R=S\circ R,\quad D=S+S_R,
 \quad F=\beta_0(S-S_R),\quad \beta_0={\sqrt8s_0\over2g^2}.
                                                               \tag{CV5}
\]
Reflection is the original signed link map
\((RU)_1(i,k,l)=U_1(m-i-1,k,l)^{-1}\),
\((RU)_d(i,k,l)=U_d(m-i,k,l)\), \(d=2,3\).
Every reflected word means precisely this substitution in CV2.
For example its root path is
\[
 t^R_{i,k,l}=
 \left(\prod_{r<l}U_3(m,0,r)\right)
 \left(\prod_{b<k}U_2(m,b,l)\right)
 \left(\prod_{a'<i}U_1(m-a'-1,k,l)^{-1}\right).            \tag{CV6}
\]
In particular all boundary products on the far side of the box remain.
Set \(G=\mu(F^2)\), \(C_F=\kappa\mu\sum(XF)^2\), and
\(\mathscr E(s)=G-C_F/s\).

\subsection*{CV2. Exact physical lifts, including both root terms}

For a real cochain \(V\), let \(p_x(V)\) be its signed additive tree
integral from the root to \(x\); all CV2 tree orientations are positive.
Define the frame and the soft fields
\[
 E_{e\alpha}=\sum_\gamma
  (\operatorname{Ad}_{t_{s(e)}^{-1}})_{\gamma\alpha}X_{e\gamma},
 \quad Y_{V,\alpha}=\sum_e V_eE_{e\alpha},
 \quad Y_{V,z}=\sum_\alpha z_\alpha Y_{V,\alpha}.          \tag{CV7}
\]
The last expression permits a variable color vector \(z(U)\); when
differentiating a function once it uses its current value.
Each \(E_{e\alpha}\) has Haar divergence zero, because its coefficients
do not depend on \(U_e\). Orthogonality of the adjoint matrices proves
\[
 \sum E_{e\alpha}^2=\sum X_{e\alpha}^2,\qquad
 \langle Y_{V_\nu,\alpha},Y_{V_\lambda,\beta}\rangle
       =\delta_{\nu\lambda}\delta_{\alpha\beta},
 \qquad\operatorname{div}Y_{V_\nu,\alpha}=0.              \tag{CV8}
\]
The absence of a derivative term in the first equality follows again
from the absence of \(U_e\) in its own coefficient matrix.

For a fixed color \(z\), differentiating each occurrence in the tree
product gives \(Y_{V,z}t_xt_x^{-1}=p_x(V)z\cdot T\). Consequently
\[
 Y_{V,z}Z_c=(p_s(V)+V_c)(z\cdot T)Z_c
                    -p_t(V)Z_c(z\cdot T).               \tag{CV9}
\]
This is an identity in the original physical variables, before any
gauge fixing. Define
\(d_{Vc}=p_s(V)+V_c-p_t(V)\), \(r_{Vc}=p_s(V)+V_c+p_t(V)\).
Quaternion multiplication gives the complete differential
\[
 Y_{V,z}q(Z_c)=c(Z_c)d_{Vc}z+\tfrac12r_{Vc}z\times q(Z_c),
 \quad Y_{V,z}c(Z_c)=-\tfrac14d_{Vc}z\cdot q(Z_c).        \tag{CV10}
\]
This proves rather than assumes the comparison with the original
additive map \(T\): its matrix is \(d_{Vc}=(TV)_c\). The second term
in CV10 is present even when that additive image vanishes.

Write \(W_r=\sum_{a'<r}w_1(a')\) and \(v_*=v_1(0)\). The full root
integrals of CV4 are
\[
 p_{i,k,l}(V_1)={v_1(k)W_i-v_*W_k\over\sqrt{2n}},\qquad
 p_{i,k,l}(V_3)={v_1(l)W_k-v_*W_l\over\sqrt{2n}}.         \tag{CV11}
\]
Using \(v_1(r)=v_*+2s_0W_r\), including \(r=0,m\), yields
\[
\begin{array}{ll}
 d_{1,(i,k,l;2)}=2(v_*-v_1(i))w_1(k)/\sqrt{2n},&d_{1,C_3}=0,\\
 d_{3,C_2}=0,&d_{3,(i,k,l;3)}=2(v_*-v_1(k))w_1(l)/\sqrt{2n}.
\end{array}                                                    \tag{CV12}
\]
In particular \(\sum a_cd_{1c}=\sum b_cd_{3c}=1\) and
\(\sum a_cd_{3c}=\sum b_cd_{1c}=0\). These are exact finite sums,
not a chart limit. For example the first is
\(\sum_{i=1}^m[v_1(i)^2-v_*v_1(i)]=1\).

For \(\nu,\lambda\in\{1,3\}\), with \(M_{1c}=a_c,M_{3c}=b_c\), put
\[
 A_{\nu\lambda}=\sum_cM_{\nu c}d_{\lambda c}c(Z_c),\quad
 B_{\nu\lambda}=\sum_cM_{\nu c}r_{\lambda c}q(Z_c).
 \quad Y_{V_\lambda,z}u_\nu
       =A_{\nu\lambda}z+\tfrac12z\times B_{\nu\lambda}.   \tag{CV13}
\]
All these are explicit finite functions of the original words.

\subsection*{CV3. A divergence-free physical conjugate field}

Choose the original soft direction \(V_3=V_{23}\) and contract it with
the original \(u_1\):
\[
 B=Y_{V_3,u_1},\qquad P=-iB,\qquad h=|u_1|^2.            \tag{CV14}
\]
This is a differential operator on the original physical Hilbert space.
Indeed under a gauge transformation, \(t_x\mapsto h_ot_xh_x^{-1}\),
the vectors \(q(Z_c)\) rotate by \(\operatorname{Ad}_{h_o}\), and
the contraction in CV7 cancels this root rotation. Hence \(B\) maps
gauge-invariant smooth functions to gauge-invariant smooth functions.
Since \(a\) has only \(C_2\) entries, CV12 gives \(A_{13}=0\)
identically, not just in expectation. Taking the color trace in CV13
therefore proves
\[
 \operatorname{div}B=\sum_\alpha Y_{V_3,\alpha}u_1^\alpha=0,
 \quad Bu_1=\tfrac12u_1\times B_{13},\quad Bh=0,
 \quad |B|^2=h.                                        \tag{CV15}
\]
The norm statement uses the orthonormality in CV8. The field is smooth
on a compact manifold, so its flow exists for all real times. Its
Jacobian preserves Haar by the divergence equation. Pullback by the
flow is thus a unitary group; differentiating gives the symmetric
operator \(P=-iB\) on smooth functions and its generator extension.
All operator identities below hold on smooth functions, which include
every function and vector used in their vacuum expectations.

Define the exact scalar correction
\[
 \Xi=2(A_{33}-1)h+u_3\cdot(u_1\times B_{13})-BD.          \tag{CV16}
\]
The full reflected \(D=S+S_R\), with CV6, occurs in the last term.
Using CV13 in \(BS=(Bu_1)\cdot u_3+u_1\cdot Bu_3\) proves
\[
 BF=\beta_0(2h+\Xi),\qquad [P,F]=-i\beta_0(2h+\Xi).
                                                               \tag{CV17}
\]
The contribution \(BD\) is neither set to zero nor assigned a
reflection sign. Although \(D\) is even, \(B\) itself need not be
reflection odd away from the identity configuration.

For complete evaluation of \(BD\), apply CV9 to every unreflected
word and the following original-link insertion rule to CV6. If
\(A_e=V_{3e}\operatorname{Ad}_{t_s^{-1}}u_1\cdot T\), then
\[
 BU_e=A_eU_e,\qquad B(U_e^{-1})=-U_e^{-1}A_e,\qquad
 B(A_1\cdots A_q)=\sum_{j=1}^q A_1\cdots(BA_j)\cdots A_q.
                                                               \tag{CV18}
\]
Every letter in CV6 and every chord letter is included, with its
displayed sign and position. Thus CV16 is a finite polynomial
receiving term with no differential equation left to define it.

The original receiving energy is equally explicit. Put
\(\ell_c=a_cu_3+b_cu_1\), and use the same \(T_{ce},R_{ce}\) as in
CV30. The gradient of \(S\) in the orthonormal root frame is
\[
 g_e=\sum_c\left[T_{ce}c(Z_c)\ell_c+
                       {R_{ce}\over2}q(Z_c)\times\ell_c\right].
                                                               \tag{CV18a}
\]
This follows by taking the dot product of CV10 with \(\ell_c\) for
the cochain supported at \(e\). For reflection, let \(e'\) be the
reflected positive edge and set
\[
 \Omega_e(U)=
 \begin{cases}
 -\operatorname{Ad}_{\,t_{s(e')}(RU)U_e^{-1}t_{s(e)}(U)^{-1}},
                                                   &d(e)=1,\\
 \operatorname{Ad}_{\,t_{s(e')}(RU)t_{s(e)}(U)^{-1}},
                                                   &d(e)=2,3 .
 \end{cases}
\quad
 C_F=\kappa\beta_0^2\mu\sum_e
       |g_e(U)-\Omega_e(U)^Tg_{e'}(RU)|^2.              \tag{CV18b}
\]
For a reversed edge the physical left infinitesimal coefficient
changes from \(A\) to \(-U_e^{-1}AU_e\); for the other two directions
it is unchanged. Conversion to the target root frame gives precisely
the two displayed matrices. Each \(\Omega_e\) is orthogonal,
including its minus sign. Thus CV18b retains the original kinetic
metric and every reflected boundary path, rather than replacing
\(C_F\) by a soft-direction energy.

Let \(x=\mu(h)\), \(V_B=\mu((Bv)^2)=\|P\psi\|^2\), and
\(J=\mu(BF)=\beta_0(2x+\mu\Xi)\). Integration of
\(B(F\psi^2)\) gives \(J=-2\mu(FBv)\), while
\(\mu(Bv)=\frac12\int B(\psi^2)dH=0\). Cauchy--Schwarz yields the
actual-vacuum covariance inequality
\[
             4G V_B\ \ge\ \beta_0^2(2x+\mu\Xi)^2.       \tag{CV19}
\]
There is no division by a possibly zero variance. If
\(B^-=(B-R_*B)/2\), its divergence is also zero, it is physical,
\(\mu(B^-F)=J\), and \(\mu((B^-v)^2)\le V_B\): reflection is an
isometry of \(L^2(\mu)\), so its odd projection is a contraction.
Thus odd projection can strengthen CV19 without changing its numerator.

\subsection*{CV4. Using the full vacuum equation rather than a trial density}

The source's Section 12 gives
\[
 \mathcal E_0=\min\{2bM,3\sqrt{NM}/a\},\qquad
 E_0\le\mathcal E_0,\qquad
 d:=\mu W\le B_W:=\min\{2M,6g^2\sqrt{NM}\}.             \tag{CV20}
\]
For completeness, its comparison integral uses the probability Haar
marginal \(2\sqrt{1-z^2}\,dz/\pi\) of \(z=\operatorname{tr}U/2\),
the unit factor \(f_t=Z_t^{-1/2}e^{tz}\),
\(Z_t=\int e^{2tz}dH\), and \(q_t=\int zf_t^2dH\).
Writing \(y=1-z\), its density is proportional to
\(e^{-2ty}y^{1/2}\sqrt{2-y}\mathbf1_{[0,2]}\).
Multiplication of the gamma density \(e^{-2ty}y^{1/2}\) by the
decreasing factor \(\sqrt{2-y}\mathbf1_{[0,2]}\) decreases its mean:
the covariance is half the integral of
\((y-y')(w(y)-w(y'))\le0\). Integration by parts gives the gamma
mean \(3/(4t)\), so \(1-q_t\le3/(4t)\).
Pairing \(z\) with \(-z\) in its defining integral gives \(0<q_t<1\).
The radius-two identities \(|\nabla z|^2=(1-z^2)/4\) and
\(\Delta z=-3z/4\) give
\(\int|\nabla f_t|^2=3tq_t/8\le3t/8\).
The product comparison has face trace \(2q_t^4\), hence energy at most
\(3\kappa Nt/8+6bM/t\). At
\(t=4\sqrt{bM/(\kappa N)}\), this is \(3\sqrt{NM}/a\).
The constant comparison gives \(2bM\). Since the actual positive
ground state is physical, this proves CV20 without replacing its
density by either comparison. Positivity of \(K\) gives the bound on
\(d\). Cube symmetries additionally give
\(\mu W_{12}=\mu W_{13}=\mu W_{23}=d/3\), since these three sums
are permuted and add to \(W\); individual face means are not identified.

The first signed CW identity, proved here by finite summation, is
\[
 u_1=-{1\over2s_0\sqrt{2n}}
  \sum_{a',k,l}w_1(a')w_1(k)
        [qZ_2(a'+1,k,l)-qZ_2(a',k,l)].                  \tag{CV21}
\]
Indeed the coefficient of \(qZ_2(i,k,l)\) is
\(-[w_1(i-1)-w_1(i)]w_1(k)/(2s_0\sqrt{2n})=a_c\);
the two endpoint values of \(w_1\) include the boundary terms and
\(Z_2(0,k,l)=I\). The exact face relation
\(Z_2(a'+1,k,l)=\widehat P_{12}(a',k,l)Z_2(a',k,l)\),
\(\widehat P_{12}=tPt^{-1}\), follows by cancellation in CV2.
For \(U,V\in SU(2)\), \(|q(U)-q(V)|\le2\|U-V\|_{op}\)
by quaternion coordinates; \(\|P-I\|_{op}^2=2-\operatorname{tr}P\).
The sum of the squared weights in CV21 is \(1/(8s_0^2)\).
Cauchy--Schwarz therefore proves the pointwise and vacuum bounds
\[
 h\le{W_{12}\over2s_0^2},\qquad
 0\le x\le x_{\max}:=\min\{4S_1^2,B_W/(6s_0^2)\},
 \quad S_1=\sqrt{2/n}C_0(C_0-c_0).                       \tag{CV22}
\]
The value of \(S_1=\sum|a_c|\) follows from the finite sine and cosine
sums; \(\sum|w_1|=\sqrt{2/n}C_0\) and
\(\sum_{i=1}^m|v_1(i)|=\sqrt{2/n}(C_0-c_0)\).

Let \(\nabla v\) mean the full original physical gradient and define
the nonnegative complementary kinetic quantity
\[
 T_{3|1}=\mu\{h|\nabla v|^2-(Bv)^2\}\ \ge0.              \tag{CV23}
\]
The sign follows pointwise from \(|B|^2=h\). Multiplying the original
vacuum equation \(-\Delta\psi=(E_0/\kappa-\xi W)\psi\)
by \(h\psi\), and integrating by parts twice, gives the exact identity
\[
 V_B={E_0\over\kappa}x-\xi\mu(Wh)
                 +\tfrac12\mu(\Delta h)-T_{3|1}.        \tag{CV24}
\]
Both the scalar part \(2bM\) of the potential and the full \(E_0\)
are present: \(W=2M-\sum\operatorname{tr}P_p\).
For clarity, the intermediate identity is
\(\int h|\nabla\psi|^2=(E_0/\kappa)\mu h-\xi\mu(Wh)
+\frac12\mu\Delta h\). It fixes the sign of the Laplacian term.

Every term in \(\Delta h\) is also explicit. For the original word
occurrence count \(n_{ce}\), including both simple root paths, put
\[
 D_{1e}=\sum_c|a_c|n_{ce},\quad
 J_{1e}=\tfrac12\sum_c|a_c|n_{ce}^2,\quad
 A_1=m\sqrt{2/n}C_0(5C_0-4c_0).                         \tag{CV25}
\]
Differentiate each letter with CV18 but with \(A_e=T_\alpha\) for
the chosen physical derivative. Its first derivative is one half
times a unit quaternion, and a second differentiated occurrence or
pair is one quarter times one. Thus
\(|X_{e\alpha}u_1|\le D_{1e}\),
\(|X_{e\alpha}^2u_1|\le J_{1e}\), and exactly
\[
 \Delta h=2\sum_{e,\alpha}
       (|X_{e\alpha}u_1|^2+u_1\cdot X_{e\alpha}^2u_1).
                                                               \tag{CV26}
\]
Here \(n_{ce}\le2\), \(\sum_eJ_{1e}=A_1/2\), and
\(\sum_eD_{1e}^2\le S_1 A_1\), by Cauchy--Schwarz with measure
\(|a_c|\): its right side is exactly
\(S_1\sum_c|a_c|\sum_en_{ce}^2\).
For a \(C_2\) chord the full length is \(2(i+k+l+1)\), while
the squared-occurrence sum is \(2i+4k+4l+2\); the shared root
prefix has \(k+l\) edges, each counted twice. With respect to
the first-row absolute weights, the averages of \(i,k,l\) are
\(mC_0/[2(C_0-c_0)],(m-1)/2,m/2\), respectively. Inserting them
in that squared-occurrence sum proves the expression for \(A_1\)
in CV25. No word or common prefix has been removed.
Consequently
\[
 \tfrac12\mu\Delta h\le6S_1A_1,
 \qquad
 V_B\le U(x):=\min\left\{4S_1^2{\mathcal E_0\over\kappa},
 {\mathcal E_0\over\kappa}x-2\xi s_0^2x^2+6S_1A_1\right\}.
                                                               \tag{CV27}
\]
The first alternative uses \(h\le4S_1^2\) and
\(\int|\nabla\psi|^2=(E_0-bd)/\kappa\le\mathcal E_0/\kappa\).
The second uses CV22, \(W\ge W_{12}\ge2s_0^2h\),
\(\mu h^2\ge x^2\), and drops only the nonnegative CV23 term.
Thus CV19 with \(V_B\le U(x)\) is a concrete actual-vacuum lower
bound for \(G\), including a computed negative curvature contribution
to the denominator. CV24 gives the exact remaining directional
kinetic quantity rather than replacing it by a directional assumption.

\subsection*{CV5. The full double commutator}

The following formulas specify every coefficient by finite operations
on the original tree and words. Write \(e\prec f\) if tree edge \(e\)
is in the root path to \(s(f)\); chords never satisfy this relation.
Differentiating the adjoint matrices in CV7, with the sign fixed in
CV1, gives
\[
 [E_{e\alpha},E_{f\beta}]
  =-\epsilon_{\alpha\beta\gamma}
  [\delta_{ef}E_{e\gamma}
      +\mathbf1_{e\prec f}E_{f\gamma}
      +\mathbf1_{f\prec e}E_{e\gamma}].                  \tag{CV28}
\]
For example an ancestor variation gives
\(E_{e\alpha}\operatorname{Ad}_{t_s^{-1}}e_\beta
=-\operatorname{Ad}_{t_s^{-1}}(e_\alpha\times e_\beta)\).
This verifies both ancestor terms and proves CV28 in all three cases.
Let
\[
 L_3(f,e)=[V_{3f}+p_{s(f)}(V_3)]\delta_{fe}
                              +\mathbf1_{f\prec e}V_{3e}.
                                                               \tag{CV29}
\]
The exact commutator \([B,E_{f\beta}]=\sum_{e,\gamma}
C_{f\beta,e\gamma}E_{e\gamma}\) has coefficients
\[
 C_{f\beta,e\gamma}
  =-V_{3e}E_{f\beta}u_1^\gamma
       -\sum_\alpha\epsilon_{\alpha\beta\gamma}
                    u_1^\alpha L_3(f,e),\qquad
 E_{f\beta}u_1
  =\sum_ca_c[c(Z_c)T_{cf}e_\beta
                         +\tfrac12R_{cf}e_\beta\times q(Z_c)].
                                                               \tag{CV30}
\]
Here \(T_{cf}=\mathbf1_{f\in t_s}+\delta_{cf}-\mathbf1_{f\in t_t}\)
and \(R_{cf}=\mathbf1_{f\in t_s}+\delta_{cf}+\mathbf1_{f\in t_t}\).
Their full root occurrences, including coincidences, are retained.
With \(i,a,b'\) denoting link/color pairs, put
\[
 A_i=[B,E_i]=\sum_aC_i^aE_a,\qquad
 Q_i=[B,A_i]=\sum_a Q_i^aE_a,\qquad
 Q_i^a=BC_i^a+\sum_{b'}C_i^{b'}C_{b'}^a.                \tag{CV31}
\]
To calculate \(BC_i^a\), use CV10, CV13, and CV30; \(T,R,L_3,V_3\)
are constant finite arrays. Thus even this derivative has an explicit
polynomial rule and involves no derivative of the unknown vacuum.
All \(E_i,A_i,Q_i\) are anti-symmetric in Haar, since they are
commutators of divergence-free fields.

Expanding the two commutators of \(-\kappa\sum E_i^2+bW\) gives
\[
 [P,[H,P]]
  =-\kappa\sum_i(Q_iE_i+E_iQ_i+2A_i^2)+bB^2W.           \tag{CV32}
\]
There is no potential-free substitution for \(H\). The scalar commutes,
but remains in \(H\psi=E_0\psi\). Integrating CV32 yields
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 \langle\psi,[P,[H,P]]\psi\rangle
 &=2\kappa\mu\sum_i
   \left[(\sum_aC_i^aE_av)^2
          +(E_iv)\sum_aQ_i^aE_av\right]+b\mu(B^2W)\\
 &=2\langle P\psi,(H-E_0)P\psi\rangle\ge0 .
 \end{aligned}                                                   \tag{CV33}
\]
For the second equality expand
\([P,[H,P]]=2PHP-P^2H-HP^2\) and use the full eigenfunction equation.
The mixed kinetic term in the first line has no asserted sign. In
particular CV20 controls its trace norm only through the total
electric energy; it does not set this tensor to zero.

\subsection*{CV6. The receiving potential term and its curvature remainder}

For a face \(p\) starting at \(x\), write its four rooted letters as
\(Z_d(x),Z_e(x+d),Z_d(x+e)^{-1},Z_e(x)^{-1}\), and let \(A_j\)
be the prefix before letter \(j\). In the following finite matrix sum,
use \(A_j\) for a forward letter, and \(A_jZ_f^{-1}\) for a reversed
letter of its positive edge \(f\):
\[
 K_p=\sum_{j=1}^4 \varepsilon_j V_{3,f_j}
                   \operatorname{Ad}_{A_j^{\rm ins}},\qquad
 k_p=\sum_{j=1}^4\varepsilon_jV_{3,f_j}=(d_1V_3)_p.
                                                               \tag{CV34}
\]
The signs are \(+,+,-,-\). The rooted plaquette is
\(\widehat P_p=t_xP_pt_x^{-1}\); write its scalar and vector as
\(c_p,q_p\). Product differentiation, with every prefix retained,
proves
\[
 BW={1\over2}\sum_pq_p\cdot K_pu_1,
 \quad B^2W={1\over2}\sum_p
 \left[c_p|K_pu_1|^2+q_p\cdot
 \{(BK_p)u_1+\tfrac12K_p(u_1\times B_{13})
                 -p_x(V_3)u_1\times(K_pu_1)\}\right].   \tag{CV35}
\]
To check the last root term, the physical left logarithmic derivative
of \(P_p\), transported by \(t_x\), is \(K_pu_1\). Differentiating
this transported vector introduces
\(-p_x(V_3)u_1\times(K_pu_1)\). A physical left derivative satisfies
\(Bq(P)=c(P)J+J\times q(P)/2\); its cross term has zero dot product
with \(J\). This gives CV35 with the displayed factor \(1/2\).
In \(BK_p\), differentiate each prefix using CV9 and
\(B\operatorname{Ad}_A z=(BAA^{-1})\times\operatorname{Ad}_A z\)
for fixed \(z\). This is another finite insertion formula.

Since \(\sum_pk_p^2=\sigma_*^2\), CV35 can equivalently be evaluated as
\[
 B^2W={\sigma_*^2\over2}h+\mathcal R_V,                 \tag{CV36}
\]
where the complete, signed remainder is
\[
 \mathcal R_V={1\over2}\sum_p\left[
 (c_p-1)|K_pu_1|^2+|K_pu_1|^2-k_p^2h
 +q_p\cdot\{(BK_p)u_1+\tfrac12K_p(u_1\times B_{13})
                      -p_x(V_3)u_1\times(K_pu_1)\}\right].
                                                               \tag{CV37}
\]
Thus the soft curl term is present with its original frequency, and
CV37 is exactly the receiving curvature and transport correction.

Here is a numerical-free finite bound for it. Put
\[
 p_*=2\sqrt m/n,\quad r_*=2p_*+\sqrt2/n^{3/2},\quad
 R_{13}=\sum_{c\in C_2}|a_cr_{3c}|\le2p_*S_1,\quad
 \ell_p=\sum_{e\in\partial p}|V_{3e}|.                  \tag{CV38}
\]
CV11 and \(|W_r|\le\sqrt m\) give \(|p_x|\le p_*\);
CV4 gives the displayed \(r_*\). Since \(d_{3,C_2}=0\),
\(r_{3c}=2p_t(V_3)\), proving the bound on \(R_{13}\).
Also \(\sum_p\ell_p^2\le4\sum_er_eV_{3e}^2\le16\), including
the actual boundary incidence \(r_e\le4\). Hence
\(\|K_p\|\le\ell_p\), \(|B_{13}|\le2R_{13}\).
Every insertion prefix has at most four rooted letters; CV9 bounds
its left logarithmic derivative by \(4r_*|u_1|\). Thus
\(\|BK_p\|\le4r_*\ell_p|u_1|\). Finally
\(|q_p|\le2\sqrt{D_p}\). Applying Cauchy--Schwarz to the actual
face means, without making them equal, gives
\[
 |\mu\mathcal R_V|
 \le(8+\sigma_*^2/2)x
   +4\sqrt{B_W}\left[(4r_*+p_*)4S_1^2+2S_1R_{13}\right].
                                                               \tag{CV39}
\]
This explicitly evaluates a bound on the receiving potential term
in CV33. It is not an assertion that the remainder is small.

The kinetic receiving tensor can also be bounded with fully evaluated
constants. Let
\[
 L_*^2=2+2Np_*^2+3m,\quad
 C_*=\sqrt{3S_1A_1}+2\sqrt2S_1L_*,\quad
 C_*'=3r_*S_1\sqrt{3S_1A_1}+2\sqrt2S_1R_{13}L_*,\quad
 Q_*=C_*'+C_*^2.                                      \tag{CV39a}
\]
These stars denote the constants in this equation only. From CV29,
\(\sum_{f,e}L_3(f,e)^2
=\sum_f(V_{3f}+p_{s(f)})^2+\sum_e|t_{s(e)}|V_{3e}^2
\le L_*^2\), because every tree path has at most \(3m\) links.
CV30 and weighted Cauchy--Schwarz give
\(\|C(U)\|_{\rm Frob}\le C_*\). To differentiate its first term,
CV10 implies \(|Bc(Z_c)|\le r_*|u_1|/2\) and
\(|Bq(Z_c)|\le2r_*|u_1|\). Consequently
\(|B(E_{f\beta}u_1)|\le(3/2)r_*|u_1|D_{1f}\).
Its other term differentiates \(u_1\), with
\(|Bu_1|\le2S_1R_{13}\). Thus
\(\|BC\|_{\rm Frob}\le C_*'\) and
\(\|Q\|_{\rm Frob}\le Q_*\), by CV31 and
\(\|C^2\|_{\rm Frob}\le\|C\|_{\rm Frob}^2\).
The original electric trace is exactly
\(\mu|\nabla v|^2=(E_0-bd)/\kappa\). Hence, writing \(R_*(x)\)
for the entire right side of CV39, CV33 gives the completely finite
bound
\[
 0\le\langle\psi,[P,[H,P]]\psi\rangle
 \le2(C_*^2+Q_*)\mathcal E_0
                       +b[\sigma_*^2x/2+R_*(x)].       \tag{CV39b}
\]
This step bounds the full kinetic tensor, including its signed mixed
term, instead of silently treating the physical soft lift as a Killing
field. It is a coarse absolute bound; the exact tensor is CV30–CV33.

\subsection*{CV7. Original-path evaluation and the next calculation}

The unchanged path is
\[
 L=j^4,\quad m=2j^4,\quad n=2j^4+1,\quad a={1\over100j},
 \quad g^2={\kappa_*\over200j},\quad\kappa=\kappa_*,
 \quad\xi={10000j^2\over\kappa_*^2},\quad s={\kappa_*\over j}.
                                                               \tag{CV40}
\]
All following limits retain each finite formula CV20–CV39. Elementary
\(\sin t/t\to1\), \(\cos t\to1\), and \(n/(2j^4)\to1\) give
\[
 {S_1^2\over j^{12}}\longrightarrow {256\over\pi^4},\quad
 {S_1A_1\over j^{16}}\longrightarrow {2560\over\pi^4},\quad
 {\mathcal E_0/\kappa_*\over j^{13}}\longrightarrow{7200\over\kappa_*},
 \quad {B_W\over j^{11}}\longrightarrow {18\kappa_*\over25}.
                                                               \tag{CV41}
\]
The curvature alternative in CV22 is asymptotically larger than
\(4S_1^2\):
\[
 {B_W/(6s_0^2)\over4S_1^2j^7}
          \longrightarrow {3\pi^2\kappa_*\over1600}.
                                                               \tag{CV42}
\]
Thus this actual mean-curvature input does not improve the bound on
\(x\) on CV40. Evaluating the first denominator bound in CV27 gives
\[
 {4S_1^2\mathcal E_0/\kappa_*\over j^{25}}
       \longrightarrow{7372800\over\kappa_*\pi^4}.       \tag{CV43}
\]
At \(x=4S_1^2\), the retained negative curvature term relative to the
extensive term satisfies
\[
 j^7\,{2\xi s_0^2x^2\over (\mathcal E_0/\kappa_*)x}
       \longrightarrow {1600\over9\kappa_*\pi^2},
 \qquad {6S_1A_1\over(\mathcal E_0/\kappa_*)x}
                         =O(j^{-9}).                   \tag{CV44}
\]
The maximum of the second quadratic bound over the actual interval
\(0\le x\le x_{\max}\) is at its right endpoint for all sufficiently
large \(j\), because its vertex
\((\mathcal E_0/\kappa_*)/(4\xi s_0^2)\) divided by \(4S_1^2\)
tends to infinity as \(j^7\). Thus even this next use of the signed
curl and full vacuum equation does not remove the \(j^{25}\) scale.
The missing cancellation has a precise value in CV24: it is
\(T_{3|1}\), together with the signed weighted-potential and Laplacian
terms there. An extensive upper bound on total electric energy loses
this particular directional cancellation.

The numerator was also evaluated, rather than identified with its
identity-configuration expression. Write
\(\Lambda=\sum_c|b_cd_{3c}|=2c_0C_0/n\). To verify this, sum first
over \(i,l\) in CV12; the remaining sum is
\(\sum_k|v_1(k)|(v_*-v_1(k))=v_*\sum_k|v_1(k)|\),
since \(\sum|v_1(k)|v_1(k)=0\) by reflection.
Consequently \(|A_{33}-1|\le2\Lambda\). Define the full occurrence
sums \(D_{\nu e}=\sum|M_{\nu c}|n_{ce}\),
\(K_e=2S_3D_{1e}+2S_1D_{3e}\), and
\(\mathcal C=3\sum_eK_e^2\), where
\[
 S_3=\sqrt{2/n}[(2-1/n)C_0^2-(c_0/n)C_0],\quad
 A_3={m\over n}\sqrt{2/n}[(9m+5)C_0^2-2c_0C_0].        \tag{CV45}
\]
For a \(C_3\) word, \(\sum_en_{ce}^2=2i+2k+4l+2\):
only its \(l\)-edge prefix is shared. The absolute third-row mass
on \(C_2\) is \(\sqrt{2/n}(m/n)C_0^2\), with coordinate means
\((m+1)/2,(m-1)/2,m/2\); its squared-occurrence mean is \(5m+1\).
The unexcluded \(C_3\) rectangle has mass \(\sqrt{2/n}C_0^2\),
coordinate means \(m/2,m/2,(m-1)/2\), and squared-occurrence mean
\(4m\). Its excluded corner has mass
\(\sqrt{2/n}c_0C_0/n\) and squared-occurrence mean \(2m\).
Adding the first two contributions and subtracting exactly that
corner proves \(A_3\) in CV45, including all boundary counts.
The word derivative argument of CV25 proves
\(|\nabla S|^2\le\mathcal C\), hence
\(|BD|\le|u_1||\nabla D|\le4S_1\sqrt{\mathcal C}\).
With the full-occurrence estimate
\(\mathcal C\le12[S_3\sqrt{S_1A_1}+S_1\sqrt{S_3A_3}]^2\),
the numerator correction has the fully evaluated bound
\[
 |\mu\Xi|\le4\Lambda x+4S_3R_{13}\sqrt x
                               +2\sqrt{\mathcal C x}
 \le16\Lambda S_1^2+8S_1S_3R_{13}
                               +4S_1\sqrt{\mathcal C}.  \tag{CV46}
\]
For its first inequality use \(|A_{33}-1|\le2\Lambda\),
\(|B_{13}|\le2R_{13}\), \(|u_3|\le2S_3\), and
\(\mu|u_1|\le\sqrt x\). The last contribution follows from
\(|BD|\le|u_1||\nabla D|\) and Cauchy--Schwarz, with
\(\mu|\nabla D|^2\le4\mathcal C\). The second inequality uses
\(x\le4S_1^2\).
Its last term is of order \(n^5\), whereas \(2x\le8S_1^2\) has
order \(n^3\). Thus CV46 gives no positive lower bound on \(2x+\mu\Xi\)
on CV40. It would be incorrect to drop this signed correction from
CV19. Likewise the last term of CV39 has order \(j^{31/2}\), while
the soft term \(\sigma_*^2x/2\) is at most of order \(j^4\).
These statements concern the proved bounds, not asserted asymptotics
of the unknown actual vacuum correlations.
The kinetic part of the explicit double-commutator upper bound CV39b
has order \(j^{33}\), because \(L_*^2\) has order \(n^2\),
\(C_*^2\) has order \(n^5\), \(C_*'\) has at most order \(n^{7/2}\),
and \(\mathcal E_0\) has order \(j^{13}\). The potential part has
at most order \(j^{35/2}\). This additional calculation shows exactly
why dropping the root-dependent kinetic commutators would give an
unsupported soft-frequency conclusion.

For an exact endpoint relation, whenever \(V_B>0\), orthogonal
projection onto \(P\psi\) gives
\[
 G-{C_F\over s}
 ={\beta_0^2(2x+\mu\Xi)^2-4V_BC_F/s\over4V_B}
   +\left\|F\psi+{i\beta_0(2x+\mu\Xi)\over2V_B}P\psi\right\|^2.
                                                               \tag{CV47}
\]
Indeed \(\langle P\psi,F\psi\rangle=-iJ/2\) with the inner product
conjugate linear in its first argument, by the same integration used
in CV19. If \(V_B=0\), CV19 gives \(J=0\) and CV47 is replaced by
that undivided identity. Equations CV16, CV24, CV30–CV37 compute the
exact original receiving terms for this conjugate method. The full
vacuum equation and CW mean defect, with the explicit estimates
CV27, CV39 and CV46, do not prove that CV47 is nonnegative on CV40.
This is the outcome of the attempted calculation, not a claimed
spectral endpoint or a theorem with a missing assumption.

\subsection*{CV8. Sources and scope}

The source data are PK103–PK108, PK143/PK143a, PK158–PK177f,
and original spatial-continuum Section 12, equations (93)–(100),
at the retained original TeX source. PRE17–PRE31 and PRE44–PRE55
were checked before this derivation: they already give the Poisson
response and the wrong-direction susceptibility inequality. The new
calculations here are the physical soft lift, divergence cancellation,
full commutator, directional energy identity and their actual receiving
defects. No novelty relative to the external literature is asserted.
The indexed literature was searched first; routing records were not
treated as read papers and no outside theorem was imported.

\begin{figure}[htbp]\centering\includegraphics[width=\linewidth,height=.72\textheight,keepaspectratio]{CONJUGATE_VARIANCE_FIGURE.png}\caption{Exact rooted soft field and evaluated variance-bound terms}\end{figure}

The left panel is a schematic of the exact ordered identity CV9, with
both root contributions. The right panel plots the full finite bound
terms in CV27 at \(x=4S_1^2\), \(\kappa_*=1\); these are neither
vacuum samples nor estimates of its actual directional variance.
CV41–CV44 prove the displayed scaling comparison. Reproduction uses
the included draw\_conjugate\_variance.py script; the PNG was inspected
for readable labels, signs and source locators. A three-dimensional
scene is not needed to show this differential identity or these bounds.

The original public definitions can be checked in
\href{https://github.com/KokunoYumeto/yang-mills-interacting-workbench/blob/485b61423c1f4e1805deb453c1b6b342b27842b6/yang-mills/continuations/20260930-s6-ns-moment-map-bridge/PERIOD_COUPLING_AND_PHYSICAL_KERNELS.md}{PK103–PK108 and PK143–PK177f}
and the full mean-defect proof in
\href{https://github.com/KokunoYumeto/yang-mills-interacting-workbench/blob/485b61423c1f4e1805deb453c1b6b342b27842b6/yang-mills/continuations/20260930-s6-ns-moment-map-bridge/sources/spatial/spatial_continuum.tex}{spatial-continuum TeX, Section 12, equations (93)–(100)}.
Exact files, hashes and actual reading coverage are in
`ACTUAL\_LIMIT\_SOURCE\_USE\_LEDGER.json`. The independent heat and CW proof files were
not edited by this continuation.
